One hundred thousand yojanas from there shines the sphere of the Sages (Saptarishi). The Pole Star is situated one hundred thousand yojanas above the sphere of the Sages.

The Pole Star acts as the pivot for the entire zodiac, holding the winds and rays, and setting the host of luminaries in motion. The sphere of the Sun is ten thousand yojanas, and the sphere of the Moon is said to be twelve thousand yojanas. Rahu is situated below the Sun, measuring ten thousand yojanas, and its sphere is said to be thirteen thousand yojanas. Being composed of darkness, it moves along the path during the eclipse and quickly covers the Sun and the Moon with its shadow. O Daksha, those who know the scriptures call this an eclipse; the spheres of the Sun and Moon become entirely covered by darkness.

The sphere of Venus is the sixteenth part of the Moon. O Brahmana, the sphere of Jupiter is one-fourth less than that of Venus. The spheres of Mars and Saturn are one-fourth less than that of Jupiter, and the sphere of Mercury is one-fourth less in width than those. The star and constellation forms are smaller than each other by four hundred, three hundred, and two hundred yojanas. The spheres of the stars are the smallest of all, measuring only one and a half yojanas; there is nothing smaller than them.

Above them are three planets that move far away: Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars are known as slow-moving. Below them are four other great planets: the Sun, the Moon, Mercury, and Venus, which are fast-moving. The Sun always moves, abiding by two solstices; he is the lord of all planets and moves below them all. The Moon moves above, having made a wide sphere. The entire constellation of stars moves above the Moon. Venus is above the stars, and Mercury is said to be above that. Mars is above Mercury, and Jupiter is above that. Saturn is above that, then the sphere of the Seven Sages, and the Pole Star is situated above the Seven Sages.

The chariot of the Sun is said to be nine thousand yojanas, and its pole is twice that size, O Prajapati. The axle measures one hundred thousand yojanas and is supported by the Pole Star. The second axle and its wheel are situated on the Manasottara Mountain. Its wheel is said to be composed of the zodiac signs and has twelve spokes. Its seven horses are composed of the seven meters, with Gayatri as the chief. When the Sun reaches the southern solstice, it moves quickly, and when the Sun moves to the north, it moves slowly.

On top of the Manasa mountain, in the eastern direction, is the great city of Mahendra; in the south is that of Dharmaraja (Yama), in the west is that of Varuna, and in the north is that of Kubera. Hear their names from me: Amaravati, Samyamani, Sukha, and Vibha, respectively. Taking the zodiac, the Lord of Lords, Prajapati (the Sun), shines at the city of Mahendra at sunrise, at the city of Yama at midday, at the city of Varuna in the evening, and at the city of Kubera at midnight.

Thus, the Sun moves, remaining constant in time through sunrise and sunset. He moves in all directions and sub-directions. Like a potter's wheel, the Lord of the Day rotates in the world, illuminating different times according to the perception of each being. He creates day and night, sustaining the subjects with his rays. These three worlds are filled with the rays of the Sun. Water, land, and the sky are called the best places for the worlds, and there is no doubt that this threefold existence has its root in the Sun.

The entire universe, including gods, demons, and humans, comes from him. He is the support in the middle and is established at the end. The brilliance of Brahma, Vishnu, Maheshvara, Indra, the Moon, the stars, fire, the serpents, the plants, the Brahmanas, and the waters is all born from the Sun. The entire radiance of all luminous things and the brilliance that exists in all worlds is distributed by the Sun himself as he plays. The Sun is the soul of all, the lord of all worlds, a portion of Ganesha, the root of the three worlds, and the deity in the form of action.

Thus ends the twelfth chapter entitled "Description of the Solar Sphere" in the second section of the glorious Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 13 : Description of the Chariots of the Nine Planets

Mudgala said: There are twelve other suns born from the partial rays of the Sun, and they shine while situated in their chariots during the twelve months of the year. Each chariot is occupied by deities, Adityas, sages, Gandharvas, Apsaras, village leaders, serpents, and demons. Dhatr, Aryaman, Mitra, Varuna, Shakra, Vivasvan, Pushan, Parjanya, Amshu, Bhaga, Tvashtr, and Vishnu are these twelve suns who shine in their chariots in the order of the seasons, starting with spring.

Pulastya, Pulaha, Atri, Vashistha, Angiras, Bhrigu, Bharadvaja, Gautama, Kashyapa, Kratu, Jamadagni, and Kaushika—these sages, who are knowers of Brahman, praise the Lord in front of the chariot with various Vedic hymns. Rathakrit, Rathauja, Rathachitra, Svabahuka, Rathasvana, Varuna, Sushena, Senajit, Tarkshya, Arishtanemi, Rathajit, and Satyajit—these village leaders hold the reins of the chariot of the Lord of lords.

Heti, Praheti, Paurusheya, Vadha, Sarpa, Vyaghra, Vyadha, Vata, Vidyut, Divakara, Brahmopeta, and Yajnopeta—these foremost demons proceed in front in order. Vasuki, Kankanira, Takshaka, Elapatra, Shankhapala, Airavata, Dhananjaya, Mahapadma, Karkotaka, Kambala, and Ashvatara—these twelve serpents carry the Sun in the twelve months, O Lord of subjects.

Tumburu, Narada, Haha, Huhu, Vishvavasu, Ugrasena, Vasuruchi, Vivasu, Chitrasena, Urnayuh, Dhritarashtra, and Suryavarcha—these twelve Gandharvas sing in front. Ghastanika, Rambha, Punjikasthali, Menaka, Sahajanya, Pramlocha, Anumlocha, Ghritachi, Vishvachi, Urvashi, Purvachitti, and Tilottama—these Apsaras please the Sun with their dances in the order of the seasons.

Thus, the deities reside with the Sun for two months each, nourishing the Sun, the treasure of radiance, with their own brilliance. The sages praise the Sun with their composed words, and the Gandharvas and Apsaras serve him with songs and dances. The village leaders hold the reins, the serpents carry the Lord of gods, and the demons accompany him. The Valakhilyas, surrounding the Sun, lead him at sunrise. These beings shine, rain, glow, blow, and create; they are said to dispel the inauspicious deeds of living beings.

They always revolve in the sky along with the Sun, seated in a chariot that moves at will and is as swift as the wind. By raining, heating, and delighting the subjects, they protect all beings until the end of the yuga. The Sun shines daily by assuming their forms. He, the Lord of subjects, is the cause of the arrangement of day and night, and he constantly nourishes the ancestors, gods, and humans. Thus, this Sun, a portion of Ganesha, is the Lord of gods himself, who, as the embodiment of time, regulates time.

The other eight planets are said to be bound to the Pole Star (Dhruva) and revolve around it, nourished by the rays of the Sun through wind-ropes. The Moon has a three-wheeled chariot with horses the color of Kunda flowers, and ten lunar rays are attached to its left and right. Just as the Sun moves through the constellations along a path, the Moon also moves, subject to waxing and waning, always supported by the Pole Star.

When the Sun is positioned beyond the Moon during the bright fortnight, the Moon is filled by one digit and continues to grow. During the dark fortnight, the Sun, through a single ray called Sushumna, nourishes the Moon, which is consumed by the gods. Thus, through the power of the Sun, the body of the Moon is nourished, and it appears full on the day of the full moon. The gods, who are consumers of nectar, drink the nectar-filled Moon in order over the half-month.

Then, when only a small portion remains in the fifteenth part, the ancestors worship the Moon in the afternoon. They drink for two periods of time. The remaining portion, which is pure and made of nectar, is the Moon's own nectar. The nectar that flows from the rays at the time of the new moon satisfies the ancestors for the month, and they remain content. The Moon is not destroyed by the drinking of the nectar-gods; thus, its waxing and waning are always caused by the Sun.

The son of the Moon (Mercury) has a chariot pulled by four lions as swift as the wind. The chariot of Bhargava (Venus) is made of gold and pulled by eight horses. The chariot of Bhauma (Mars) is beautiful and pulled by eight rams. The chariot of Brihaspati (Jupiter) is made of gold and pulled by eight red horses. The chariot of Manda (Saturn) is made of iron and pulled by eight dark vultures. The chariot of Svarbhanu (Rahu) is yoked with black lions, and that of Ketu is yoked with doves. These are the great chariots of the nine planets.

All are bound to the Pole Star with reverence by wind-ropes. The planets, constellations, stars, and lunar mansions are always bound to the Pole Star. It revolves and makes them revolve with the wind-ropes of all directions. Therefore, this wheel of light is supported by the Pole Star, O Lord of subjects.

Chapter 14 : Description of the Higher Worlds

Mudgala said: Above Dhruva (the Pole Star) lies the Maharloka, at a distance of ten million yojanas, where those who have attained the fruits of their kalpa-long merits reside in happiness. Janaloka is ten million yojanas above Maharloka, where Sanaka and the other sages reside, traveling in celestial vehicles made of subtle particles. Tapoloka is ten million yojanas above Janaloka, where the Vairaja deities reside, free from all fear.

Satyaloka is ten million yojanas above Tapoloka; it is the supreme abode of Brahma, representing the state of non-return. There, the teacher of the worlds, Brahma, the soul and creator of the universe, resides, eternally drinking the supreme nectar of yoga along with the yogis. There dwell the ascetics, the celibate Brahmacharis, the practitioners of vows, the sages, the perfected beings, and the priests of the Supreme Lord. The gateway to that realm for those yogis proceeding toward the supreme Ganapati is beyond description, radiant with a multitude of flames.

Beyond that lies the unsupported, formless void, completing the supreme Virat form up to Satyaloka. This entire expanse is fifty million yojanas in breadth, being ten times the size of the earth. Two million yojanas above the realm of Brahma lies the Shakti-loka, which is unsupported, radiant with light, and filled with brilliance. It is the refuge of the Great Maya, where those yogis who are free from desire and devoted to the Divine Mother attain. In that realm, which extends for ten thousand yojanas and is filled with divine beings, the Shaktis reside. By their own power, they create beings of equal form and, assuming the feminine aspect, play with them, being one with the universe.

Four million yojanas above that is the ancient Sun-loka, a mass of great brilliance. Those who are free from desire and follow the path of light (Shukla-gati) attain this realm, where the Sun resides. Other devotees of the Sun who are engaged in desireless worship also reach there, shining with the brilliance of the Sun. Its extent is equal to that of the Shakti-loka, where the Sun resides in a human-like form. Having created Maya, which is composed of action, from his left side, the Lord, known as Brahma, eternally sports with her, who is named Samjna.

Eight million yojanas above that resides Vishnu, who dwells eternally in Vaikuntha, possessing a form of pure bliss. The extent of that Vaikuntha realm is ten thousand yojanas in all directions. It is unsupported, eternal, and composed of pure light. Those Vaishnavas who are free from desire and devoted to bhakti attain it. There, men and women, filled with eternal joy and delighting in perpetual happiness, move about according to their own will. From his left side, the Lord creates Bhoga-Lakshmi, and from his right side, Mukti-Lakshmi, and he eternally sports with both. The Lord, the knower of supreme dharma, who is bliss itself, plays as he wills by manifesting these two aspects through his Maya.

Sixteen million yojanas beyond that is the Kailasa of Shankara, which is most wonderful. In that unsupported, sorrowless, and blissful realm, those human beings who are desireless devotees of Shiva attain. In that luminous realm, which always bestows auspiciousness, Lord Shambhu himself resides as he wills. It extends for ten thousand yojanas, where Shankara, the knower of yoga, sports through his yogic power. The left side of this great-souled Shankara is in the form of Prakriti, and the right side is in the form of a male. The union of these two is called bliss; the Shakti is Narayani, and Shiva is the half-woman, half-man.

That Shakti of the Lord appeared in three forms, differentiated by Shiva himself to bestow joy upon the world. Manifested through her, the great Lord Shiva, naturally free from delusion, sports eternally, supported by Maya in his own realm. Beyond that lies the Brahman known as Svananda (Self-Bliss). There is nothing beyond that state, as established in the Vedas. This Brahman, which is self-evident, appeared in four ways, and the wise call the union of these four as being one with Him.

The unmanifest, known as Shiva, is the Brahman devoid of delusion. Similarly, the Vaishnava Brahman is described as equal to it. Then there is the Atman-form, remembered as the solar form, and then the Brahman existing in the form of Shakti. The attainment of non-duality among these is called Svananda. Thus, in all these principles, their own respective bliss is spoken of. Brahman is described in various ways due to the distinctions of space and the like. There, for the sake of order, Svananda remained in its own glory. In the end, it attains fullness and is remembered as Ganapati himself. Svananda, in the union of all, is called the primordial Samadhi. In every place, Ganesha himself resides in his own bliss, through a portion of his essence, for the sake of play and for the attainment of all things for everyone.

Thus, O Prajapati, have the higher worlds been described to you in brief; their hearing bestows all desires.

Chapter 15 : Description of the Seven Netherworlds

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Mudgala said: Atala, Vitala, Sutala, Talatala, Mahatala, Rasatala, and Patala are known as the seven netherworlds. Atala is black in color and is situated ten thousand yojanas below the earth, extending for a hundred thousand yojanas. It is adorned by the son of Maya, Namuchi, and various other Nagas. Vitala is white in color and is situated ten thousand yojanas below that. There, Nagas and Asuras like Kambala, Shankukarna, Hayagriva, and Jumbhaka live in great comfort. Sutala is yellow in color and is situated below that. There, the bird-king Garuda, the Daitya king Bali, Kalanemi, and other Daityas and Nagas reside happily and fearlessly.

Talatala is known to be rocky, inhabited by the followers of Maya, such as Bana and Andhaka, along with other Nagas who live there free from distress. The Kalakeyas, those of great stature, and the Nivatakavachas also reside there. Mahatala is black in color; there, the serpent Takshaka and many other Nagas live in luxury. Thousands of Daityas from Hiranyapura also reside there, having cast aside all fear of the gods. Rasatala is a great region made of mountains, situated below that. It is inhabited by Nagas led by Vasuki and various types of Daityas. In that beautiful and pleasant region, radiant and venomous beings dwell.

Patala is blue in color and is situated below that. It is the place established by Shesha for the sake of his duties. There, Daityas, Suras, and Nagas always worship the great serpent king with devotion and wisdom. In these nether regions, various houses, forests, villages, and cities have been created. They are filled with wonderful enjoyments, diverse trees, birds, palaces, and lotus-filled lakes. O Daksha, the beings there enjoy pleasures equal to those of heaven; they are healthy, prosperous, and live for millions of years. They possess boundless life, great radiance, and are difficult for the gods to conquer, moving about as they please.

O Prajapati, thirty thousand yojanas below that, the serpent king Shesha himself always resides. He is endowed with devotion to Ganesha, is an expansion of Ganesha, an incarnation of a portion of Vishnu, and is known as Kala-Rudra. He is the thousand-headed one, possessing great fortune and immense strength, whose fiery breath could consume the entire universe. He is the only one devoid of death, hence he is called Ananta. At the end of Brahma's day, he remains after causing the dissolution of the world. The serpent kings led by Vasuki and the Asuras led by Prahlada always worship him with devotion. Other Naga women also worship the eternal Ananta with devotion to fulfill their various desires.

Below that, a thousand yojanas away, is the abode of Vishnu in his incarnation as the tortoise (Kurma). There, Varuna and others worship the Lord, who is the personification of the tortoise and free from all afflictions. A thousand yojanas below that, Vishnu himself resides in the form of the Boar (Varaha), the conqueror of enemy cities. Earth and others worship him with devotion, the great Lord with a fierce face who grants fearlessness to his devotees. This entire world is supported by Shesha, who is supported by the tortoise, and the tortoise is supported by the Boar-form of Vishnu. The Boar, being without support, remembers Ganesha in his heart, and by that power, he is known as the supporter of all.

Below that are the Hells starting with Raurava, which serve to purify sinners through suffering. These include Tamisra, Maharaurava, Andhatamisra, Suchimukha, Lohadanda, Taptavaluka, Khadgapatraka, Lalasya, and Krimibhakshya, among many others. Those who commit sins are tormented in these hells. I have described this cosmic sphere to you in brief, O Prajapati; it is impossible to describe it in detail. Your confusion arose due to the intensity of the spell of ignorance. Through the worship of Ganesha, this confusion will be destroyed, and then you will know everything as it truly is, without a doubt.

In this way, the world shines in all universes, which are endowed with eight coverings and supported by the Lord of Ganas. I have told you in brief this form of Ganapati, which is the embodiment of the universe and the bestower of all accomplishments. Any person who listens to or reads this with devotion will attain all desires, gain worldly enjoyment and liberation, and be freed from the cloak of sin.

Thus ends the fifteenth chapter in the second section of the glorious Mudgala Purana, titled the Description of the Seven Netherworlds.

Chapter 16 : The Description of the Life of Rishabha

Mudgala said: Priyavrata had a son named Agnidhra, and from him was born Nabhi. From Nabhi, through a sacrifice, the Supreme Lord Vishnu himself was born as his son. Being the embodiment of sacrifice, Vishnu took a physical form through a portion of his energy and resided in Nabhi's palace as his son.

Nabhi had a hundred sons, of whom Bharata was the eldest. Eighty-one of these sons became Brahmins through their penance. Nine others became the famous Navayogis, who were highly auspicious, liberated from the physical body, and followers of Ganesha. They were Kavi, Hari, Antariksha, Prabuddha, Pippalayana, Avirhotra, Drumila, Chamasa, and Karabhajana. They were eternal yogis, celibate, omnipresent, and honored by all as they wandered through the worlds.

In Jambudvipa, there were nine kings among the sons of the Lord of the People. Among these regions, the eldest son, Bharata, was the chief. Rishabha, having established Bharata as the supreme ruler over his nine brothers, divided his kingdom and departed for the forest. There, through various penances, he gradually became an Avadhuta—a great soul who, having mastered yoga, practiced it intensely.

By practicing various forms of yoga and purifying different levels of consciousness, he attained a state of natural existence, appearing at times like a dullard, a madman, or a spirit. He took refuge in the natural, Shiva-like Brahman, which is devoid of delusion, beyond the fourth state, and independent. Through this, he became a follower of Shiva, illuminating the path of Shiva, and wandered about, teaching people the essence of Shiva.

However, observing that even in his state of yoga there was a subtle dependence on the Brahman he had attained, he lost his peace and became astonished. He realized that in the true Brahman, there is no such thing as dependence or independence; Shiva is inherently independent, and one cannot become a true master of yoga otherwise. Thinking thus, he went to the abode of Shiva. The yogi bowed to him and was greatly honored by Shiva. Seeing the great yogis and disciples of Shiva residing there, he was filled with wonder.

Finding an opportunity, the great yogi bowed to Shiva with folded hands and spoke with a heart full of devotion. Rishabha said: "Salutations to Shiva, the embodiment of yoga. Salutations to the foundation of the world and the Lord of Brahman. O Lord, teach me the yoga of peace that grants complete tranquility. I am your servant and disciple; have mercy on me, O ocean of compassion. Through discernment, I have known the Brahman beyond the natural state, but I saw a sense of dependence there, and because of that, my peace was lost."

Mudgala said: Hearing the words of Rishabha, Shiva was filled with joy and spoke to the great devotee the complete wisdom of the Pashupata path. Shiva said: "O great yogi, you have asked about the path that is the savior of all yogis. Listen with a focused mind, and I will tell you with great affection. The Yoga of Peace was described in the Vedas long ago; it is the yoga of Ganesha, always served by yogis. Everything originates from him and rests upon him. In the end, through yoga, everything merges back into his form.

Listen to the secret of the Vedas, as stated in the Samaveda, by which you will undoubtedly attain peace. Everything that is perceived or unperceived, consisting of mind and speech, is the 'Ga' sound, and within it, the 'Ga' is Brahman. That which is beyond mind and speech, existing in the state of yoga and non-yoga, is the 'Na' sound, and the 'Na' is established there. O wise one, various forms of Brahman are born from the 'Ga' sound, and those that are of the nature of restraint are described by yogis as born from the 'Na' sound.

The 'Ga' and 'Na' sounds are the names of Ganesha. Know, O yogi, that these are the forms of Brahman from the mouth of the Vedas. Their master is Ganesha, who exists in the form of yoga. Worship him according to the prescribed path of peace, my son. Perceive everything that is of mind and speech as the form of Ganesha, and realize that which is beyond mind and speech as his true essence. Everything of mind and speech is called the form of Maya, while that which is beyond them is considered his true nature. He is known as the illuminator of the five states of the mind; therefore, he is called Chintamani, the Lord of the Ganas. Worship him, O great yogi, by remaining in the form of yoga, and by the path of renouncing the states of the mind, you will attain peace. Ganesha is beyond union and separation; the delusion arises from Maya—abandon it, and you will attain happiness."

Having said this, Shiva, who brings auspiciousness to the virtuous, became silent. Rishabha, with a joyful soul, bowed to him and went to the forest. Practicing the excellent yoga as instructed by Shiva, the former yogi gradually became the embodiment of peace. Yet, the sage did not forget Ganesha for a moment. Because of this, Ganesha himself came to him. Seeing Ganesha arrive, bound by the power of devotion, the sage bowed down fully, his voice choked with emotion. With his body covered in goosebumps and eyes filled with tears, the glorious sage praised the Lord of the Ganas with folded hands and a joyful heart.

Rishabha said: "Salutations to you, O Ganesha, the embodiment of Brahman, the ocean of compassion. Salutations to the Lord of the Ganas, who is beyond distinction and non-distinction. Salutations to the formless, the eternal, the one whose form is the world, the infinite, the Supreme Lord, and the one beyond the beyond. Salutations to you, who are without beginning, middle, or end, the embodiment of qualities, the Lord of qualities, the creator, protector, and destroyer of the world. Salutations to you, who take many incarnations to protect your devotees, who are the essence of both gods and demons, and who are worshipped by both. Salutations to you, the yoga, the giver of yoga, the embodiment of yoga, the Lord of yogas, and the Lord of Brahman. Salutations to you, the beginning of all, the leader of the Ganas, the one worshipped by all, the seeker (Dhundhi), the one who remains at the end of all, and the remover of obstacles. Salutations to you, Heramba, who dwells in your own bliss, served by the siddhas, the Lord of success and wisdom, the one who performs various divine plays. Salutations to the giver of Dharma, Artha, Kama, and Moksha. What can I praise you with, O Lord of the Ganas, who are the embodiment of yoga and peace, whom even the Vedas and Shiva cannot fully praise? Yet, I have praised you according to my limited understanding. Grant me your firm and unwavering devotion."

Mudgala said: Having said this, the glorious sage fell to the ground, holding the feet of Ganesha. Ganesha raised him up with respect and spoke to Rishabha, the best of devotees, the disciple of Shiva, the wise one, and the eternal Vishnu himself.

Ganesha said: "O sinless one, my devotion will be unwavering in you. The hymn you have composed will be a source of peace. I will grant whatever is desired by the one who recites this hymn, and there is no doubt that hearing it with a heart full of devotion will bear fruit."

Having said this, the Lord Ganesha, the beloved of the Ganas, disappeared. Rishabha remained forever peaceful, a yogi who had attained the state of Ganesha. Whoever listens to this primary life story of Rishabha, or reads this highly meritorious account, will undoubtedly attain the state of Brahman. This has been told with devotion for those of peaceful minds to grant them tranquility.

Thus ends the sixteenth chapter of the second section of the glorious Mudgala Purana, titled "The Description of the Life of Rishabha."

Chapter 17 : The Description of the Teachings to Pulaha

Mudgala said: King Rishabha performed various spiritual practices that illuminated the path of Yoga. Initially, seeing these, the king, while living in his household, adopted them. However, being of poor intellect, he abandoned the path of righteous action and caused his subjects, who were devoid of dharma, to behave like animals. At that time, the Jain path emerged, and it was suppressed by Bharata, who followed the path of righteous action. In the Kali age, this path will exist in every house, and people, afflicted by the faults of the age, will adopt it. The path of the renunciant, which Rishabha adopted after leaving his home, is said to lead to hell when practiced by householders.

Bharata, the eldest son of Rishabha, was devoted to dharma and ruled the kingdom righteously, pleasing all his subjects. After handing over the kingdom to his son, the illustrious king went to the forest. There, he gradually withered his body through penance. Staying in the hermitage of Shaligrama, devoted to the meditation of Vishnu, that king, who knew the supreme dharma, performed severe austerities.

Once, while he was on the riverbank performing oblations to his ancestors, a sudden, terrifying roar of a lion occurred. Hearing that sound, a pregnant deer nearby jumped up in fear and fled into the river. As she leaped, her fetus fell into the river water. She went a distance away and died instantly from grief. Bharata, with a compassionate heart, rescued the fawn from the water and raised it with great effort. Gradually, the great soul developed an attachment to the fawn; he nurtured it and played with it. His entire life of penance was consumed by his attachment to the deer, as he failed to see the fault in his association.

One day, that deer, having joined other deer, went deep into the forest, and the sage did not know where it had gone. When Bharata did not see the deer, he was filled with grief and wandered from forest to forest. He could not find the deer anywhere and lamented; constantly filled with sorrow, he eventually died. At the time of death, he saw the deer near him, and due to the power of that attachment, he was reborn in the form of a deer. In that life, due to his previous penance, he remembered his past life. Possessing knowledge, he wandered in fear of further attachment. Abandoning the company of deer, he went to his former hermitage. There, free from violence and indifferent to the pairs of opposites, he ate dry grass and remembered the eternal Vishnu, while bathing in holy waters and longing for the sight of the righteous.

Once, while wandering in the forest, the deer, by the merit of his past deeds, saw the excellent hermitage of Pulaha. Some great sages, who were devoted to the worship of Ganesha, lived there. There, Bharata, in his deer form, lived near the sages, happy and devoted to the company of the righteous.

Once, Lord Brahma went to Pulaha’s hermitage. Seeing him, Pulaha and the others bowed to the grandfather. Pulaha, filled with great devotion, worshipped him and, having pleased the Lord, sat at his feet. Filled with emotion, Pulaha asked the great Lord, the primordial deity, about the nature of Ganesha.

Pulaha said: O Father, why are all creatures afflicted by sorrow, and how do they become liberated and attain the state of Brahman? Tell me this.

Mudgala said: Hearing Pulaha’s words, the Lord, the Creator, spoke to his son, who stood with folded hands.

Brahma said: Listen, my son, I will tell you what you have asked, for it destroys all sins and illuminates the path of peace. By ignoring the Lord of Obstacles (Ganesha), people become afflicted with sorrow and are born into all worlds, plagued by obstacles and fear. Whatever causes sorrow is undoubtedly a form of obstacle; it is considered the fruit of evil deeds, leading to hell and other horrors. Everything associated with worldly action is considered a provider of heavenly enjoyment, but know for certain, my son, that this is also a form of sorrow. There is no doubt that in the heavens, there is the duality of opposites; see that the attachment to male and female is also an obstacle that causes delusion. The gods live there in fear of demons and others; those who are fearless destroy them, but at the time of dissolution, they face the fear of death. The entire universe, with its creation and destruction, is considered to be consumed by obstacles and afflicted by the three gunas.

Without action, a man attains liberation; there is no doubt about this. But listen, my son, to what constitutes an obstacle to liberation. The embodied soul, deluded by the four bodies, is bound. By practicing the right means, it becomes free from bondage. The soul, deluded by ignorance, is considered to be everywhere; know, O wise one, that this itself is the form of the obstacle. The union of the body and the soul is conceptualized as Brahman, but through differentiation, it becomes dualistic. Through non-dualistic Yoga, one remains in one's own glory; that which is devoid of differentiation is free from obstacles.

The Sankhya philosophy, which is established in the Brahman-self, has no obstruction. If it is not established in the Self, then it must become one with the Self. That which is held in the Self is the true state; anything else is not, my son. That which arises from the perception of "other" is the form of the obstacle. Sankhya and Brahman are undoubtedly the seeds that hold the rise of the Self. It becomes seedless by itself. Therefore, it is never highly regarded by the Yogis.

The Brahman that is called "Self-Bliss" is the form of Samadhi. In that state, one's own union occurs in that form. It is called the state of non-duality. There is nothing beyond self-realization that provides non-dual union. Therefore, the Yogis have called that which is associated with obstacles as the source of duality. Because of the fault of association, it is revealed as an obstacle; therefore, it is never accepted in the paths of peace. The state of Yoga that is always described as separate is not a union with anything else. Because it is not Brahman, it is called "non-yoga" and is thus filled with obstacles. The fault of exclusion is said to be the cause of restlessness. By abandoning the sense of union and non-union, you will attain peace; there is no doubt, my son, that you will then be free from obstacles.

Abandon the five-fold nature of the mind, O best of sages. You will undoubtedly become one with the non-dual form of Yoga. The five-fold nature of the mind and the intellect is called Maya. The attainment of power in the five-fold delusions is called "deluding success." In that, the image of Ganesha falls due to the form of Yoga; it is said to be dualistic and filled with Maya. Abandon that image-consciousness, O Lord.

In the past, my son, I told you about our family deity. You have worshipped the form of Ganesha with devotion. Because of that, you have become a vessel for the peace of Yoga, O great sage. Now, my son, I have told you the form of complete peace. Always worship Him according to the rules with the Yoga of peace. You will then become a lord of Ganesha; there is no doubt about this.

Having said this, the Creator Brahma disappeared. Pulaha remained there, practicing Yoga while engaged in penance.

Chapter 18 : the Abandonment of the Deer Body by Bharata

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Mudgala said: Having gradually attained the peace of Yoga, Pulaha himself began to worship the idol of Ganesha daily with devotion. Always absorbed in meditation, the Yogi contemplated only upon the Lord. After some time, Ganesha, the Lord of the Ganas, went to the sage. When the sage, due to his deep meditation, did not perceive Him, Dhundhi (Ganesha), who resides in the heart, deluded him. He could not see Ganesha, who was hidden within his heart. Then, as if confused, he looked and saw the Lord of the Ganas outside. Suddenly rising, the sage bowed to Him with folded hands and worshipped Vinayaka, the remover of all afflictions, according to the prescribed rites. Having worshipped and bowed, he praised Him with folded hands, his body thrilled with joy and his voice choked with emotion.

Pulaha said: Salutations to the King of Obstacles, the remover of obstacles for the devotees, and the creator of obstacles for the non-devotees; salutations to Ganesha again and again. Salutations to You, who are Brahman, the form of Brahman, and formless. Salutations to You, the witness, the foundation of all, and the beginningless reality. Salutations to You, the Lord who is free from affliction, beyond reasoning, and of great splendor. Salutations to You, O Vakratunda (Curved-trunk), O Heramba. Salutations to You, who dwell in Svananda, the protector of devotees, the Lord of Yogas, and the bestower of Yoga. Salutations to the peaceful One, the form of peace, the Lord of peace, the bestower of peace to devotees, and the embodiment of Yoga-peace. Salutations to You, who take on various incarnations, the foundation of the universe, the Lord of gods and demons, and the One who pervades all. Salutations to You, who are worthy of worship by all, who exist at the beginning of all, who are the same at the end of all, and who take on many forms in the middle. Salutations to You, O Lambodara (Pot-bellied), who are devoid of beginning, middle, and end, the creator of creation, sustenance, and dissolution, and who reside within the infinite. Salutations to You, O Gajanana (Elephant-faced), who have the face of an elephant, the form of an elephant, the ears of an elephant, and are the complete One. Salutations to You, who have the mouse as your banner, who ride the mouse, who hold the noose and the goad, and who are praised through various glories. Salutations to You, the four-armed One, the embodiment of the four goals of life, the form of Brahman, and the bearer of a body.

When even the knowers of the Vedas, the Vedas along with their limbs, the Yogis, and gods like Shiva and Vishnu are unable to praise You, how can I praise You, the Lord of gods, the embodiment of Yoga? Therefore, I bow to You; may You always be pleased. Grant me a boon, O soul of Yoga, by which I may be satisfied; grant me firm, unwavering devotion to You, O Dhundhi. Having said this, the sage danced, overwhelmed with love. The Lord of the Ganas, pleased with this essence of devotion, spoke to him.

Ganesha said: O great Yogi, my devotion, which is firm and devoid of fickleness, will be yours and will grant you bliss. Whenever you remember Me, O wise one, you will see Me standing right before you, O Brahman. This hymn composed by you, which grants devotion, is said to bestow worldly enjoyment, liberation, and the peace of Yoga. Any mortal who reads or hears this will attain all happiness; there is no doubt that he will become like you.

Mudgala said: Having said this, the Lord of the Ganas disappeared right there. Pulaha remained there and worshipped the Lord of the Ganas. Bharata, the king of the earth, who had taken the form of a deer, was staying there and heard the complete knowledge according to the scriptures. The same knowledge that was told by Brahma to the wise Pulaha was always held in his heart by that king. Absorbed in meditation in his mind, he, born in the body of a deer, remained there, always happy, and dwelt in the presence of Ganesha.

Then, O Prajapati, listen to what he sang: "Alas, I have encountered a great and terrible obstacle that is difficult to overcome. Having abandoned my kingdom, I lived in the forest daily, remembering Hari, yet even there, due to association with a deer, I attained the form of a deer. There is no doubt that even the gods led by Vishnu are subject to obstacles. Who am I, a mere human, to overcome such a formidable obstacle? By the grace of the King of Obstacles, even Shiva, Vishnu, and all other beings overcome insurmountable obstacles. Blessed is the devotion I have practiced, which is Vaishnava and supreme; through it, I have attained excellent knowledge and purification. I have seen the great Yogi Pulaha, who belongs to Ganesha. Oh, what luck, what great luck I have, that I live daily in the company of those who belong to Ganesha, and being free from obstacles by nature, I worship the Lord of the Ganas."

Thus, filled with devotion to Ganesha, the noble deer, having meditated on the Lord of the Ganas for a long time, abandoned his body.

Thus ends the eighteenth chapter named the abandonment of the deer body by Bharata, in the second part of the Shrimad Mudgala Mahapurana, which is the final Upanishad of the Puranas.

Chapter 19 : The Killing of the Thieves

Mudgala said: In the lineage of Angiras, there was a preeminent sage. He had a son named Bharata, who was the best among kings. Being fearful of worldly attachments, this great sage, filled with wisdom, remained in the presence of his father while mentally meditating on Ganesha. Having previously heard the hymns and wisdom concerning the greatness of Lord Ganesha as described in the Vedas, he had firmly established that knowledge within himself.

Fearing worldly entanglements, the excellent brahmin acted like a dullard, concealing all his intelligence from everyone around him. To avoid marriage and other worldly obligations, he manifested complete dullness, behaving like a fool and remaining in a childlike state. His father, the sage of Angiras' lineage, performed his sacred thread ceremony and taught him the rituals of twilight prayers, purity, and conduct. However, the wise son did not grasp what his father taught; if forced, he would perform the actions incorrectly. Even when his father held his hand to guide him through Vedic rituals and purification, he would perform them only as instructed, but when asked again by his father, he would not remember anything. The great soul abandoned all conduct, appearing as if he were dull-witted.

Consequently, all the people in the world gave him the name "Jada" (the Dullard), and he was always referred to by that name wherever he went. Whether he was treated with respect or insulted, he remained indifferent, having abandoned all sense of ego like a dull person. People said that his wisdom was not certain, but the great fool endured all praise and blame alike. Because of his perceived dullness, his father did not arrange his marriage, which brought him great joy.

In time, his father passed away, and his virtuous mother followed him in death. He had nine stepbrothers, and the sage, who possessed the yoga of peace, fell under their control. Their wives were arrogant and showed him no affection; they fed him only leftover, disgusting, burnt, and chaff-filled food. His brothers did not treat him with respect, abandoned all affection for him, and called him a disgrace to the family. Yet, the great yogi ate even that disgusting, spoiled, and burnt food as if it were nectar, remaining covered in rags.

Even then, his sisters-in-law would sometimes deny him food, and the sage would go hungry for two or three days at a time. Driven by hunger, the wise devotee of Ganesha would wander from house to house in search of food. People would take him in to perform their chores, and he would gladly do all their work in exchange for food. Pleased, they would give him clothes and food, and he became a willing laborer.

Seeing this, his brothers brought him back to their home and made him their own servant, full of joy. Even then, the sisters-in-law gave him only disgusting food mixed with chaff, but he ate it as if it were ambrosia, showing no reaction. Once, his brothers placed the sage in the fields to guard the ripened grain day and night. The wise one, who knew the nature of the body and was equal in happiness and sorrow, stayed there and did exactly as told for the sake of food.

O Prajapati, listen to the wonder that occurred there. The goddess Bhadrakali was worshipped by a band of thieves as their patron deity. She had promised that if she received abundant wealth, she would provide them with a human sacrifice. Having obtained such wealth, the thieves sent their men, who arrived at the field by chance, weary from their night's work. They forcibly seized the sage and took him to their place. The yogi understood this to be the result of his past karma.

There, the leader of the thieves honored him with worship and offerings, and the excellent brahmin ate the delicious food. Then, the wicked leader, accompanied by loud musical instruments, led him to the presence of the goddess, worshipping him with great devotion. After worshipping the great Kali, the leader drew his sword to behead the sage. Seeing the end of his physical body, the great yogi felt no agitation; instead, he remembered Ganesha in his heart. He thought, "Whether this body falls today or lives for a long time, what does it matter to me? O Gajānana, I bow to you."

At that very moment, the goddess, filled with rage, emerged from the image of the great Kali, a devotee of Ganesha. She playfully snatched the sword from the hand of the thief-leader, severed his head with it, and drank his blood. The other thieves who had gathered were also destroyed by the sword. The goddess, intent on praising Ganesha, played with her attendants. O Daksha, the great yogi bowed to her and wandered off, a free-spirited soul, eating alms and constantly remembering Ganesha.

Chapter 20 : The Meeting of Jadabharata and Rahugana

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Mudgala said: The king of the Sindh region was known as Rahugana. Due to the impressions of his past lives, he was internally inclined toward the Self. The king made great efforts to attain peace but could not find it. With great reverence, he went to the Kapila Ashram located near the Ganges. Having heard from a noble Brahmin on the southern bank of the Gautami river, the king departed from his city, seated in a palanquin.

As he traveled, the king, being in a hurry, exhausted his bearers. His own men then seized others to serve as bearers. By divine providence, they captured the great sage, a descendant of Angira, and forced him to carry the palanquin. The great yogi, knowing his body was subject to the fruits of past actions, accepted this with reverence and became a bearer for the king, free from any distress.

Being a yogi who considered non-violence the highest duty, he observed the ground while walking and placed his feet only where there were no living creatures. Because of this, all the bearers moved slowly, causing the palanquin to sway, which greatly distressed the king. Filled with anger, Rahugana said to them, "Carry the palanquin properly and with great effort."

Terrified, they all said to him, "O King, this wicked man was brought just now; he walks like a drunkard." The king then said to the great soul, "O rogue, why are you moving like a madman in my presence?" Then, by the power of destiny, the descendant of Angira, recognizing the king as a worthy recipient who desired yoga, spoke without regard for his status.

Jadabharata said: All this is illusory, a form of delusion, and unreal. Where is the person to be seen among the king's men? We have cast aside the duality of the world and are established in equanimity. The king, despite his great power, cannot be our teacher. Why should we abandon the path of yoga and stay here? Does he speak out of ignorance, thinking we are subject to him?

Having said this, he remained silent and continued to carry the palanquin as before, the great yogi descendant of Angira moving while meditating on the Lord. Hearing these profound words, woven with the essence of yoga, the king was struck with fear and leaped down to the ground. Seeing the disheveled Brahmin wearing a sacred thread and bark garments, yet radiating eternal Brahman-splendor, he prostrated himself with his whole body and stood before him with folded hands. Filled with fear, Rahugana, who knew the principles of Dharma, spoke.

Rahugana said: O Brahmin, ocean of compassion, please forgive my immense offense. This act was done out of ignorance. Tell me, are you a Brahmin? Great souls, the Avadhutas, wander wherever they please, and you appear to me as one who wears the sacred thread. Are you Kapila, Narada, Angira, Pulaha, Kratu, Dattatreya, or Yajnavalkya? Tell me, O ocean of mercy.

I fear offending Brahmins, O lord of sages. There is no deity in the moving or unmoving world equal to a Brahmin. Brahmins are considered the very forms of the Supreme Lord Himself. They are the support of all actions and the foundation of all knowledge. Whatever is heard through the mouth of a Brahmin is as if it has come directly from the gods; there is no doubt about this. Whatever is given to Brahmins grants an imperishable reward. Brahmins are indeed accepted as the deliverers of mankind.

The gods are subject to mantras, and mantras are subject to the Brahmins. Therefore, the entire life of the gods depends on the twice-born. Any action performed without mantras is fruitless. The entire world is subject to action, and therefore, it is rooted in the Brahmin. Those who are established in action, austerity, and knowledge are the best of the twice-born; they are yogis and serve as saviors in this world.

Brahmins are the gurus of all and are accepted as the givers of knowledge. There is no doubt that Brahmins are the direct form of Brahman. Even those like Shiva fall by merely insulting them, and by serving them, a person attains everything. Even the lords of the gods, such as Vishnu, have fallen into incarnations due to the curse of Brahmins and were restored by them. Therefore, I fear you, O lord of sages. Tell me who you are, O great-minded one. I do not fear even Vishnu, Shiva, and other gods as much as I fear you.

Having said this, he again took hold of his feet and bowed down. Placing his head upon the lotus feet of the sage, he wept, overwhelmed by fear. Then, the compassionate Jadabharata, having lifted the king, who was bowed in humility, spoke to him with gentle words.

Thus ends the twentieth chapter, titled "The Meeting of Jadabharata and Rahugana," in the second section of the great Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 21 : Describing the Attainment of Siddhi by Rahugana

Jadabharata said: I am known as Jadabharata, born in the lineage of Angira. Although I was formerly Bharata, the son of Svayambhuva, I am now called 'jada' (inert/dull) because of that state. O king, do not worry; the insult you offered to my body out of ignorance will not result in any sin for you. Go wherever you wish, O king; you have no fear from me. I shall go my own way, controlled by my past karma.

Mudgala said: Having heard these excellent words from the foremost of yogis, the highly illustrious Rahugana bowed to him and spoke.

Rahugana said: My lineage, my parents, my knowledge, my hermitage, my penance, my learning, my fame, and my charity have all become blessed by the sight of your feet. I am the ruler of the Sindhu land, known as Rahugana. O best of sages, I have renounced my kingdom and worldly attachments. I am going to see Kapila, who resides on the banks of the Gautami, for the sake of peace. You are the embodiment of Brahman himself, and I have attained your vision by divine grace. O sage, O best of the virtuous, please liberate me, your disciple, through the knowledge of Yoga. Tell me of that peace by which I may become tranquil.

Hearing these words, the highly radiant Angirasa, filled with supreme compassion, spoke to the king.

He said: Listen, O king, I shall tell you that which is difficult to grasp and the secret of the Vedas, yet it is the supreme path to peace through Yoga. The body is said to be of four types, and the dweller within the body is also of four types. Due to illusion, the one appears as many. That which is called Brahman in the bodies is seen as a mere point. The Brahman within the embodied beings is indeed the 'I' (Self), which is of the nature of the primordial essence. Their union is described as the nature of consciousness. This Brahman, rising of its own accord, is considered to be of various kinds due to mental constructs.

O king, when consciousness is destroyed, no further rising occurs. That Brahman, which is of the nature of Sankhya, is solely absorbed in its own bliss. Where there is no supreme bliss, how can there be self-bliss? Therefore, the Brahman that is beyond and endowed with rising is proclaimed. The seed of that rising, which is distinct and supreme, is fixed in the nature of self-bliss due to the absence of consciousness. Know, O best of kings, that which is devoid of rising from itself or another is the indicator of self-bliss, and it is the form of non-dual union.

Self-bliss is of five types and exists everywhere. The fourth is the 'neti-neti' (not this, not this) form, which is equal to both truth and untruth. This is what is called rising from itself and another. The experience of both is its form. From these two, the Vedas describe Brahman as various, like food and the like. Know, O best of kings, that the world is likewise of various forms. Everything consisting of name and form is declared to be unreal. The Brahman associated with Shakti is what the knowers of the Vedas describe. That which is of the nature of name and form is always considered to be fragmented. The immortal, solar Self is that Brahman, the illuminator of all.

Vishnu, known as Bliss, is the one who makes them non-dual. That Brahman is equal in both, being the essence of both existence and non-existence. Shiva is known as the doer of the 'neti' (negation), the fourth state, which is devoid of the three. That which is unmanifest, free from delusion, and self-dependent is considered Brahman. The union of the four, where non-duality is perceived, is the Lord Vinayaka, who is of the nature of the Self and endowed with Maya.

The one who destroys this union and is the non-union everywhere, that Ganesha is called the one without Maya by the yogis, O king. In non-union, O king, the cessation of yogis occurs. Because it is devoid of its own distinctions, that Brahman is called 'ayoga' (non-union). Therefore, it is attained through the cessation known as self-destruction. Brahman, which is devoid of all unions caused by Maya, is what is known. The yogi does not see union or non-union in Yoga; that very Lord of the Ganas is known as the embodiment of the peace of Yoga.

Whether associated with Maya or the wielder of Maya, or devoid of Maya and absolute—that is merely an illusion which should always be abandoned by yogis. The mind is said to be of five types, and that is the supreme wealth, O wise one. By abandoning the two types of delusion, you will become the embodiment of peace.

Having said this, the great yogi became silent. The king bowed to him and, having renounced his kingdom, went to the forest. Thus, King Rahugana, having practiced accordingly, attained peace and became a devotee of Ganesha.

Thus ends the twenty-first chapter, describing the attainment of Siddhi by Rahugana, in the second part of the great Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 22 : the Story of Jadabharata

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Mudgala said: Then the sage of the Angirasa lineage wandered upon the earth and, by divine providence, arrived at the sacred region of Mayuresha. Seeing and recognizing the place of the previous incarnation of the great Lord Ganesha, he remained there, meditating like one dull-witted. After a year had passed, Ganesha himself, mounted on his mouse and accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi, came to grant him a vision. Seeing Him, the sage first bowed down and then, with folded hands, praised the Lord, his body trembling with joy, eyes filled with tears, and immersed in the sentiment of devotion.

Jadabharata said: Salutations to the embodiment of peace, the giver of peace, and the compassionate one. Salutations to you, O Lord of obstacles, O Heramba, salutations again and again. Salutations to you, the Lord of success and its bestower, the destroyer of the fears of devotees, the lover of devotion, and the devotee of your devotees. Salutations to you, O Lord of intellect, the giver of wisdom, the essence of all knowledge and arts, the light of knowledge, O Ganesha, salutations again and again. Salutations to you, O rider of the mouse, the four-armed one, the dweller of Svananda, O Lord with the curved trunk, salutations to you. Salutations to you, who possess various forms and engage in diverse pastimes, who are immeasurable, and who ever reside in the peace of yoga, salutations again and again. I am blessed and fulfilled, for I have seen the elephant-faced Lord. You, who are beyond the reach of the Vedas, have suddenly appeared out of your grace. What have I done, O Lord, that you have come for my sake? I now realize that there is nothing dearer to you than devotion. By meditating on you in my heart with devotion, I remained like one dull-witted; it is you who, enchanted by my devotion, have come into my presence. Oh, I have neither seen nor heard of anything like devotion, by which you, who reside in the peace of yoga, have been enchanted and have come here suddenly. Everything that exists is born from a portion of you, O Lord of Brahman. What can I offer you, O Lord of Ganesha, by which you will be satisfied? Therefore, I bow to you and offer this body to you. Grant me a boon, O mighty-armed one, by which I may be satisfied. Grant me, O Lord, O ocean of mercy, firm and unwavering devotion at your feet; I ask for nothing else ever.

Having said this, he bowed to the Lord of the Ganas again and again. The great yogi danced with love, and the Lord of the Ganas spoke to him.

Ganesha said: O best of sages, your devotion to me shall be firm and unwavering, O Angirasa, there is no doubt about this. This hymn composed by you, which grants the peace of yoga, will be most pleasing to me, O fortunate one. I shall grant whatever the reader of this hymn desires, and the same shall happen for the one who listens to it; there is no doubt.

Having said this, the Lord of the Ganas disappeared, O Prajapati. The sage, filled with devotion, remained there. In the end, that best of yogis attained the form of Ganesha. Even today, the cave of that great sage is famous there. In the northern part of Ganesha’s region, at a distance of a krosa in front, is the place of the Angirasa, the dull-witted Bharata. Those humans and others who visit that place attain eternal liberation; there is no doubt about this. This most wonderful story of Bharata, which grants both worldly enjoyment and liberation, should be listened to or read by a person with devotion. Thus, many such kings were born in the lineage of Priyavrata, all of whom were knowers of dharma and devoted to various gods.

Thus ends the twenty-second chapter named the Story of Jadabharata in the second section of the glorious Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 23 : The Story of Dhruva

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Mudgala said: The son of Svayambhuva Manu was known as Uttanapada. He ruled the middle region of the earth righteously. He had two famous wives, Suniti and Suruchi. Dhruva was born to Suniti, and Uttama was born to Suruchi. The king, infatuated with Suruchi, was brought under her control and did not even think of Suniti in his mind. Once, the young Dhruva came near the king, wishing to sit on his lap, and stood by his side. There, he was insulted by Suruchi due to her wicked nature. Then, filled with anger, Dhruva went to the forest.

While he was on his way, he met Narada. The highly intelligent and righteous boy bowed to him. Having learned the entire story, the great-souled Narada instructed him in the twelve-syllable mantra of Vishnu. After bowing to Narada, he went to Mathura and performed penance there, meditating on the eternal Vishnu. For a thousand years, he performed supreme penance. Pleased, the Lord appeared before him due to his devotion. He granted him an unbroken and wonderful kingdom on earth, and at the end, the heavenly kingdom and the eternal abode of Vishnu.

Then, returning home, Dhruva bowed to his parents. The king honored him, seeing him endowed with great radiance. In time, the king made Dhruva the ruler and went to the forest to perform supreme penance. There, the Ganesha-devotee Narada came and said to him, "O King, worship Ganesha, who is devoid of distinctions like difference and non-difference. Shiva, Vishnu, and other gods, yogis like Sanaka, and sages like Vashistha worship Him, there is no doubt. Without Ganesha, peace is not attained; there is no doubt about this. The Ganesha has been served by kings starting from Svayambhuva."

Having said this, he gave the six-syllable mantra of Ganesha. By the power of the mantra, the king became a devotee of Ganesha. At the time of his death, the excellent king remembered the Lord of the Ganas and attained the state of union with Him.

Dhruva then ruled the kingdom righteously. Suddenly, the compassionate Narada arrived there. Seeing him, Dhruva bowed and worshipped his guru with devotion, standing with folded hands. The great yogi Narada, who possessed divine vision, spoke to his devoted disciple with compassion.

Narada said: Listen, O Dhruva, to these beautiful words. I have just come from Vaikuntha, sent by Vishnu for your sake, O wise one. Go to the region of Mayuresha, which is supreme and filled with Brahman on earth. Go there and perform the great pilgrimage according to the rituals, my son. By doing so, you will attain my eternal abode; otherwise, you will be beset by obstacles and will fall, there is no doubt. Furthermore, the compassionate Vishnu has said that you should chant the single-syllable great mantra of Ganesha. By the power of that mantra, at the time of the dissolution of the universe at the end of Brahma's day, you will attain peace when the pole star dissolves. You will become one with Brahman, there is no doubt. Otherwise, you will never attain yogic peace.

Then, the yogi Narada gave him the single-syllable mantra of Ganesha according to the prescribed rites, and the king became accomplished. Dhruva immediately arrived at the region of Mayuresha and performed the pilgrimage of the gates according to the rituals. He installed Ganesha with the help of learned Brahmins. Half a krosha away from Ganesha, near the eastern gate, he built a vast and highly valuable temple. It had a beautiful golden spire and was worshipped with great devotion. The king named it Dhruva-Vinayaka. Dhruva resided there for a year and a month.

Afterward, he returned to his city and ruled with great strength. In the end, he went to the heavenly realm known as Dhruva, which holds the stars. There, he remained meditating on Ganapati, and at the end of the destruction of the Dhruva-loka, he easily attained Svananda. A human who sees Dhruva-Vinayaka and hears this story of Dhruva attains steady worldly enjoyment and liberation.

Thus ends the twenty-third chapter named the Story of Dhruva in the second section of the great Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 24 : The Description of the Glory of Prithu

Mudgala said: From the lineage of Dhruva was born a king named Anga, who was radiant, righteous, respected by all, and protected his subjects like a father. This noble king had a wife who was the daughter of Mrityu (Death), and from her was born a son named Vena, who was extremely cruel. Despite his father's efforts to educate him, the son did not become virtuous, and the king, filled with sorrow, abandoned his kingdom and went to the forest.

Following this, the kingdom became leaderless, and thieves began to roam freely, looting the belongings of the people everywhere. The subjects, terrified, sought refuge with the Brahmins. The Brahmins then anointed Vena, the son of the king, to rule the paternal kingdom. Upon attaining the throne, the formidable Vena killed the thieves in all directions and ruled the lands, causing the earth to tremble. However, he abandoned the path of justice and began to act according to his own whims, causing great suffering to those who followed the duties of their respective castes and stages of life.

The people again sought refuge with the sages. When the sages warned Vena, he spoke to the noble Brahmins: "I am the one to be worshipped by all, and I am undoubtedly the protector. O great sages, serve me, for I am the one who always ensures your welfare." Hearing these words, the Brahmins became angry and killed the unjust king with their curse.

To ensure the continuity of the royal lineage, they churned his left thigh. From it emerged a man who was cruel and full of sin; he was short, dark-skinned, bearded, and had yellowish eyes. Seeing him, the Brahmins said, "Sit" (Nishida), and thus he became known as Nishada, and the sages gave him a place to live in the forest; the Nishadas are his descendants.

Then, the noble Brahmins churned the right thigh of Vena, and from it emerged a couple of great radiance. They were direct manifestations of Vishnu's energy and bowed to the Brahmins. The Brahmins anointed this excellent king to rule the kingdom. He became famous in the world as King Prithu, a ruler devoted to his duty and the establisher of the Kshatriya code of conduct. Under his rule, the people flourished, following their duties, and were happy and well-treated in cities and countries everywhere.

After some time, the people became devoid of food and, suffering from hunger, sought refuge with the king. Because of Vena's unrighteousness, the Earth had hidden all sustenance and was unable to provide it again to the king, who represented righteousness. Hearing of the people's suffering, the king became angry, took up his bow, and aimed a supreme arrow. He intended to strike the Earth and pulled the bowstring to his ear. As he released the arrow, the Earth, terrified, fled in the form of a cow. The king, with his arrow drawn, followed her like another Yama.

Finding no refuge, the Earth finally sought shelter with the king himself. Trembling, she stood with folded hands and said, "O protector of the earth, why do you kill me, a woman and a cow? O descendant of the Kshatriya race, protect me, for I have come to you for refuge." Hearing the Earth's words, the king said, "Listen, I will kill you if you disobey my command. If you wish to live, O wise one, then manifest all the various grains and food at all times, without doubt."

The Earth then humbly replied, "O prince, all the herbs and grains that I had consumed, how can I be capable of giving them back? Therefore, O best of kings, first make me level. I will stand in the form of a cow; make a calf of your own choosing, and I will provide you with all the herbs in the form of milk. Appoint a milker quickly, and then all will be well. Otherwise, O king, why would you kill me in vain?"

Hearing her words, the king used the tip of his bow to crush the mountains and made the earth level. He then designed the layout of cities and villages on the earth. From that time on, the construction of cities began. He accepted the Earth as his daughter, and she became known as Prithvi (the daughter of Prithu). He then made Manu the calf and acted as the milker himself, milking the Earth of all the six-flavored grains.

Thereafter, all the beings of the three worlds milked the Earth, each taking the food they desired with great devotion. Brihaspati made Brahma the calf and, acting as the milker, took the milk of knowledge. Rudra made the God of Death the calf and, acting as the milker, took the milk of life. Vishnu made the performer of sacrifice the calf and milked the essence of sacrifice. The Sun made Dhruva the calf and milked the light that sustains all. Vasuki made Shesha the calf and milked the food for serpents. Nandi made Shiva the calf and milked the great milk of wisdom. Thus, all beings, including the gods and the moving and non-moving creatures, milked the Earth as envisioned by Prithu.

Prithu worshipped the Earth, and she, cherished by the king, lived happily as his daughter. Having thus milked the Earth, the mighty Prithu ruled as the sole monarch of the world. He performed a hundred Ashvamedha sacrifices, accompanied by great charity. At his sacrifice, Brahma, Vishnu, Shiva, Indra, and the Gandharvas were all present. Sages like Kashyapa, Nagas like Shesha, and people of all kinds attended.

When the final sacrifice began, Indra, filled with anxiety, disguised himself as an ascetic and stole the sacrificial horse. He was spotted by the king's son, who released him thinking he was a holy man. Indra abandoned the horse and vanished. He then disguised himself as an ash-smeared ascetic and stole the horse again, but the prince did not strike him. Indra stole the horse six times in this manner. Prithu, of great prowess, then prepared to kill him, but Brahma pacified him, and the king did not kill Indra. He had successfully performed ninety-nine sacrifices. Urged by Brahma, Indra bowed to Prithu. Prithu abandoned his enmity and embraced Indra. All the gods were worshipped and returned to their abodes, and Prithu continued to rule his kingdom, protecting his subjects while remaining steadfast in his duty.

Chapter 25 : The Story of Prithu

Mudgala said: The excellent king, who was himself a manifestation of Lord Vishnu and possessed prowess equal to Him, taught the path of righteousness to his people. While the king was residing in his city and delighting his subjects, the great sages, Sanaka and his brothers, the sons of Brahma, arrived there. They were four in number, radiant like the sun, and always appeared as young boys. They were the ancestors of the ancients, highly respected, and free from all delusion. Seeing them, the excellent king suddenly stood up in excitement and bowed down to them, placing his head at their feet. After rising, he worshipped them with great devotion, offering them water for their feet, respectful offerings, and seats, feeling extremely joyful. He personally served them by massaging their feet with reverence and spoke sweet words, for Prithu was skilled in speech.

Prithu said: This earth is blessed today, and this excellent city is sanctified. My palace has been honored by your arrival. My lineage, my family, my parents, my knowledge, my hermitage, my penance, and my learning are all blessed by the sight of your feet. I, who was without a protector, have been made protected by you; there is no doubt about this. You have shown me immense grace, especially since you are so compassionate. What great merit have I accumulated in my previous births? Otherwise, it would not have been possible to have the vision of you, who are the embodiment of Brahman. Those who are desireless, established in Yoga, and omniscient, when they see a devotee, they act for his benefit and give instructions for his welfare. Therefore, O great souls, please give me your command now; by following it, I shall surely become fulfilled.

Mudgala said: Hearing the words of Prithu, Sanaka and his brothers, who were satisfied by his devotion and established in Yoga, spoke to him.

Sanaka and his brothers said: You are blessed, O best of kings, O Prithu of great prowess. Seeing your humility today, we are truly pleased. We have not come here out of any desire for gain, O wise one. We came to see you because we heard of your glory in the heavens. Give us permission, for we shall return to the eternal Brahmaloka. We are satisfied and joyful to have seen you and heard of your fame.

Hearing their words, Prithu, filled with devotion and humility, spoke again to the great sages.

Prithu said: I have enjoyed this kingdom, which is filled with various pleasures. Now, O sages rich in penance, please tell me the Yoga that leads to peace.

Hearing Prithu's request, the great sages, seeing him as a worthy vessel, spoke to him about the eternal Brahman.

Sanaka and his brothers said: You are blessed, O tiger among kings; your intellect is truly wonderful. You have attained detachment from worldly enjoyments, O king of great fame. Know that the Yoga of Ganesha is full of peace. Worship Him with great devotion, and through that, you will attain peace. Once, we all gathered at the abode of Shiva and saw Him sitting at the root of a banyan tree, absorbed in meditation. We all bowed to Him and stood there with humility. We awakened Him with hymns, and then Shiva awoke. Leaving His meditation, He said, "Salutations to Ganesha." Hearing this, we were confused and asked Him with doubts: "Who is this Lord of the Ganas, O Master? Why do You bow to Him? You are Shiva, the natural form of the Supreme, always independent." Hearing our words, Shiva was filled with joy and spoke the primordial knowledge of Ganapatya, which grants great peace.

Shiva said: How can the Brahman described in the Vedas be approached? It is independent and natural, O sages, while that which is dependent is of three kinds. Through the distinctions of Karma Yoga and others, the Vedas describe Brahman in various ways, but that Brahman is associated with Maya. The union of all actions occurs in Brahman; therefore, Brahman is considered to be of the nature of action, and similarly, it is considered to be of the nature of knowledge. The word 'Gana' denotes a group; it is a collection of external and internal differences. Words like 'Anna' (food) and 'Prana' (life force) are considered indicators of Brahman. All of them are in the form of Ganas, and their Lord is Ganesha. The various Yogas known to the wise, such as union and separation, are all embodied in Ganesha; there is no doubt about this. Since the Gana is said to be of the nature of Yoga, we are its Ganas. We bow to Him with devotion, as He is the very form of Yoga. Maya is said to be the cause of obstacles and the giver of delusion. People overcome it by the service of the Lord of Obstacles (Ganesha). Those who become like Brahman are known as the Ganapatyas. I am the form of Ganesha; the difference is only due to Maya. The mind is said to be of five types, and the intellect is also of various kinds. The lordship over them is the wonderful Siddhi. Ganesha resides there as the reflection. Being deluded by Maya, He appears everywhere. By abandoning the reflection and serving Ganesha, and by transcending the five-fold mind, one becomes the Chintamani himself. When I am Ganesha, O sages, where does the question of union or Yoga arise? I attain the Yoga of peace. I am never different from Ganesha; there is no doubt about this. The great Yogi will become one with Brahman through this Yoga of peace. This is my secret meditation that I have described. Therefore, I am a servant of Ganesha and always bow to Him, O sages.

Having said this, the great Yogi Shiva became silent. O King, our doubts were dispelled, and we, Sanaka and his brothers, began to wander the earth, the heavens, and the netherworlds. Being Ganapatyas by nature through the path of Yoga, we developed a longing to see Ganesha. At that time, the Lord of the Ganas was born as the son of Kashyapa. The King of Kashi, a great devotee, was devoted to Ganesha. The Lord went to his house to accomplish His own purpose. We went to Kashi to see Him, O wise one, and we saw Ganesha in the form of a Brahmachari. We bowed to Him, O King, and stood there with devotion. We praised the Lord, who had taken the form of a boy and was playing with other children.

Sanaka and his brothers said: Salutations to Vinayaka, the beloved son of Kashyapa. Salutations to the Brahmachari born from the womb of Aditi. Salutations to Ganesha, who is the support of Maya yet devoid of it. Salutations again and again to Heramba, who is controlled by devotion. You are the eternal Brahman, the Lord of all by Your power. You play through the distinctions of Yoga and non-Yoga; there is no doubt about this. You are the beginning, the middle, and the end; You are Prakriti and Purusha. You are the subtle sound and the manifest form, O Lord. You are the embodiment of gods and demons, taking the form of humans and serpents. You shine in the forms of water, land, and so on, O Elephant-faced One. You are devoid of everything, wearing a form free from Maya. Who can know Your supreme state, which is beyond Maya? How can we praise the Lord of the Ganas, who is always the embodiment of Yoga? Even the Vedas and Shiva are unable to praise You. We are blessed, we are blessed, for You have appeared before us. O Dhundhi, You are truly the family deity of us Yogis.

Having said this, we remained silent and bowed to Him like a staff. The Lord of the Ganas, moved by our devotion, spoke to us.

Vinayaka said: The hymn you have composed, which grants the peace of Yoga, will always be effective for those who read or hear it, O great souls. I will grant whatever is desired by those who are devoted to this hymn. You shall become Ganapatyas, just like Shiva.

Having said this, the Lord, though the embodiment of Yoga, played like a normal child with other children, just as He did when born to Kashyapa, O King. We went to the King of Kashi to see Him, and after being honored by him, we returned to our abode. I have told you everything, O King, about the great Yoga of peace. Worship Him, who is known as Ganesha and is full of knowledge.

Mudgala said: Having said this, Sanaka and his brothers departed to the realm of Brahma. Prithu divided his kingdom among his sons and went to the forest. There, through the practice of Yoga, he became a Ganapatya. With a humble mind and accompanied by his wife, he finally merged into Ganesha. Whoever hears or reads this story of the glorious Prithu will attain all that is desired.

Chapter 26 : The Twenty-Sixth Chapter: The Story of Pracinabarhi

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Mudgala said: In his lineage was born a king named Barhi, who was righteous, a wielder of weapons, and devoted to the protection of his subjects. He performed various sacrifices, being dedicated to the path of action, and covered the entire earth with kusha grass pointing eastward. Once, having gone to the forest for hunting, the king married the daughter of the ocean, and ten sons were born to her. They were all of equal appearance and conduct, and because they were of one mind, they were known as the Pracetas. For the sake of progeny, they stayed near the ocean performing penance, and there, at a lake named Narayana, they worshipped Lord Vishnu.

Knowing that the royal sage Pracinabarhi was engaged in rituals at his own home, the compassionate Narada arrived. The king worshipped him with devotion, standing with joined palms, and the best of sages, the giver of knowledge, spoke to the king.

Narada said: O King, tell me, what will you achieve through these actions? It is declared in the Vedas that rituals lead to bondage; there is no doubt about this. You have performed many great deeds through various sacrifices, which are always fierce, violent, and based on the slaughter of many animals. All these slaughtered animals, filled with great rage, are waiting for you with intensity to avenge their death. Now that you are aged, O King, abandon these rituals and take refuge in the path of knowledge, which is like the fire that burns the sprouts of karma.

Mudgala said: Hearing the words of Narada, the king was astonished, and knowing the sage's intent, he spoke again with humility.

Pracinabarhi said: O best of sages, I know that the nature of the world is based on action. I know that a living being attains results through action; there is no doubt. Tell me, O Lord, what is that knowledge which cuts the root of karma? O ocean of mercy, out of compassion, deliver me from the ocean of worldly existence.

Hearing his words and knowing him to be devoted to action, the best of sages spoke to him about the pure form of knowledge.

Narada said: Know that knowledge is yoga-based, and yoga is known as the state of non-duality. A yogi becomes one with Brahman through yoga. O King, see in this body the reflection of Ganapati; it is the soul-form residing in the heart, covered by delusion. O best of kings, the activity of the mind is fivefold: distracted, dull, oscillating, focused, and restrained. That which is known by a man through his heart as 'distracted'—know that to be the distracted state of the mind, O King. The mind that is 'dull' does not understand like a man who is foolish; that is known as the dull state. The mind of those seeking liberation is 'oscillating,' longing for Brahman, not immersed in worldly pleasures, nor yet fully merged in Brahman. The 'focused' mind is one that remains established in the form of the Self in all states; one should see that form through inner knowledge and the practice of yoga. The 'restrained' state is devoid of the body and the embodied, bestowing liberation, and is attained through the practice of yoga, ending in the union and disunion of the self.

O King, the mind is said to be fivefold, and that is the supreme intellect. Know that it resides in the heart and plays with Ganapati. The fivefold activity of the mind is where delusion is said to reside, and it is the form of divine power (aishvarya) residing in its own place. That illusion of divine power is a perfection that exists everywhere; it is said to be fivefold regarding dharma, artha, kama, moksha, and Brahman.
