It causes beings to wander here and plays with Ganapati. O King, I have described that Maya to you; there is no doubt about this. The intellect itself, in the form of knowledge, is called perfection (siddhi) arising from divine power. The reflection of Ganesha in both is known as the living soul, filled with delusion. It is always filled with Maya and longs for worldly pleasures. O King, see the illuminator residing in the heart, bound by karma. The fivefold activity of the mind is illuminated by Him; therefore, they call Him Chintamani, the jewel of Ganesha.

The body is said to be fourfold: gross, subtle, causal, and the fourth is the sound-form (nada), while the point (bindu) is their ultimate destination. The embodied soul, residing in the enjoyments of the body, is fourfold due to delusion. Know, O King, that even though it appears as many, it is one Brahman—'I am That.' Through such yoga, there is spontaneous rising, and Brahman is established in the Vedas. It is the truth, the rising beyond, and the supreme bliss. In the union of these two, Brahman is known as fivefold, beyond the distinctions of truth, untruth, and the like. Where there is the eternal union of Brahmans through the non-duality of yoga, it is called the bliss of samadhi, known as self-joy. The state devoid of this is the giver of liberation. When union and disunion are destroyed, yoga bestows peace.

This Ganesha, O great King, is known as the Lord of the groups (Ganas). The groups are of the nature of Brahman and should be known through the eye of knowledge. Worship that Lord of the Ganas residing in your heart, who is the illuminator of the intellect, the complete One, the Lord of wisdom, and the giver of peace.

Having said this, the great yogi Narada became silent. The king, having heard this, bowed down and spoke to the best of sages.

Pracinabarhi said: O Yogi, O ocean of mercy, I have been delivered by you today. The learned men near me do not know this supreme truth.

Narada vanished instantly and went on his way, remembering Ganapati, a yogi devoted to singing with his veena, wandering at will, and always happy. The royal sage Pracinabarhi, having quickly handed over the kingdom to his ministers, went to the forest, intent on the meditation of Ganesha. As the supreme knowledge was told to him by Narada, he gradually became a yogi of Ganesha according to the path. In the end, the king merged into Ganesha, who is the form of Brahman. Thus, O King, the best of kings became one with Brahman. Whoever listens to or reads the story of the royal sage Pracinabarhi, his sins are destroyed, and he attains everything.

Thus ends the twenty-sixth chapter titled "The Story of Pracinabarhi" in the second section of the Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 27 : the Story of the Pracetas

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Mudgala said: The sons of Pracinabarhi performed excellent penance for a thousand years, and being pleased by this, Janardana (Vishnu) arrived to grant them a boon, mounted on Garuda and appearing with four arms. Seeing Him, they bowed down, worshipped Him, and praised Him with joy.

The Pracetas said: Salutations to You, O Keshava; salutations to You, O Creator of beings. Salutations to the formless Krishna, the all-pervading Vishnu, the powerful Lord. Salutations to You, O Vasudeva, who are without beginning, middle, or end. Salutations to the destroyer of all ignorance and the compassionate bestower of knowledge. Salutations to You, O Lord of Lakshmi, the protector of devotees. Salutations to You, O God who loves devotion, who reclines on the serpent Shesha. Salutations to You, O Lord of Vaikuntha, the bestower of worldly enjoyment and liberation. Salutations to You, O Narayana, the peaceful one who is endowed with the quality of goodness (Sattva). We and our ancestors are blessed to have beheld Janardana. Please grant us a boon, specifically the power to create.

Thus praised, the pleased Hari spoke to them Himself: You shall have incomparable power in creation, and you shall be happy. There is no doubt that you have become perfected through your penance. Whatever you desire shall be fulfilled for you. This hymn composed by you shall bestow both worldly enjoyment and liberation; for those who read or hear it, it shall be the supreme bestower of My grace. Having said this, Vishnu, the conqueror of enemy cities, disappeared. They also immediately left the hermitage and went to their excellent home.

As they were traveling on the path, many trees grew in the road, obstructing their way in the absence of a king. Seeing them, they became angry and created a fierce fire, by which all the trees in the forest were burnt here and there. While only a few remained, the Moon arrived there, took the daughter born from the trees, and gave her to them according to the rites. She was born from the power of the sages and protected by the trees. Having accepted her, they attained peace and made the fire peaceful.

Having attained their own kingdom, they all protected their subjects with respect, like sons, and the Pracetas remained restrained. O Daksha, you were born from the Pracetas through Marisha at that time, being omniscient due to Rudra's curse and also endowed with brilliance. O son of Brahma, by the boon of Ganesha, you shall not perish, and you shall be a protector of subjects. Having established you in the kingdom, the Pracetas went to the forest and performed severe penance there with respect, for the sake of yogic peace.

There too, the yogi Narada, intent on singing the praises of Ganesha, arrived, highly pleased, acting for the welfare of the Pracetas. Seeing him, all the powerful Pracetas were delighted; they bowed, worshipped him with devotion, and stood with folded hands. The best of sages said to them: What do you desire, O Pracetas? Tell me, and I shall fulfill it, being pleased by your devotion. Hearing the words of Narada, the Pracetas said to him: O ocean of compassion, please tell us of the yoga that bestows peace.

Hearing their words, filled with great joy, he spoke of the entire yoga that bestows the state of peace. Narada said: The knowers of yoga and those who have become one with Brahman call the yoga of peace the Ganesha-yoga; worship Him, O Pracetas. I am non-different from Ganesha, and this yoga is the bestower of peace; it was told to me by Vishnu previously and is the supreme bestower of peace. By practicing that peace, I wander everywhere remembering the Lord of the Ganas, with a mind free from delusion and filled with joy. A person who always abandons the delusion of the five states of the mind and remains in the form of yoga undoubtedly attains the state of peace. Everything is said to be illusory, causing delusion and flattering the intellect; having abandoned that, O Pracetas, yogis become peaceful.

Having said this, the great yogi Narada went away according to his own will, remembering Heramba, filled with joy in Kailasa. The Pracetas also practiced the yoga told by him with respect, and by degrees, they became supreme devotees of Ganesha. In the end, O Prajapati, they attained the form of Ganesha, becoming Brahman-realized, peaceful, and powerful. Whoever reads or hears the story of the Pracetas, for him, both worldly enjoyment and liberation shall become happiness-bestowing.

Thus ends the twenty-seventh chapter named the Story of the Pracetas in the second section of the glorious Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 28 : The Description of Creation by Kashyapa

Mudgala said: Daksha, the son of Prachetas, performed excellent penance. After one hundred years, the Lord of the Ganas (Ganesha) became pleased and granted him a boon. By the grace of Ganesha, he became empowered and created progeny, including gods, yakshas, humans, and demons, by the thousands. When the various species he created did not multiply sufficiently, the Prajapati Daksha initiated the process of sexual reproduction on earth.

From him, ten thousand sons of great power were born. Daksha himself told them to engage in penance to further creation. Saying "so be it," they bowed to him and departed with devotion to perform penance. On their path, Narada, the son of Brahma, suddenly appeared. He instructed them in the eternal path of yoga. Influenced by his teachings, they all abandoned the path of worldly action and did not engage in the creation of progeny. Having abandoned the path of duty, they became followers of Ganesha, wandering freely, devoid of delusion, and having conquered their senses.

Learning that his sons were lost, Daksha created another thousand sons of great brilliance through Asikni. With a pleased heart, he told them to perform penance to facilitate creation. They bowed to him and departed for the forest with devotion. Once again, Narada appeared and imparted the same pure knowledge to them. They, too, became absorbed in yoga and wandered according to their own will.

Upon learning of this grave situation, Daksha became filled with anger and cursed Narada, the son of Brahma, who was of supreme nature. He said, "You have destroyed my sons by diverting them from the path of duty. You have caused those who were meant to follow the order of social classes and stages of life to become wayward. Therefore, Narada, you will be a wanderer, devoid of a permanent home. You shall not be able to stay in one place for more than two muhurtas (forty-eight minutes). In any land on earth, in heaven, or in the netherworlds, you will wander, O great wicked one; there is no doubt about this curse of mine."

Having cursed him, Daksha went to Brahma with devotion. Bowing to the Lord of the Universe, he said with humility, "O Lord of gods, tell me by what path I should create. Narada, with his wicked nature, has made my creation futile." Brahma then told him, "Create beautiful-eyed daughters." Saying "so be it," Daksha went to his place and began to create.

Daksha, filled with brilliance, created sixty daughters through Virini. They were beautiful and became the mothers of all living beings. He gave ten to Dharma, thirteen to Kashyapa, twenty-seven to the Moon, four to Arishtanemi, two to Bahuputra, two to Krishashva, and two to Angirasa. I shall now describe them in detail.

Marutvati, Vasu, Yami, Lamba, Bhanu, Arundhati, Sankalpa, Muhurta, Sadhya, and Vishva were the ten wives of Dharma. Hear about their sons: Vishva gave birth to the Vishvadevas, and Sadhya gave birth to the Sadhyas. From Marutvati were born the Marutvans, and from Vasu, the Vasus. From Bhanu were born the Bhanus, and from Muhurta, the Muhurtakas. From Lamba were born Nada and Ghosha, and from Yami, the Nagavithikas. Everything uneven on earth was born from Arundhati. The sons of Sankalpa are known as the Sankalpas. Through their sons and daughters, the entire world expanded. The eight Vasus are known as Apa, Dhruva, Soma, Dhanya, Anala, Anila, Pratyusha, and Prabhasa.

Aditi, Diti, Kadru, Arishta, Surasa, Danu, Surabhi, Vinata, Tamra, Krodhavasha, Ira, Muni, and Khasha were the wives of Kashyapa. Hear about their sons: Vivasvan, Dhata, Bhaga, Tvashta, Amshaka, and the gods headed by Varuna are all the sons of Aditi. All the Daityas are the sons of Diti, as accepted in the scriptures. Similarly, the sons of Danu are known as the Danavas, headed by Tara. Tara, Viprachitti, Shambara, Kapila, Svarbhanu, Shankara, and the mighty Vrishaparva are the prominent Asuras and Danavas I have mentioned.

From Surasa, a thousand serpents were born. Arishta gave birth to a thousand Gandharvas. Shesha and other great serpents are the sons of Kadru. Tamra gave birth to six beautiful-eyed daughters: Shuki, Shyeni, Bhasi, Sugriva, Granthika, and Shuchi. Through their sons and others, the entire flock of birds was born. Surabhi gave birth to buffaloes and cows. Ira gave birth to all trees, creepers, vines, and grasses. The sons of Muni are known as the Yakshas and the Punyajanas. Krodhavasha gave birth to the fierce race of Rakshasas. Vinata had two sons, Aruna and Garuda. The sons of Khasha are the sky-wanderers, ghosts, and spirits.

These descendants of Kashyapa, both moving and stationary, are those who are famous in this Vaivasvata Manvantara, as I have described. The sons of Arishtanemi are the embodiments of the Creator. The four wives of Bahuputra are known as the lightning-born. Similarly, the sages born of the wives of Angirasa are honored by the gods. The son of Krishashva is the divine weapon-wielder. These are born again and again at the end of every thousand yugas, performing similar tasks in all Manvantaras with their own names.

Having produced these sons, Kashyapa, the knower of the supreme truth, remained unsatisfied and performed severe penance for the sake of his sons. As he was performing penance, two sons of great power appeared, named Vatsara and Asita, both of whom were knowers of Brahman. From Vatsara was born Naidhruva and the highly illustrious Raibhya. From Raibhya were born the Rudras, the best among the radiant ones. Sumedha, the wife of the great soul Naidhruva, gave birth to the Kundapayin sons. From Asita and Ekaparna was born the great knower of Brahman named Devala, who is known as a teacher of yoga. Shandilya, the supreme yogi and knower of all truths, and many other sons of Kashyapa have been mentioned by me.

By remembering these, a living being has their desires fulfilled. There is no doubt that by reading this, one attains the essence of these primordial beings.

Chapter 29 : The Description of Vashistha’s Penance

Mudgala said: The noble daughter of Trinabindu, known as Ilavila, was given in marriage to Pulastya by the royal sage with great humility. From her, the great sage Vishrava was born to Pulastya. He had four beautiful wives with lovely eyes: Pushpotkata, Raka, Kaikasi, and Devavarnini. Devavarnini gave birth to the eldest, Vaishravana. Kaikasi gave birth to Ravana, the terror of the worlds, as well as Kumbhakarna, Shurpanakha, and Vibhishana.

Ravana, Kumbhakarna, and the valiant Vibhishana performed intense penance for thirty thousand years. Pleased, Brahma appeared to grant them boons. Ravana, proud of his strength, asked for a kingdom free of thorns and invulnerability in the three worlds—heaven, earth, and the netherworld—forgetting to include monkeys and humans in his request. Kumbhakarna, deluded by divine speech, asked for the boon of sleeping for six months at a time. The righteous Vibhishana, by his nature, asked the Creator for the boon of devotion to Vishnu and a mind inclined toward righteousness.

After granting these boons, Brahma returned to his abode. These great Rakshasas became famous throughout the three worlds. After conquering the three worlds, Ravana, along with his brothers, ruled for sixty hundred thousand human years. Eventually, the wicked Ravana and Kumbhakarna were killed by Rama, who then established Vibhishana as the king.

Vaishravana, having performed penance, pleased Lord Ganesha by chanting the single-syllable mantra. After ten thousand years, the elephant-faced Lord was pleased and granted him devotion to His feet and the friendship of Shiva. He made him the lord of the northern direction, known as Kubera, and he became famous among the gods.

Pushpotkata bore Vishrava’s sons Mahodara, Mahaparshva, Prahasta, Khara, and the daughter Kumbhinasi. Raka gave birth to Trishira, Dushana, Vidyujjihva, and Mahakala. These ten are the cruel-natured, powerful Rakshasas of the Pulastya lineage. Pulaha’s sons were the wild beasts, fanged creatures, many ghosts, boars, and elephants. Kratu is remembered as having no progeny in the Vaivasvata Manvantara. Marichi’s son was Kashyapa, who became a Prajapati. Bhrigu’s son was the wise and glorious Shukra, the teacher of the Daityas, who was devoted to study.

Being a devotee of Shiva and a constant worshiper of Shankara, he went to Kailasa for peace and was instructed by Hara. To attain the perfection of peace, the poet (Shukra) worshiped Ganesha. Gradually, he attained peace and became a leader of the Ganas (Ganapatya).

Atri’s son was the great and nectar-like Soma. From Vashistha and Arundhati, Shakti was born. From him, the great ascetic Parashara was born, who served Shankara with great devotion. Parashara asked his grandfather Vashistha, who was a master of yoga, blameless, skilled in his duties, and peaceful, about the cause of peace.

Parashara asked: "Shiva is free from delusion and is considered the eternal doer of nothing. How can delusion, which causes confusion, exist in the Brahman who is free from delusion?"

Mudgala said: Hearing Parashara’s words, Vashistha, filled with joy, spoke to his grandson about the eternal yoga of peace.

Vashistha said: "Listen, son, I will tell you the yoga of peace that Brahma told me. I wandered extensively for peace but could not find it. I went to the famous realm of the supreme Brahma. There, I saw Brahma worshiping an image of Ganesha. I stood there and bowed to it. After finishing his worship, he came into the assembly. I bowed to him and said, 'All beings are born from you, there is no doubt. They dissolve into you, and there is nothing beyond you. Why do you worship him? Who is superior? Tell me, for I have wandered for many years and have not found supreme peace.'

Hearing my words, Brahma, pleased, spoke to me as a worthy recipient. Brahma said: 'Listen, son, Ganesha is the giver of yoga and peace. He is the family deity of us all. We were born from him and placed in our positions by him. I constantly worship him, the bestower of all perfections. In the end, we all dissolve into him, there is no doubt. Therefore, Ganesha is to be worshiped first; he is remembered as the Mother and Father. Look at the title of Jyeshtharaja (King of the Elders) mentioned in the Vedas. He is the first of all. Whatever is the beginning will be the end; this is confirmed by the scriptures. Therefore, we will undoubtedly dissolve into him. In the middle, he plays in various forms as the elephant-faced one, and we are remembered as his parts. If you desire peace, I will tell you briefly. By abandoning the five states of the mind, you will become peaceful: scattered, deluded, distracted, focused, and restrained. The Chintamani is the one who illuminates the mind. If I am the form of Ganesha, then there is no separation or non-separation; there is no sense of difference, and thus delusion vanishes. Know the world to be Brahman, and know that the incomparable wealth and perfections are merely causes of delusion. The intellect is described in the scriptures as having the form of Brahman and the five states of the mind. The image that illuminates both is the great Ganesha. See the heart, which is deluded by Maya, as his form. Abandon the sense of the image, and you will attain peace.'"

Vashistha continued: "Having said this, the grandfather remained silent. I bowed to the Lord and departed. I went into the forest and performed intense penance, meditating and chanting the single-syllable mantra. Gradually, abandoning the delusion of the stages of yoga, I became the Chintamani myself, holding the yoga of peace. Even then, I performed severe penance to see the great Lord of the Ganas. After a hundred years, the elephant-faced Lord came to me, riding his mouse, embodying the peace of yoga."

Chapter 30 :  The Granting of the Boon to Parashara

Mudgala said: Seeing the Lord of the Ganas (Ganesha) arrive, filled with joy, he bowed down to the ground like a staff and praised Him according to his intellect.

Vashistha said: Today this earth is blessed, as are my parents, my penance, and my hermitage. My knowledge, vows, and everything else are blessed by the sight of Your feet. You are the doer and the cause of all causes, there is no doubt. You are described by those who know the Vedas as being both the destination and the path. You are the form of all, yet You are devoid of all, and You are the illuminator of all. You are the embodiment of the various paths of Yoga; how can I praise You, O Lord of the Ganas? Still, O Elephant-faced One, bound by the cords of devotion, You have come to grant grace, assuming a name and form, O Lord. With the wisdom born from seeing You, I praise You, the Lord of Brahman, who is praised by the Vedas and the sages. Salutations to the Lord of the Ganas, salutations to the witness of all. Salutations to You who are the form of all and known only to Yourself. Salutations to You, O Lord of Siddhi and Buddhi, the giver of success and wisdom, the One with immeasurable power. Salutations to You, who are the body of the unmanifest and the manifest, and the embodiment of Yoga in the union of both. Salutations to You, the light of the peace of Yoga, the embodiment of peaceful Yoga, the giver of Yoga to the yogis, and the Lord of Yoga. Salutations to You, the Curved-trunked One, the One-tusked One, the bearer of the signs that constitute the form of Brahman.

As he praised Him, his throat choked with joy. He danced with hair standing on end, oblivious to his own body. Seeing him immersed in the nectar of devotion, the Lord Ganesha, who knows the heart and fulfills all desires, spoke to His great devotee.

Ganesha said: This hymn of devotion that you have composed will be the grantor of righteousness, wealth, desire, and liberation. Your devotion to Me will be unwavering, O sinless one. Just by remembering Me, I shall come to your home, O best of sages.

Having said this, the Lord of the Ganas disappeared while I was watching. I remained in my heart, seeing Him and filled with peace. By the nature of Ganesha-devotion and being dedicated to the worship of His image, I, Vashistha, became a blessed one, worthy of being honored by yogis.

Vashistha said: O son, I have told you this auspicious knowledge that grants the peace of Yoga. Worship Ganesha with devotion through this very path.

Having been told this, the grandson Parashara bowed to the sage and went to the forest to perform severe penance. He practiced the great knowledge that grants Yoga, which was told by his grandfather, following the path of Yoga. Abandoning the earth, he himself became a yogi devoted to Ganesha. Lord Vishnu Himself, in the form of Vyasa, became his son. Even so, that sage, along with his affectionate wife, performed very severe penance for the great-souled Ganesha. The great sage made a vow for the sake of having a son, thinking, "May the Lord of the Ganas Himself become my excellent son."

After ten thousand years, the Lord of the Ganas, pleased and satisfied by his devotion, came to grant him a boon. Seeing Him, he stood up suddenly, bowed with folded hands, and with a steady mind, he and his wife praised Him.

Parashara said: Salutations to the Elephant-faced One, salutations to the One in human form, salutations to the One who is both man and elephant, salutations to Ganesha. Salutations to the attributeless One, the One who is the foundation of all attributes, the Supreme Soul, the transcendent Lord, the beginningless and eternal One. Salutations to the One with infinite faces, infinite hands, and feet, the One with infinite glory, the One who is the syllable 'Ga'. Salutations to the One who is devoid of all, the Lord who is free from Maya, the One who is always the embodiment of Brahman, the One who is the syllable 'Na'. Salutations to You, the Lord of both, Ganesha, the dweller of Svananda, the giver of worldly enjoyment and liberation. How can I praise You, O Lord of Lords? You are the Supreme Brahman, whom the Vedas and Vedanta seek; therefore, I bow to You. If You are pleased, O Lord of Lords, then become my son, O Lord of the Ganas, so that my mind may remain steady. You are our family deity, O Lord of the Ganas. We worship You daily as the embodiment of Brahman, O remover of obstacles. I wish to worship You as a son, having entered this world. Through that, my worldly existence will be successful and become the form of Brahman.

Hearing these words of the sage Parashara, the Lord of the Ganas spoke to His devotee, who was established in Yoga.

Ganesha said: O Brahmin, whatever you have prayed for will be fulfilled. I will become your son for the sake of slaying Gajasura. The hymn you have composed will grant all desires to those who read or hear it, providing both worldly enjoyment and liberation.

Having said this, Ganesha, the Lord of Brahman, disappeared. Parashara, with a pleased heart, remained with his wife.

Thus ends the thirtieth chapter named the granting of the boon to Parashara in the second section of the Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 31 : The Story of the Birth of Parashara’s Son

Daksha asked: Who is this demon named Gajasura, who is so incredibly fierce? Tell me his entire story, up to the point of his destruction. Why did the Lord of the Ganas have to become the son of a sage for his sake? Tell me everything, O Mudgala, for the birth of this demon was also blessed.

Suta replied: Hearing this pleasant request from Daksha, the best of the yogis narrated the entire history of that demon king.

Mudgala said: Listen, Daksha, I will tell you this ancient history, which destroys sins and grants both worldly enjoyment and liberation. The Mother of the Universe had destroyed Mahishasura, and he had a son of great power known as Gajasura. Distressed by the death of his father and desiring to kill the gods, he engaged in the worship of Shankara, following the mantras instructed by Shukracharya. He performed severe penance without food, propitiating Shankara through the five-syllable mantra. After ten thousand years, Shiva himself was pleased and came to the forest where Gajasura was performing penance to grant him a boon. Seeing the demon standing like a log of wood, the compassionate Shankara was amazed and spoke to his devotee.

Shiva said: O wise Gajasura, ask for a boon from me. I am pleased with your penance and will grant you even that which is difficult to obtain. Hearing the words of Shankara, the great demon was filled with joy; he bowed down and worshipped Shiva with the sixteen offerings. The demon king praised Shambhu with the Atharvashirsha and asked for a very cruel boon. Listen to it, O Prajapati.

Gajasura said: If you are pleased, O Lord of the gods, grant me this boon: let there be no death for me from any of those who dwell within the universe. Give me the authority and the kingdom of the entire cosmic sphere. I ask for no other boon than this, O Maheshana, which surpasses all else.

Shiva said "So be it" and disappeared. The great demon, filled with joy, returned home. Honored by his mother and friends, Gajasura was joined by all the demons. He went himself to bring his guru, Shukracharya, and then, along with the sages, performed his coronation. Gajasura became the king of the demons and Danavas. With them, he conquered the earth and marched to heaven. The gods, knowing the cruelty of Gajasura, fled and took refuge in Shankara, remaining there in great fear. Surrounded by demons, Gajasura then marched to Kailasa. Shankara, filled with anger, went out surrounded by the gods. Knowing that Shankara had come for battle, the great demon himself led his army forward, desiring war. A fierce battle ensued between the gods and the demons, and eventually, the demons began to flee. Seeing this, the powerful Gajasura, filled with rage, unleashed a terrible volley of arrows that destroyed the gods. Struck by his arrows, all the gods fell to the ground, their limbs severed, and they felt as if the final dissolution had arrived. Indra and the other guardians of the directions fell, tormented by the arrows. He struck Vishnu with a mace, and even He fell to the ground. Then, Shankara, filled with anger, took up his trident and struck the great demon with the force of the fire of dissolution. Though struck by the trident, the demon king did not falter; due to the power of the boon, all weapons became futile. Suddenly, the demon king came forward, grabbed the feet of Maheshana, laughed, and hurled him across the battlefield. Then, all the gods, terrified by the demon, fled in all ten directions. The demon then went to Shiva's abode along with his demons and installed the leading Danavas in the positions of the gods. They remained in the positions of Vaikuntha and others, honored by him, and the enemy of the gods made all the demons enjoy the pleasures of heaven. The powerful demon installed himself on Kailasa, while Daksha himself returned to earth surrounded by the demon kings. The wicked one stayed in the city of Mahisha with the demon lords and ruled the three worlds with unparalleled power.

After a long time had passed, all the gods, terrified, hid in the mountains along with the sages. They deliberated on how to kill Gajasura. There, Vishnu spoke these words, which satisfied everyone.

Vishnu said: There is no death for him from those who dwell within the universe; because of the boon granted to the demon, everyone has been defeated. The Lord of the gods, Ganesha, who dwells in Swananda Loka, is directly beyond the universe; therefore, his death will come from Him. Hence, let all the gods and sages worship that Lord of the gods with intense penance. He will kill that great demon and restore your positions. If you desire your own welfare, O gods, follow my advice, which will bring you happiness.

Hearing the words of Vishnu, the gods and sages said "Well said, well said," and remained engaged in penance. Some chanted mantras, some chanted his name, and others were absorbed in mental worship. Some meditated on Ganesha, some praised the Lord, while others remained without food, eating only air or leaves. Some performed fire sacrifices, some practiced the five-fire penance, and others inhaled smoke. In this way, they propitiated the Lord with devotion. After a hundred years, the elephant-faced Lord appeared before them and spoke these words, being fond of his devotees.

Ganesha said: O gods and sages, ask for the boon you desire in your hearts. I am greatly pleased by your penance and will grant you even that which is difficult to obtain. Hearing the words of Ganesha and seeing the Lord standing before them, the sages and gods bowed down to the ground like a staff. They worshipped the Lord of Brahma with supreme devotion. Filled with joy and with their hair standing on end, the sages praised him.

The divine sages said: Salutations to Ganesha, the embodiment of the protector of the Ganas. Salutations to you who exist everywhere in the form of the Ganas. Salutations to you who ride a mouse and are beginningless. Salutations to you who are formless yet possess a form. Salutations to you, Heramba, the protector of devotees. Salutations to you, the Lord of obstacles, who are fond of your devotees. Salutations to you, the Lord of success, the giver of success, and the seeker. Salutations to the giver of wisdom, the vessel of intelligence, and the one who dwells within all. Salutations to you, the Lord of Swananda, who are known only to yourself. Salutations to you, the embodiment of yoga, the peaceful one, the Lord of all yogas. Salutations to you who are without beginning, middle, or end, the creator and the destroyer. Salutations to you who are the protector and the Lord beyond the three qualities. Salutations to you, O Vakratunda, the Lord with the large belly, who dwells in the hearts of all, O Vinayaka. Since even the Vedas with their limbs are unable to praise you, what are we, O Lord, who possess only the knowledge from the Vedas?

Having praised Ganesha in this way, they fell at his feet, their minds fixed on his form, restrained by devotion. Rising again, they all spoke to the Lord Ganesha, the master of all, with folded hands.

The gods said: If you are pleased, O Lord of the gods, kill the demon Gajasura. We have been removed from our positions by him and are deprived of our duties. Grant us your devotion, which destroys all sorrows. This is the only boon we desire, O elephant-faced one.

Hearing the words of the divine sages, the Lord of the Ganas, out of his love for his devotees, spoke these auspicious words to gladden them.

Ganesha said: The killing of Gajasura is very difficult for the gods and sages. Nevertheless, I will do it at your request; there is no doubt about this. My devotion will become steady and very difficult to obtain. I will become the son of Parashara for the sake of this destruction. This hymn composed by you will increase my love; it will be complete and grant both worldly enjoyment and liberation, there is no doubt. O Brahmins, those who read or listen to this will attain all their desires; it will always destroy their sorrows and grant them great wisdom.

Having said this, the Lord Ganesha, the giver of all success, disappeared. He entered the womb of Vatsala and became the son of Parashara. The best of sages performed all the birth rituals, filled with joy, along with his wife. The child grew there, gladdening his mother and father with his own actions and childish play. In the fifth year, the best of sages performed his sacred thread ceremony and taught him the Vedas along with their limbs. He performed his marriage to Siddhi and Buddhi, who are eternal. He gave them to him according to the scriptures, as they were born from Marichi.

Thus, I have told you everything about the son of Parashara. Now, listen to the deeds of the Lord of the Ganas regarding the demon.

Chapter 32 : The Destruction of Gajasura’s Army

Mudgala said: Once, when the demon king was sitting in his assembly, the sage Narada, who was fond of mischief, suddenly arrived there. The demon honored him, and the great sage said, "Listen, O Lord of demons, I will tell you something that is for your benefit. In the forest, the gods and sages, filled with devotion, have worshipped Ganesha, the son of Parashara, for the purpose of your destruction." Having said this, Narada, who possessed divine vision, departed. The demon, filled with rage and intent on killing the divine sages, arrived where the gods and sages were staying, surrounded by his powerful and valiant demon army.

As the wicked demon seized all the gods and sages and raised his weapon to kill them, they invoked the Lord of Obstacles (Ganesha). As soon as he was invoked, Ganesha confused the demon's intellect. The demon thought to himself, "These sages and gods are undoubtedly under my control. I must punish them so that they submit to me. After some time, I will kill them by force." Having decided this, Gajasura said to them: "O gods and sages, listen to my beneficial words if you wish to live. Press your ears with your hands and strike your heads against the ground near my feet daily. Only then will I release you, O best of sages; otherwise, I will undoubtedly behead you all. You have prayed to Ganesha, the beloved of the gods, and he has been born as the son of Parashara; go and join him."

Hearing these words, Vishnu respectfully spoke to him, mentally invoking Ganesha and using conciliatory language: "We shall do the penance you have prescribed. Release all the gods and sages, and we will go to Gajanana (Ganesha). We will bring the Lord of the gods here, and together with him, we will perform the service at your lotus feet as you have requested." Deluded by Ganesha’s illusion, the powerful demon king released all the gods and sages. He added, "If you do not return to my presence by the fifth day, I will kill you all along with Ganesha."

Saying "So be it," they all went to the house of Parashara. Seeing Gajanana there, they bowed down to the ground. Parashara was astonished and personally worshipped the gods and sages who had arrived. Together with Parashara, they recounted the actions of Gajasura, praising him with various hymns. "O Lord, how can we, who are bowed at your feet, press our ears and strike our heads before that demon? Death is better than bowing to a low person. Therefore, O Gajanana, kill the gods, the sages, or the demons."

Mudgala said: Hearing the words of the gods and others, the Lord smiled and spoke to them, bound by his affection for his devotees. Ganesha said: "Do not worry; I will kill the demon in an instant. Do as I say, for it will be pleasant for all. The penance prescribed by the demon king is dear to me. Perform it with all your heart near my feet. Then, while you watch, I will kill the terrible demon in half a moment. Have no doubt about this, O best of sages."

Hearing Ganesha’s pleasant words, the gods and sages looked at each other and smiled. Shiva then said, "He is our family deity, so let us do as he says." Saying this, Shiva himself pressed his ears with his hands and struck his head at Ganesha’s feet, making a sound. The sound was soft and sweet, and Gajanana was greatly pleased. Then, Vishnu and the other gods, along with Bhrigu and the other sages, followed suit, which pleased Ganesha immensely.

Mounted on his mouse and adorned with his four weapons, Ganesha himself set out to kill Gajasura. Upon reaching the city of Mahisha, he let out a lion-like roar, which confused the demons and Gajasura himself. Meanwhile, the demons stationed in the thickets arrived, their bodies broken and torn, and reported the divine activity. The demons said: "O Lord Gajasura, the gods and sages, joined by Ganesha, have arrived to fight with great arrogance." Hearing their words, the powerful Gajasura ordered all the demons to get ready. He himself became fully armed, mounted his chariot, and set out, followed by his demon commanders and their army.

Gajanana, the dispeller of the fear of time, saw Gajasura approaching with an infinite army. Gajasura, in anger, ordered the demons: "Destroy all the gods, sages, and Yakshas." Commanded by their king, the victorious demons attacked the gods with vigor using various weapons. The gods, filled with anger and determined to die, struck the demons with their own weapons. A fierce battle ensued between the demons and the gods, with mutual destruction. A river of blood flowed, enchanting the heroes. The demon ranks were broken, suffering from the attacks. Seeing their ranks broken, the demons fled in all ten directions. Seeing this, the demon king was astonished.

Then, the cruel demon commanders and their chiefs entered the fray and struck the divine army with great force. Indra and the others fought a fierce battle, killing some and leaving others unconscious on the battlefield. The demon ranks fled in all directions, crying out in terror. Seeing this, the demon king was filled with extreme amazement. "I see a reversal; it is as if fire has become cold. My entire army has been defeated by the gods." Distressed in his heart, with eyes reddened by anger, Gajasura himself came forward to fight, mounted on his chariot.

Chapter 33 : The Story of the Slaying of Gajasura

Mudgala said: Seeing the great demon approaching, the gods were filled with fear and went to their Lord, the elephant-faced Ganesha, to report the situation. Hearing the news from the gods, Ganesha became enraged and set out, armed with his axe, noose, and mace. Seeing the primordial Lord riding upon his mouse, the demon king, puffed up with pride, addressed the radiant Ganesha.

Gajasura said: Why have you come to the battlefield, you lowest of Brahmins? You should be performing penance; why have you taken up the path of a warrior? You should fight with those who have conquered Brahma and the others; you will only meet a vain death here. Go back to your hermitage, O Brahmin; I will not kill you, as I forgive your actions born of childishness.

Seeing him speak thus, Lord Shiva, filled with anger, spoke to the great demon for his own benefit. Shiva said: You are a fool, O king of demons; this is no ordinary Brahmin. This is Ganesha, who is always worshipped by the most exalted yogis. He is the son of Parashara, and having been propitiated by his own penance, he will surely kill you by the power of his boon. Abandon your enmity and seek refuge in Ganesha, O demon king; if you do not, you will lose everything and meet your death. Stay in the netherworld, and Ganesha will not kill you.

Hearing these words, the demon struck Shiva with his mace. Shiva fell to the ground in a faint, and the demon also incapacitated the other gods with his weapons. Seeing the gods and Vishnu fallen and suffering on the battlefield, Ganesha took up his weapon. He empowered his axe with the mantra of destruction and hurled the weapon, which resembled Yama, at the demon army. The demons were killed by the axe wherever they stood, and the demon king watched as his chief warriors and sons were slain. He tried various efforts to stop the weapon of destruction, but all were in vain.

The great demon alone survived the destruction due to the power of his boon, as the axe could not kill him. Then, the demon rushed in anger toward Ganesha, abandoned his weapons, and prepared for a wrestling match. Seeing him, Ganesha, filled with rage, revealed his supreme form of Swananda to the demon. It was as radiant as infinite suns, adorned with an elephant face, filled with the light of lights, a yogic form bestowing supreme peace. The demon king tried to grasp the Lord standing before him, but like the sky, that form could not be held in his hands.

Considering this a miracle, the demon king was deeply bewildered. He thought: "Alas, I tried to grasp Dhundhi, but he is not within my reach. What is this mystery? This Ganesha is eternal, the very form of Brahman; there is no doubt he will surely kill me. My birth, my parents, and my hermitage are blessed. I will die by the weapon in Ganesha's hand, there is no doubt."

The demon king, having attained the Ganesha whom even sages like Shuka attain through difficult yogic practices, bowed to him with great devotion and desire for a boon. He addressed the Lord of Ganas with respect and emotion: "O Brahma, O Lord of Brahma, kill me, your servant, O Ganesha. Grant me this boon; I bow to you again and again. My mind no longer finds joy in the world after seeing you. By being killed by you, I shall surely become one with Brahman."

As the great demon prayed, Ganesha struck him with his goad, and he attained union with the Lord. Shiva and the other gods and sages were deluded and did not understand the Lord's actions; they stood there as if bewildered. Seeing the demon slain, they were overjoyed, and Ganesha withdrew his form and stood before them. Seeing him in his previous form, the gods and sages praised him, worshipped him with devotion, bowed, and danced before him.

The divine sages said: Salutations to you, the elephant-faced one, the beautiful Ganesha. Salutations to the son of Parashara, the son of Vatsala. Salutations to the brother of Vyasa and the uncle of Shuka. Salutations to the beginningless Lord of Ganas, who dwells in Swananda. Salutations to Ganesha, who creates through Rajas, sustains through Sattva, and destroys all through Tamas. Salutations to you, the supreme soul, the form of both Prakriti and Purusha, the form of pure consciousness, the absolute. Salutations to you, the self-realized Lord, the Yoga, the Ganapa, the form of peace, the Lord of Brahman. Salutations to Vinayaka, the hero, the enemy of the elephant demon, who dwells in the hearts of sages and protects them. Salutations to the protector of the gods, the Lord of obstacles, the curved-trunk one, the brave one, the single-tusked one. You have killed this powerful elephant demon, a great wonder in the universe, O Lord. With the demon slain, the whole world will now find joy and will be established in its own dharma, full of offerings.

Having said this, all the divine sages bowed to the Lord of Ganas and stood silent, their distress gone. They touched his feet and bowed their heads, and Ganesha was pleased. He said to them: "Those who are my most devoted followers should always offer such salutations. I am most pleased by them and will grant their heart's desires. There is no doubt that such devotion is dear to me." Having said this, Ganesha, along with Siddhi and Buddhi, vanished, and the gods and sages returned to their own abodes.

His father Parashara and mother Vatsala, hearing the news, fell into an ocean of grief. Then, the compassionate Lord, the illuminator of their hearts, taught them the eternal knowledge of yogic peace. "Create an image of me, install it with the help of Brahmins, and worship it daily with devotion. Perform your worldly duties while keeping peace in your heart; by seeing me in my yogic form, you will always transcend time." Having said this, Ganesha, who dwells in the heart, vanished. The noble couple did as instructed. They installed an immovable image that became the giver of supreme success, granting liberation upon sight and fulfilling the desires of those with worldly wishes.

This is the entire story of the slaying of Gajasura, the auspicious account of the Lord of Ganas that destroys all sins. Whoever listens to or reads this with devotion, O King, will attain both worldly enjoyment and final liberation.

Chapter 34 : The Greatness of Vyasa

Mudgala said: Parasara’s son, Vyasa, was the direct incarnation of Janardana (Vishnu). In ancient times, he was requested by the gods to divide the Vedas and other scriptures. Due to the pride of his knowledge, he did not remember Gajanana (Ganesha), thinking, "I, being the form of Vishnu, need not bow to anyone." Consequently, all the knowledge he had acquired from the Vedas and Shastras vanished. Distressed, he sought refuge in Brahma. Instructed by Brahma, Vyasa listened to the primordial Ganesha Purana from the Lord, through which he regained his wisdom. After performing intense penance, he became a devotee of Ganesha, a practitioner of Yoga, and the direct embodiment of Narayana himself.

Thereafter, Vyasa divided the Vedas into various branches for the convenience of humanity and the attainment of knowledge regarding rituals. First, he divided the Yajur Veda into four parts and established the four-fold priestly duties for the rituals of the Creator. He assigned the Adhvaryu duties to the Yajur Veda, the Hotr duties to the Rig Veda, and the Udgatr duties to the Sama Veda, all by universal consensus. He performed the Brahmana duties through the Atharva Veda. Having thus established the four-fold rituals, he became worthy of honor. He created various Samhitas (collections) with mantras, the Brahmana texts for rituals, and the Aranyakas for the sake of Yoga.

He appointed his highly intelligent disciple Paila as the keeper of the Rig Veda, Vaishampayana as the keeper of the Yajur Veda, Jaimini as the master of the Sama Veda, and the excellent sage Sumantu as the keeper of the Atharva Veda. The great-souled Vyasa created eight divisions of the Rig Veda, eighty-six divisions of the Yajur Veda, one thousand branches of the Sama Veda, and nine divisions of the Atharva Veda. The sage gave the Puranas in their unified form to his disciple Romaharshana, the Suta who was humble in nature. The great-souled Vyasa then created eighteen Puranas, which are categorized into Puranas and Upapuranas.

The eighteen Puranas are: Brahma, Padma, Vishnu, Shiva, Bhagavata, Bhavishya, Narada, Markandeya, Vamana, Agni, Vayu, Matsya, Varaha, Kurma, Linga, Garuda, Skanda, and Brahmanda. I shall also tell you the Upapuranas: Ganesha, Naradiya, Narasimha, Kapila, Nandikeya, Varuna, Daurvasasa, Ambika, Kalika, Maricha, Aushanasa, Bhargava, Sanatkumara, Kaumara, Maheswara, Saura, Parasharya, and Maudgala. For the sake of establishing Dharma and Adharma, he composed the excellent Mahabharata. The wise Suta learned all of this from Vyasa. These are the Ganesha-related texts that the wise should know, along with others that contain histories and praises of Ganesha.

Hearing these wonderful words, the sages were captivated and asked the wise Suta to resolve their doubts. The sages led by Shaunaka asked: "O wise Suta, why were the Puranas of Shiva and others described as Ganesha-related? Please tell us to remove our doubts." Hearing their words, the highly intelligent Suta, who was well-versed in all meanings, spoke to resolve their doubts.

Suta said: "O best of sages, the five-fold nature of Ganesha as described by Vyasa should be understood, and I shall explain their differences. The first, the Brahma Purana, is declared to be Ganesha-related because it describes the attributeless (Nirguna) Ganapa as the speaker of Brahman. That which illuminates the intellect in the hearts of people is His form, which the renunciates meditate upon. It is devoid of form, the eternal witness, beyond mind and speech, and should be known by those who have renounced the world. The four types of Yogis and Sannyasis meditate upon this Ganesha who resides in the heart and moves the intellect.

The last Purana, the Brahmanda, describes the Lord as the one with attributes (Saguna) in the form of Omkara. He is the one who plays in the universe and manifested in five ways. The Pranava (Om) is the form of Gajanana. The 'A', 'U', 'M', the resonance (Nada), and the dot (Bindu) are the five divisions through which He performs various plays. The wise meditate on the Lord of Obstacles (Vighnesha) as the soul of the universe, who exists in the form of the world and is characterized by the shape of the Pranava. The twice-born always remember Him at the beginning of the Vedas for the success of their rituals; thus, the Vighnesha of the Brahmanda form is called Saguna.

Similarly, among the Upapuranas, the Ganesha Purana is the first, which describes Ganesha in His various forms. He is both Saguna (with attributes) as the world-form and Nirguna (attributeless) as the mover of intellect. To attain knowledge of both, sages praise Him in His manifested form. They worship the Lord of the Ganas, marked by the elephant face and other signs, using mantras with deep devotion. By His worship alone, the Saguna and Nirguna Brahman is easily attained by the best of Yogis and ascetics.

The final Maudgala Purana is the essence of the Upanishads, where the greatness of Vighnesha is described in the form of Yoga. The Saguna and Nirguna Brahman in manifested form, and the union of the three, were revealed by Mudgala. Yogis like Shuka worship Him through non-dual Yoga, transcending the divisions of Varna and Ashrama. Therefore, the worship of Ganesha at the beginning is universally accepted, and there is no doubt that He is also at the end, especially in the Vedanta. He who is at the beginning remains at the end of the dissolution; therefore, Ganesha is present in the beginning and end of all Puranas.

The fifth form, the middle form of the great Ganesha, is described as having names and forms through His partial incarnations. Gajanana plays through the distinctions of Shiva, Vishnu, and others; it is within these that the Puranas were narrated by Vyasa. Therefore, there is no doubt that all Puranas contain the description of Ganesha, as stated by the infinitely wise Vyasa. Any person who knows these five forms of the great Ganesha will be freed from delusion.

Four types of Ganesha worship are always prescribed, granting success in all endeavors to the devotee. The first is idol worship, the second is the Pranava-form, the third is the bestower of intellect, and the fourth is the peaceful form. Thus, some people remember or worship the Brahman-form at the beginning of texts for all success. Others remember the intellect-bestowing deity residing in their hearts. Some worship or remember Him in His Omkara form at the beginning of all things. Others worship the deity in His idol form. Thus, Gajanana shines in the beginning, middle, and end in His Yoga form, bestowing peace and loving His devotees.

Instructed by Brahma, the powerful Vyasa, a devotee of Ganesha, created various divisions to attain Ganesha. To cut through the attachments of people and for the sake of Yoga, the Puranas of Shiva and others were described in sequence. This is the entire greatness of Vyasa told to you. Whoever listens to this with devotion or reads it will always attain everything."

Thus ends the thirty-fourth chapter named "The Greatness of Vyasa" in the second section of the glorious Maudgala Mahapurana, which is the essence of the Puranas.

Chapter 35 : The Tale of Shuka

Salutations to Shri Ganesha. Mudgala said: Vyasa had a son named Shuka, who was Shiva himself incarnate. Through his penance, he became the crest-jewel among yogis. Upon emerging from the womb, Shuka fled out of fear of worldly attachment, knowing that attachment destroys the knowledge of those obsessed with sense objects. Seeing him depart, Vyasa ran after his son, instructing him with various logical arguments. He called out, "Stay, stay, O great yogi! Listen to my supreme words. First, one should practice celibacy, then household life, followed by the forest-dwelling stage, and finally, renunciation. Only when these are followed in order does yoga reach perfection. Otherwise, by rushing, a person falls from yoga, or due to the loss of dharma, falls into hell. Therefore, for the sake of conquering the mind, follow the gradual path; there is no doubt you will eventually become one worthy of being served by the masters of yoga. Do not suddenly renounce, for it is difficult to master; with a fickle mind, you will surely fall."

Though instructed by Vyasa with such diverse arguments, Shuka, the yogi, did not listen, fearing the very nature of attachment. Knowing what was to come, Vyasa returned to his hermitage, meditating upon Ganesha with a worried heart. Meanwhile, Brahma arrived there. Vyasa bowed to him and narrated the story of Shuka. Brahma replied, "Do not worry, O sage. Shuka will not fall, nor will he cease to be Shiva; there is no doubt about this." Having reassured Vyasa and accepted his worship, Brahma departed, and Vyasa became joyful.

Shuka resided on the banks of the Narmada, engaged in yoga, contemplating the eternal Brahman in all things. He mastered the eight-fold yoga according to the Vedic path and attained the state of the supreme yogi. Lord Shankara himself, as Shukeshvara, resided there. Guided by Shiva within his heart, Shuka practiced yoga and attained the highest state through the gradual stages of yogic discipline. Having mastered the Brahman that rises from within, he attained the supreme knowledge of Sankhya. Absorbed in that Brahman, he wandered the entire earth as a liberated soul. Seeing the Sankhya path as groundless, he became doubtful and went to King Janaka with joy. Janaka explained the supreme state of liberation, and Shuka, the son of Vyasa, became free from doubt.

Observing that knowledge is lost in Sankhya, the sage was astonished and reflected deeply in his heart, imbued with the spirit of yoga. He realized that knowledge arises from the upliftment of the living being, and without knowledge, yoga cannot lead to such upliftment. He concluded that the yoga-based Brahman is devoid of such instability and remains in the form of Brahman. He realized that the Sankhya view of liberation, being devoid of attributes, was not considered the primary source of peace by the great yogis. Thinking that his father, Vyasa, who had divided the Vedas and composed the Puranas, was the all-knowing one who could grant him peace, the wise Shuka approached him with folded hands.

Vyasa was teaching the supreme yoga to other yogis. Hearing this, Shuka asked, "O Master, listen to my question for the sake of yogic perfection. Who is the giver of the yoga of peace, and what is the nature of the supreme yoga? O ocean of compassion, tell me, for you are the giver of yoga to the yogis. You are the master of yogis; I was misled by delusion." Hearing Shuka, Vyasa replied, "Listen, O fortunate one, to the yoga that grants peace. Ganesha is the embodiment of peace and the giver of yogic peace. Worship Him, O great yogi, and you will find peace. I was instructed by Brahma to worship Ganesha, and through that, I attained omniscience and became served by the masters of yoga. The word 'Gana' refers to the group of elements; the Lord of these groups is Ganesha. By the union of the internal and external, the supreme is attained by yogis."

Having said this, Vyasa gave him the Vedapada Stotra, composed by Vishnu, which grants the yoga of peace. By this, Vishnu became peaceful and happy in this world, becoming the protector and bearer of the divine name. Shuka accepted and practiced this wonderful path, becoming a devotee of Ganesha, radiant and revered by yogis. Shuka constantly recited the Ganesha Mahatmya and the Vedapada Stotra with devotion, day and night. This is the greatness of Shuka, O Prajapati; whoever reads or hears this will attain supreme happiness.

Chapter 36 : The Story of Gautama

Mudgala said: Angira had three sons who were speakers of Brahman, followers of Ganesha, highly fortunate, omniscient, and givers of happiness. The first was named Utathya, the second was Brihaspati, and the third was Samvarta, who was devoted to the science of Yoga.

Utathya was educated by Angira for a short time and immediately became a knower of Brahman and a follower of Ganesha. Brihaspati, gradually becoming an expert in the planes of Yoga, became absorbed in the practice of these planes and was naturally a devotee of Shiva. Seeing the independence in that path, he went to Shankara for peace. Shiva gave him the six-syllabled mantra of the Lord of Ganas (Ganesha). After learning the form of the Yoga of peace in detail and bowing to Shiva, he went to the forest.

He meditated on Ganesha daily and worshipped him according to the scriptures. A great longing arose in the mind of the wise one to have a vision of Ganesha. Through the path of Yoga, he himself became a peaceful Yogi and attained the state of a follower of Ganesha, becoming equal to Shiva. Pleased, Ganesha went to his hermitage. Being praised and worshipped by him, the Lord of Ganas was satisfied. He granted the great soul unwavering devotion and, after becoming invisible, returned to his own abode. From that time on, Brihaspati was called a great Yogi; he became the best among those established in Brahman and attained the status of divine glory.

Samvarta, initially a Yogi, was particularly mistreated by his elder brother (Brihaspati) regarding the division of property. Abandoning the world, he wandered naked like a madman, constantly meditating on the natural Brahman of Shankara while staying in Kashi.

Once, King Marutta, desiring to perform a sacrifice, went to his teacher, Brihaspati, who was in heaven. Indra, hearing of this, told the teacher, "O Brihaspati, do not officiate the sacrifice for Marutta, who is my rival. Be on the side of either the gods or the humans." At his words, the great King was abandoned by his teacher. Knowing Marutta was distressed, the sage Narada went to him and said, "O King, go to Kashi and bring the great teacher Samvarta. He resides there in the guise of a madman, but he goes to worship Shankara every morning. You must wait for him at the gate while holding a corpse. When he sees you, he will turn away, but you must persist."

Having said this, the happy Yogi Narada left. The King did as instructed. Seeing the naked sage, the King bowed to him. Though the sage tried to deceive him with his behavior, the King did not let him go. Hearing the story, Samvarta himself came and became the teacher for the King's sacrifice. When Brihaspati learned of this, he became angry. Indra, instigated by Brihaspati, threw his thunderbolt, but Samvarta made it ineffective through his meditative power. Fearing this, Indra sought refuge with him along with the gods.

After the sacrifice was completed by the gods and sages, Samvarta returned to his place. Through his deep meditation, he realized the Shambhavi state. He went to ask Angira about how to attain the supreme peace of Brahman. Angira revealed the Yoga of Ganesha, who is the embodiment of the Yoga of peace and truly wonderful. Samvarta practiced this excellent Yoga and became a Yogi established in peace. He came and settled in the blissful region, where he served Mayuresha (Ganesha) with devotion.

Their sons were also highly fortunate, omniscient, and eventually became followers of Ganesha. From them, the lineage of Dirghatamas and others continued, all of whom held Ganesha in their minds with unwavering devotion. Gautama was the chief among them, devoted to the path of peace and famous among the sages.

Once, there was a terrible drought on earth for twelve years, and the world was distressed. Seeing the suffering of the people, Gautama became compassionate and prayed to Shankara for rain. Shankara, the Lord, told his best devotee, "This drought has been caused by Time, which is the form of Brahman. No one in the universe is capable of changing it. Therefore, O best of sages, abandon your pride and remain patient."

Hearing Shiva's words, Gautama was saddened and surprised. Gautama said, "O Lord, what are you saying? You are the supreme authority. Grant my wish, or I shall abandon my body." Seeing Gautama's pride, Shambhu was surprised and said, "Listen, Gautama, there is no use in abandoning your body. Worship Vinayaka; through him, everything will be accomplished. Because he is the leader (Nayaka) of all, he is clearly called Vinayaka. We all act by his command; there is no doubt. Our movement according to the Vedic path is approved everywhere. If we deviate from the path, he creates obstacles. Therefore, O Gautama, take refuge in the Lord of Obstacles."

Having said this, the great Lord gave him the six-syllabled mantra of Ganesha and the best method for his worship. Gautama bowed to him and went to the forest for penance. Through intense meditation, he pleased the Lord of Obstacles. After a year, the Lord came to grant him a boon. Seeing him, the sage was overjoyed and bowed. He praised the Lord of Ganas with the Atharvashirsha. Pleased, the Lord said, "Ask for your wish."

He requested, "If you are pleased, grant me unwavering devotion to you and make me a complete follower of Ganesha. O Lord Vinayaka, remove the suffering caused by the drought. This is the only boon I ask." The Lord of Ganas said, "O best of sages, as you have said, everything will happen in your hermitage. If you sow seeds in the evening, crops will grow by the morning. Feed the sages who come to you with that grain. Everyone will be happy in your hermitage, and people will be free from the suffering of the drought. My devotion will be constant in you, and by my grace, you will become a perfect Yogi established in peace."

Having said this, Ganesha returned to his abode of bliss. From that day on, Gautama became a follower of Ganesha. He went to his hermitage and did as Ganesha had instructed, removing the suffering of the people. He fed all the sages who came from various directions with the grain that grew daily. Gradually, the twelve difficult years passed, and rain, which removes suffering, fell everywhere.

Then, the sages thought perversely, "We have all been fed by Gautama. He is blessed and will gain prominence. What should we do to destroy his prominence?" They created a magical cow and sent it to Gautama's hermitage, where it ate the sage's sacrificial offerings. When Gautama touched it gently, it fell to the ground and died. The deceitful Brahmins cried out in feigned grief and left the hermitage.

Gautama performed penance and prayed to the heavenly river (Ganga), which was on the head of Tryambaka (Shiva), and it appeared immediately. By bathing there, Gautama became pure; the great sin of killing a cow is removed just by bathing.

Once, the wise Gautama, while meditating, realized the deceit of the Brahmins. Filled with anger, the sage cursed the Brahmins: "Those sages who are ungrateful and have deceived me will become outcasts from the Vedas." The sages became confused by the curse and, realizing their mistake, became terrified. They all sought refuge in Shankara. Shankara, surprised, remembered Vishnu. He asked the great Vishnu, the giver of happiness to the twice-born, for the way forward. Hari said to Shambhu:

"O Sadashiva, the actions of those who are outcast from the Vedas are meaningless; they have no destination after death. Therefore, for their benefit, create scriptures that are deluding and lead to hell for those who follow them."

Then, Shiva revealed the Vama Tantra, and Vishnu composed the deluding Buddhist scriptures. The sages also composed other scriptures like the Kapala, which are particularly deluding. Shiva and Vishnu provided these for the sake of the ungrateful Brahmins. Those who follow them will go to hell, as they are devoid of the path of the Vedas. Those who follow these scriptures will be destroyed in a short time in the Kali Yuga. They will be attached to those paths and fall into the Raurava hell. In the Krita Yuga, people will be purified again and will attain liberation through the path of Yoga.

Thus, these scriptures were created by Shiva and others for their benefit; they should never be followed by the wise. Only the scripture based on the Vedas is the supreme giver of happiness. Any scripture devoid of it is deluding and leads to hell. It should not be followed by humans who are free from the faults of the Kali Yuga. It is considered fearful due to Gautama's curse. In the Kali Yuga, there will be many sinners; people should not follow the scriptures mentioned by them. Whoever listens to or reads this greatness of Gautama will be free from impurities and will be happy here and hereafter.

Chapter 37 : The Greatness of Narasimha

Mudgala said: Two sons of great strength were born to Diti from Kashyapa, conceived during the twilight hour, and they were extremely formidable demons. They performed severe penance for a thousand divine years. Pleased by the power of their penance, Brahma appeared to grant them boons. They asked for the boon that they should not meet death by any created being, and having granted this, Brahma returned to his abode. Because they had forgotten the specific condition of being killed neither by dry nor wet weapons, nor during the day or night, that became the very circumstance of their death.

By the power of their boons, they conquered the three worlds. The elder, Hiranyakashipu, established a supreme kingdom. He was a hater of the path of duty, a tormentor of gods and Brahmins, a wicked soul, and addicted to the wives of others and to intoxicants. The gods, led by Indra, being oppressed, approached Brahma and then sought refuge in Vishnu. Vishnu then assumed a terrifying form, the Narasimha (man-lion), which was extremely fierce and caused fear in all. He arrived at the demon king's city filled with rage and immediately made the world tremble with his terrifying roars.

The terrified demon hosts reported his actions. Hearing their words, Hiranyakashipu himself, surrounded by demons, went to fight that mighty being. The cruel demon approached the one who had taken the form of Narasimha. His body shone like Mount Meru and Mandara, and he appeared like the fire of destruction, eager to fight the gods. Surrounded by his sons, led by Prahlada, the great demon fought Narasimha with sharp weapons. Many demons were killed by Narasimha on the battlefield, and rivers of blood flowed in all ten directions. The Lord, in his Narasimha form, fought with swords and his own claws, appearing like the fire of universal dissolution.

Seeing this, due to his past impressions, supreme Vaishnava knowledge manifested in the great-souled Prahlada. In the meantime, the Lord, in his Narasimha form, was struck by the demon king with a mace and fell down, appearing unconscious. After two hours, he recovered, feeling strong and healthy, though he acted as if he were powerless to make the demon flee. Hiranyakashipu, thinking himself victorious, returned home. Prahlada, filled with devotion, engaged in the worship of Vishnu. He tried to enlighten his father, saying, "O Lord of demons, take refuge in Vishnu, who is the embodiment of bliss and is present everywhere equally."

Hearing his son's words, the angry demon struck him with his fist, asking, "Why have you taken refuge in my enemy?" Even then, the Vaishnava did not waver in his firm devotion; rather, Prahlada increased his love and devotion. Then, Hiranyakashipu himself tried to kill his son by water, fire, and poison, but he was rendered powerless by Vishnu. He tried to kill his son with various weapons, but Prahlada did not die, as he was always protected by Vishnu.

While this conflict between father and son was ongoing, listen to the story of Narasimha. Narasimha went to Vaikuntha, where Narayana was resting peacefully, and bowed to him in fear. Hearing the account of Narasimha, the Lord was astonished and pondered the cause. He realized that because Narasimha had forgotten to worship Ganesha, he had not yet been able to defeat the demon. He gave him the eight-syllabled mantra of Ganesha. Narasimha went to the forest and performed supreme penance, meditating on the Lord of Obstacles in his heart without food.

After one year, the Lord of Obstacles appeared and offered a boon to Narasimha, his supreme devotee. Narasimha bowed, worshipped Ganesha, and praised him with the Ganesha hymns. He prayed, "O Lord of Lords, O Lord of Obstacles, grant me devotion and victory in the battle against the demon." Ganesha replied, "So be it," and immediately disappeared.

In the meantime, Hiranyakashipu, sword in hand, began to kill Prahlada. As Prahlada remembered Vishnu, the Lord appeared in his Narasimha form and struck the wicked demon with his palm. The demon king, though unconscious, regained his senses, took up his mace, and fought Narasimha along with his soldiers. From the body of Narasimha, many other Narasimhas emerged and destroyed the army of Hiranyakashipu. Then, filled with rage, at twilight, the Lord seized the great demon and tore him apart with his claws. Since the claws were neither dry nor wet, the demon king died, and the gods praised Hari. Vishnu, in his Narasimha form, worshipped and remembered by the gods, disappeared, and the gods returned to their abodes.

Whoever listens to or reads this greatness of the Man-Lion, there is no doubt that he will attain everything.

Chapter 38 : The Greatness of Varaha

Salutations to Lord Ganesha.

Mudgala said: After Hiranyakashipu was destroyed, the demons made Hiranyaksha their king, and he conducted his rule accordingly. Tormented by the death of his brother and having conquered the gods, the demon king resolved to destroy them at their very roots. Through this contemplation, he attained a profound insight: if the rituals (karma) of the gods were destroyed, their death would inevitably follow. There is no doubt that rituals are the nectar of the gods; if their sustenance is destroyed, how could the gods continue to exist in their embodied forms?

The wicked demon king took the earth, which is the foundation of all rituals, and carried it away to the netherworld, playing with it at his own whim. The gods and sages then all went to Prajapati and reported the demon’s actions, explaining that the earth, along with all moving and non-moving beings, had been lost. Urged by Brahma, they all went with reverence to Vaikuntha, praised the Lord of Lakshmi, and informed Him of the great calamity.

Moved by compassion, the Lord assumed the form of a boar (Varaha). After worshipping the Lord of the Ganas (Ganesha) at Kailasa, He proceeded to the netherworld with great vigor. There, the Lord lifted the earth upon the tip of His tusk and brought it out. Hiranyaksha, desiring to fight, approached Him. Then, O Prajapati, a fierce battle ensued between them. At twilight, the Lord struck the wicked demon with His dry and wet tusk. The demon died instantly, and the Varaha, the son of the earth, emerged and established the earth as it was before.

Having established the earth, the Varaha went to Mayuresha. He is the form of the Imperishable Brahman and the bestower of knowledge to all. The earth, in a feminine form, approached Him as His consort. She is known as the foundation of rituals, the support of the Imperishable, and the bestower of happiness. Both of them, devoted and intent on worship, performed intense austerities in that region of supreme bliss to ensure their permanent residence there.

Pleased, Lord Ganesha, the leader of the gods, appeared before them as a bestower of boons and said with affection, "Ask for a boon." Then, having first bowed and worshipped the Lord of the Ganas, they praised Him with devotion using the eight-fold name and hymns. They asked, "O Lord of Obstacles, grant us a residence in this region of supreme bliss, which bestows the happiness of Brahman, for the sake of achieving perfect devotion."

Hearing their request, Ganesha, filled with compassion and knowing their intense devotion, spoke joyfully to the Sun. Ganesha said, "Your gateway, which is of the nature of the northern path (shukla-gati), is the supreme bestower of liberation. Place the Earth and Varaha within your own form, O bestower of honor." The Sun agreed, and both of them remained at the gate. Taking refuge in the northern part of Ganesha, they continued their worship.

I have thus narrated to you the greatness of the Varaha form. Hearing or reciting this bestows liberation upon all human beings.

Om Tat Sat. Thus ends the thirty-eighth chapter titled The Greatness of Varaha, in the second section of the Ekadanta-charita, in the great Mudgala Purana, which is the essence of the Upanishads.

Chapter 39 : The Story of the Birth of Chyavana

Mudgala said: Thereafter, Prahlada, the king of the Daityas, became a devotee of Vishnu. Having abandoned his enmity, he shone, honored by the gods. After some time had passed, the Creator (Brahma), the grandfather of the worlds, came to the house of that noble-souled king of the Daityas with affection. However, the Asura, having been gradually corrupted by wicked and great Daityas, became filled with the faults of bad association and ceased to respect the Grandfather. Enraged, Brahma cursed that fallen Vaishnava, saying, "O Prahlada, your knowledge will perish, and you shall become powerful (in arrogance)."

Having cursed Prahlada in this way, Brahma went to his own abode. Prahlada, influenced by the Daityas, became intent on destroying the gods. He did not contemplate Vishnu or Shambhu (Shiva), the lord of the immortals, in his mind. Having conquered all the gods, the lord of the Asuras ruled the kingdom. Prahlada was then treated by the gods with a facade of friendship, and they endured his tyranny with great respect. Over time, the Daityas, who were protected by the leaders of the gods, were defeated by force by the gods with the help of Vishnu.

While the Daitya Prahlada was established in his own kingdom, the great sage Gritsamada, capable of wandering anywhere, arrived to see him. Seeing him, the illustrious king of the Daityas bowed down. He seated the sage on his own throne and worshipped him with devotion. The Daitya, a devotee, stood massaging the sage's feet and spoke sweet words, filled with joy.

Prahlada said: My lineage, knowledge, learning, and charity are blessed today. Having seen your lotus feet, I am purified; there is no doubt about this. O Sage, you are the foremost among yogis; your excellent vision is not attained by those who commit sins, as it grants the path of worldly enjoyment and liberation. O King of Yogis, I was cursed by Brahma and, being deluded, became surrounded by Asuras and intent on wicked deeds. I became constantly addicted to women and intoxicants, devoid of knowledge, and intent on destroying the gods out of hatred. Now, O Great Sage, by the sight of your lotus feet, a glimmer of knowledge has arisen in me; there is no doubt. By the strength of my past merits, I have attained your great vision. Because of that, O Great Sage, my demonic nature has departed today. O Great Yogi, impart knowledge to me and rescue me from the ocean of worldly existence. I am a seeker of refuge, a Brahmin, and your disciple, O Teacher of the World.

Mudgala said: Being requested in this way, the great sage Gritsamada, filled with supreme compassion, spoke to him specifically.

Gritsamada said: Listen, Prahlada, I will tell you the knowledge that grants peace and happiness. Worship Ganesha, and you will be free from obstacles. The demon Madasura, a powerful Daitya, resides in your heart, obstructing your knowledge with his demonic nature. Therefore, O Lord, destroy him by worshipping the One-Tusked (Ekadanta). Then, true knowledge will become firmly established in your heart.

Hearing these words, Prahlada asked him again: Who is this sinful Madasura who resides in my heart, O Lord? Tell me now how to worship Ekadanta. How did he become the slayer of Madasura, the elephant-faced one? Tell me all this in detail, as I am a disciple requesting it, O Brahmin, so that my knowledge may become firm.

Mudgala said: Hearing the words of Prahlada, the sage Gritsamada spoke of the greatness of Madasura and his dependence on Ekadanta.

Gritsamada said: Listen, Prahlada, I will tell you everything you have asked. The character of Ekadanta grants all accomplishments. Once, a great and wicked demon, who was a seeker of faults, went to the hermitage of Bhrigu while the sage had gone to collect sacrificial wood. Seeing Bhrigu's wife alone, he asked, "O Suloma, tell me truly, are you the wife of Bhrigu?" A king had intended to give his daughter to the demon, but in the meantime, the daughter was sought by Bhrigu. The king abandoned the demon and gave his daughter to Bhrigu. "Therefore," the demon thought, "I will undoubtedly take Bhrigu's wife."

Hearing his cruel words, Suloma, the wife of Bhrigu, remained silent. Then the demon asked the Fire God: "O Fire, tell me, you are the witness accepted by all. Is this woman the wife of Bhrigu or someone else? Tell the truth, O radiant one." Hearing the demon's words, the powerful Fire God, fearing a curse, said nothing. Asked again, the Fire God, filled with fear, said: "A person who is asked by someone who does not know, and who is in a dilemma, should not speak; otherwise, he will certainly become a sinner. Therefore, I will tell you the truth, there is no doubt. Initially, the king had promised her to the demon. Therefore, according to the scriptures, you are the rightful claimant, O demon. If I tell you this, I will not incur any sin. However, O Lord of Asuras, do not be determined to take the woman. Consider this, O wise one. If a girl is promised to someone, she must be given to him; otherwise, the man becomes a sinner. The sin of breaking a promise is incurred by him, O King of Daityas. If a girl is given to another, she is not considered tainted."

Then the Fire God said to the cruel demon: "This is the wife of Bhrigu, O virtuous one; do not take another's wife." Hearing the words of the Fire God, the demon took her and flew away. As he carried her, she was pregnant, weeping, and fainted in the sky. The sage who was in her womb, possessing the brilliance of Bhrigu, became angry and fell from the womb himself to kill the demon. By the fire of his anger, the demon was reduced to ashes in an instant. The sage then took his son and returned home. The goddess Suloma, distressed and weeping, was overwhelmed with fear. At that time, the powerful Bhrigu returned from the forest. Told of the incident by his weeping wife, the yogi became angry, like Rudra himself. With eyes red with rage, he said to the Fire: "I curse you now, O ignorant one, you shall be the consumer of all things and intent on wicked deeds."

Having cursed the Fire, the sage Bhrigu consoled Suloma and praised his son. Because he fell from the womb in anger, he was named Chyavana, the best of beings.
