"He will be my son and the protector of his mother." The yogi Bhrigu, knowing the supreme truth, lived happily in his hermitage with his wife and son, remembering Ganapati.

Chapter 40 : The Glory of Agni

Mudgala said: Cursed by Bhrigu, Agni went to his own abode. He remained there, deeply distressed, having made himself invisible. Without Agni, a great cry of distress arose everywhere. The sages, along with the gods, sought refuge in Brahma.

The sages said: O Lord, Agni has been cursed by Bhrigu and has disappeared. We are now devoid of rituals and fire. We have come to you for refuge; protect us, O Grandfather. If you do not, there is no doubt that the entire moving and non-moving world will perish.

Hearing their words, Brahma went with them to Agni. He consoled him with the most wonderful words of the Vedas.

Brahma said: O Agni, listen, you are highly fortunate; it is stated in the Vedas that you are great. Your name is definitively established as the All-Devourer and the Pure One. Bhrigu is not at fault; what was destined has happened, there is no doubt. Do not worry in vain; be calm, O wise one. Therefore, although you are the All-Devourer, you are considered supreme and pure. You shall be free from the faults of consumption, there is no doubt.

Hearing these words, Agni became angry and prepared to cause universal destruction. In his rage, he cast flames of dissolution upon the moving and non-moving world. Being scorched by the flames, Brahma and the others fled. They meditated on Ganesha in their minds and remained fearless.

In the meantime, a Brahmin named Ekadanta appeared there. He approached Agni and spoke beneficial words.

The Brahmin said: O Agni, why are you so agitated? Why are you destroying the world? Filled with anger at the wrong time, have you forgotten Vinayaka? Observe where true independence lies; do not be arrogant, O Lord of the gods, for without Vinayaka, there is no independence.

Even after being told this, Agni burned that Brahmin with his own radiance. Smiling, that Lord of the Ganas, who was the best of sages, said: O Great Fire, you burn a mere straw with your own power, but you cannot burn the one I have empowered, nor the moving and non-moving world.

Having said this, the Brahmin threw a straw there. By his own power, Agni was unable to burn it. Then, the fire, his pride gone, bowed down to him. "Who is this named Vinayaka, known as the leader of all? Tell me, O ocean of compassion, so that I may worship him specifically. Who are you in the form of a Brahmin, deluding me, O Lord?"

The elephant-faced one in the form of a Brahmin then spoke to the calmed Agni, enlightening him with profound words, as he is the knower of all meanings.

The Brahmin said: Know that Vinayaka, who dwells in Svananda, is free from suffering. He is the leader of all, the perfect one, devoid of any other leader. The knowers of peace worship him through the path of peace. I will tell you that yoga; listen with a focused mind, O Lord. The Pranava (Om) is known to be joined with various distinctions. Know that, O radiant Fire, as Sampragnata Samadhi. That which is known as Brahman, joined with the distinctions of union and non-union, know that through the service of that yoga as Asampragnata. O carrier of oblations, the Yoga of Peace, which bestows peace in both Sampragnata and Asampragnata yoga, is called Ganesha-yoga. The Ganas are groups, and their master is Gajanana. Worship him with devotion, O highly radiant Fire.

Having said this, he gave him the supreme single-syllable mantra, which is Ganesha-related and bestows all accomplishments. He revealed his own form, abandoning the Brahmin guise, marked with the elephant face and other signs, the protector of devotees. Seeing him, Agni fell to the ground, filled with joy. Then the form disappeared, and he became sad and pure.

Leaving heaven, Agni went to the forest to practice yoga. He performed penance through Samadhi for the sake of attaining Ganesha. After a hundred divine years, the Lord of Lords, who is fond of his devotees and has a pleased soul, Gajanana, came to him. Seeing Agni engaged in penance, he said to his devotee: "Ask for a boon, O highly fortunate one; I am pleased with your penance."

Gritsamada said: Hearing the beautiful words of Ganesha, he was filled with joy. He bowed down to the ground with a heart full of emotion. Having worshipped him according to the rules, the carrier of oblations, with a pleased soul, praised Ganesha, the giver of yoga and peace.

Agni said: Salutations to the destroyer of obstacles, the benefactor of devotees. Salutations to the creator of obstacles for the non-devotees, O Vinayaka. Salutations to the one who rides a mouse, the elephant-faced, the wise one. Salutations to the one who is without beginning, middle, or end, and who is the form of the beginning, middle, and end. Salutations to the four-armed one, the giver of the four goals of life. Salutations to you, the single-tusked Heramba. Salutations to the pot-bellied god, the elephant-eared, the Dhundhi. Salutations to the form of yoga-peace, the giver of yoga-peace. Salutations to the giver of yoga to yogis, the lord of yogis. Salutations to the one who is the moving and non-moving, the bearer of the form of Pranava. Salutations to the one who is Siddhi and Buddhi, the giver of Siddhi and Buddhi. Salutations to the lord of Siddhi and Buddhi, the beloved of devotees. O infinite-faced Lord of gods, be pleased, O ocean of compassion. I am your servant, O leader of the Ganas; protect me especially. I am blessed among all gods to have seen your feet, O Vinayaka. I am fulfilled, a great yogi, and have become Brahman, there is no doubt. If you are pleased and wish to grant a boon, O Gajanana, then make me free from the curse, O best of the lords of gods. Give me firm devotion to you, by which delusion is destroyed. May I have the company of your devotees, O leader of the Ganas. Whenever I am in trouble, by remembering you, may I be free from trouble through your grace.

Having said this, he bowed to the Lord of the Ganas with devotion. The leader of the Ganas, out of his love for his devotees, spoke to him.

Ganesha said: This hymn composed by you, which increases my pleasure, will always be heard and recited by humans. Whatever desire you contemplate, I will grant it eternally. I am highly pleased with your devotion and this hymn. My devotion will always be firm in you, O sinless one. Trouble will be destroyed merely by remembering me. Bhrigu is my special devotee and holds the yoga of peace; I will never make his words false. Although you are the All-Devourer, you will always be pure. The twice-born and others will treat you as worthy of honor as before. I will remove the sorrow of being the All-Devourer; listen to my words. You will become an eater of nectar. The impurities of being an all-devourer will not affect you. I have made you an eater of nectar, there is no doubt.

Having said this, the Lord of the Ganas disappeared. Agni, filled with joy, returned to his own home. From that day, O demon, the fire became the All-Devourer, yet he was pure, an eater of nectar, a god, and the holder of yoga-peace. With the nature of Ganesha-devotion, he worshipped the leader of the Ganas. He became devoted to the company of Ganesha's devotees, O lord of demons.

Whoever, being the best of men, listens to or reads this glory of Agni, will attain even that which is extremely difficult to obtain.

Thus ends the fortieth chapter named "The Glory of Agni" in the second section of the Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 41 : The Description of Chyavana’s Penance

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gṛtsamada said: The son of Bhṛgu, a great yogi, bowed to his father Bhṛgu and went to the forest. He performed severe penance while standing in the water at Prayāga. Ten thousand years passed as the sage performed his austerities, and the entire universe became filled with his radiance. Once, while the sage was in the water, some fishermen arrived and cast their nets. By divine ordinance, the sage was caught in their net along with the fish. Seeing the great sage, the fishermen were terrified and went to King Nahuṣa to report the incident with great respect.

Fearing the consequences, the King, surrounded by his ministers and Brahmins, went to the great sage, bowed before him, and stood by his side. The fish, distressed by being out of the water and near death, were placed back into the water by the sage due to his compassionate nature. Seeing King Nahuṣa with folded hands, the sage said, "O wise one, release me from these fishermen now. Compensate them with the value of my body and satisfy them." Hearing this, the King spoke to the fishermen, offering a hundred gold coins to release the sage. The sage then asked the King, "Is the value of my body only a hundred gold coins?" The King then offered a hundred thousand, but the sage deemed it insufficient. Fearing the sage’s wrath, the King offered a village, then a city with its surrounding lands, then half his kingdom, and finally his entire kingdom. Still, the sage was not satisfied.

Terrified and unable to decide what to do, the King and his ministers feared that the enraged Chyavana would curse them. Seeing the King’s distress, Chyavana said, "O King, consult with the Brahmins and offer a fair price." Just then, the illustrious sage Bharadvāja arrived. Nahuṣa bowed to him, and Bharadvāja, having understood the situation, said, "O King, do not worry. The Creator divided the lineage of Brahmins and cows into two equal parts for the success of rituals. Cows provide the clarified butter (ghee) for sacrifices, and Brahmins provide the mantras. Give a cow to the fishermen and set the sage free." Having said this, Bharadvāja departed. The King followed this advice, and the sage, pleased, bestowed various blessings upon him.

After honoring the King, the sage went to a beautiful forest with pleasant shade, fruits, and water. There, he performed severe penance for a thousand years without food. Meanwhile, King Śaryāti arrived in that forest while hunting. Due to the sage’s long penance, an anthill covered with grass had grown over his body. The King instructed his men not to enter that area. In the meantime, the King’s daughter, Sukanyā, arrived there with her companions. While wandering, she saw the anthill and, noticing two glowing lights within it, was filled with curiosity. In her innocence, she poked the lights with a thorn, and the sage’s eyes were pierced. Blood flowed out, and terrified, she fled with her companions.

Suddenly, the King’s army began to suffer from an inability to pass urine and feces. Terrified, the King asked everyone what offense had been committed to anger the sage. Sukanyā, trembling with fear, confessed to her father that she had poked the glowing objects in the anthill with a thorn. The King went to the forest, prayed to the sage, and sought his forgiveness. The sage replied, "O King, your daughter committed this act out of ignorance. Give her to me in service, and all will be well." The King, fearing for his people, gave his beautiful daughter to the sage and returned to his city with his army.

Sukanyā served her husband, Chyavana, with great devotion, forgetting all worldly attachments. One day, the Ashvins, the physicians of the gods, arrived. The sage asked them to restore his youth and eyesight. The Ashvins agreed, provided he shared the sacrificial offerings with them. The sage agreed, and they all entered a pond. They emerged with divine beauty, resembling Kamadeva. Sukanyā was amazed to see three men of identical appearance, but she identified her husband. Pleased with her devotion, the Ashvins departed.

Later, when King Śaryāti visited the hermitage, Sukanyā embraced her father. Seeing the sage in his youthful, radiant form, the King asked for an explanation, and Sukanyā told him everything. The King bowed to the sage, and later, taking Chyavana and his daughter with him, he performed a great sacrifice. The gods, celestial nymphs, and sages from all directions gathered to witness the magnificent ceremony. All classes of people came to see the glorious sacrifice with great joy.

Thus ends the forty-first chapter of the second section of the Mudgala Purāṇa, describing the penance of Chyavana.

Chapter 42 : The Story of Bhrigu

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gṛtsamada said: When Chyavana performed the sacrifice, the Ashvini Kumāras were included as recipients of the sacrificial share. Seeing them there, Indra spoke these words.

Indra said: O Sage, the Ashvini Kumāras are known only as physicians; therefore, they are not qualified. O Lord, I declare that they do not deserve a share in the sacrifice.

The foremost of sages, Chyavana, ignored Indra's words and proceeded to invite the Ashvini Kumāras to the sacrifice, O Lord of the Asuras.

Then, filled with anger, Indra said to the Brahmin Chyavana: "If the Ashvini Kumāras come, I will strike them down with my thunderbolt (Vajra)."

Chyavana brought the Ashvini Kumāras to the sacrifice. Seeing them, Purandara (Indra) raised his thunderbolt.

Chyavana, through the power of his penance and influence, paralyzed Indra's arm holding the thunderbolt. The sage then created a being named 'Mada' (Arrogance/Intoxication).

Urged by Chyavana, Mada went to devour Indra. The sages and gods were astonished to see the power of the great sage.

They saw Mada at the sacrifice—a fierce-faced, heroic figure, licking the sky, dark as a mountain of collyrium.

Terrified, the gods, the hosts of sages, and the people abandoned the sacrifice and fled in all ten directions.

Mada approached Indra to devour him. Seeing him, Indra became terrified and sought refuge with Chyavana.

Indra said: O Sage, I accept that the Ashvini Kumāras are worthy of a share. O Lord of Brahmins, protect me from Mada, for he will surely devour me.

Then, the Bhrigu sage granted fearlessness to Indra, who had sought refuge, and restrained Mada through his own penance.

After completing the sacrifice, the sage Chyavana went to his father's house. Accompanied by his wife, he bowed to Bhrigu, who was well-versed in Yoga.

Honored by his father and being accomplished in penance, Chyavana lived there for some time, eager to serve him.

Once, Bhrigu, the knower of the supreme truth, spoke to his son out of compassion for the sake of his spiritual realization.

Bhrigu said: Listen, my son, I will tell you what sorrowful deeds you have committed. You created Mada through your own penance, which became a source of terror for Devendra.

O ascetic, you are performing deeds fueled by the arrogance of your penance. You are filled with pride, my son; abandon it through the practice of Yoga.

Listen first to my own story, which I will tell you, regarding what I did when I was filled with such pride.

I was constantly engaged in great penance, and among all the sages, I became the greatest through my austerities.

Once, all the sages gathered on the banks of the Gautami river when Jupiter was in Leo, and a great debate arose, my son.

I believed the Grandfather (Brahma) to be the supreme, the one with attributes, the great soul, the creator of all.

Some said Shiva was supreme, others said Vishnu, and some said Brahma.

My son, I was sent by all of them to test the three, as they sought certainty.

I believed Brahma was the greatest, so I went to my father to test him, O great sage.

I sat on a high seat without bowing to him, the Lord of the Universe. Seeing my insolence, the Grandfather became angry.

The best of gods said to me: "Why have you come filled with arrogance, you fool? Go back to your home now."

I replied: "I am your son, born from your body; why do you call me a fool?"

My father was extremely agitated and prepared to curse me. I bowed to him like a staff on the ground with devotion.

I said to him: "O great one, forgive my grave offense. I was sent by the great sages to test you, O Grandfather."

"Forgive this act; you are supreme in every way." After praising him with various words, I went to Kailasa.

Seeing me, a brother ascetic, Shiva, the giver of happiness to his devotees, suddenly came to embrace me.

To test him, I spoke very harsh words: "Do not touch me, O lord of ghosts, you who are devoid of Varna and Ashrama."

"You are always impure, marked with human skulls and such. Though named Shiva, you are actually Ashiva (inauspicious). I know your deeds."

Hearing my words, Sadashiva became angry, opened his third eye, and prepared to burn me.

Then, my son, I bowed like a staff on the ground and praised him: "I did this to test you; please forgive me."

Knowing I was a Brahmin, Shankara suppressed his anger and remained there on Kailasa. Then I went to Vaikuntha.

I saw Vishnu sitting with Lakshmi and was delighted. Even he immediately bowed at my feet, my son.

I rebuked him severely: "Do not touch my feet, you fool. You have taken the form of a woman, and thus you are considered impure."

"You have nurtured my wife, you wicked one; you have done a despicable thing." With such various words, I insulted him greatly.

Then Vishnu himself stood with folded hands, my son. In my anger, I kicked him in the chest with my foot.

Even then, he did not get angry; instead, he held my foot and massaged it, saying it must be tired from the impact.

At that very moment, out of love for Brahmins, he bore the mark of Bhrigu's footprint on his chest, my son.

I reached a conclusion and went to the assembly of Brahmins, telling them the whole story, and declared Narayana to be the greatest.

Where there is forgiveness, O Brahmins, everything is established. Brahma is attained gradually by a mind endowed with forgiveness.

I then became a Vaishnava, devoted to Vishnu. Once, at Daksha's sacrifice, Vishnu was the chief.

Ignoring Shiva and seeing Hari as the chief in the sacrificial rites, I became the leader and the promoter of the sacrifice, my son.

Parvati was burnt there, and Shiva, filled with anger, came surrounded by his Ganas upon learning of Sati's death.

He defeated Vishnu, killed Daksha, and destroyed the sacrifice. My chin was struck down there by Virabhadra.

Consoled by Brahma, Shiva revived Daksha and himself completed the sacrifice with all the shares.

My chin was replaced with that of a goat by that great soul. I was cursed by Nandi to become a follower of Varuna.

Then, my son, I was astonished in my mind, remembering Shankara. Shiva is Time itself, and this world is subject to Time.

This Shiva is called the Lord; there is nothing beyond him. Reflecting on this, I kept Shankara in my heart and became devoted to his worship.

I became a Shaiva, constantly smeared with ashes, and worshipped Shankara through various Pashupata paths.

Once, a sacrifice was started in a place called Mahabala by the Shaivas, attended by Brahma and the sages.

In that great sacrifice, they forgot Ganesha. At dawn, they abandoned the eldest (Ganesha) and performed the sacrifice.

Seeing Gayatri, who had attained the status of the chief, Savitri became angry and cursed all the gods.

"You will surely become water-forms. You began this sacrifice after abandoning the eldest, O gods."

Then the gods, led by Shiva, became water-forms. By performing penance to Ganesha, the wives of the gods released them.

Through his grace, they regained their divine forms. There is no doubt that Ganesha existed in the form of a part in the water.

Then I realized that Ganesha is the Lord of Obstacles, the sustainer of all existence, and by nature, unrestrained.

He himself was the great sacrifice, and he destroyed it and turned the gods into water, my son.

Then, satisfied by their devotion, he came to the sacrifice and restored the gods to their divine forms and completed the sacrifice.

He is present everywhere, and that is why he did such things. Realizing this, I became devoted to the worship of Ganesha.

Then, once, by fortune, I met Vyasa, and I asked the great sage:

"Vyasa, you are undoubtedly the creator of the branches of the Vedas, the Puranas, and the Mahabharata, O great-minded one."

"What is the essence of the Vedas and Puranas that is accepted by all? Tell me, O ocean of compassion, so that a man may attain peace."

That great soul spoke of the excellent path of Ganesha. He made me listen to the Purana of the Lord of Ganas, my son.

Then, by practicing the path of Yoga and worshipping the Lord of Ganas, I abandoned all arrogance and attained peace, there is no doubt.

I was ruined by arrogance and tried to test the Lord. I am all-knowing, but listen, my son, to the power of the test.

Therefore, my son, worship the Lord of Obstacles by my command. You will attain peace everywhere and become a Ganapatya (devotee of Ganesha).

Mada (arrogance) is present everywhere as a pervasive force. The one you created will surely torment you.

What sorrowful deeds have you done, my son, fueled by the arrogance of penance? Now, worship the Lord of Ganas with devotion through the practice of Yoga.

Having said this, Bhrigu, who was well-versed in all meanings, became silent. He was a bestower of peace, a direct devotee of Ganesha, and of great fame.

Whoever hears or reads this story of Bhrigu will attain all their desires; there is no doubt about this, O Prahlada.

Thus ends the forty-second chapter named "The Story of Bhrigu" in the second section of the Shri Mudgala Mahapurana.

Chapter 43 : The Glory of Chyavana

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gritsamada said: Hearing the words of Bhrigu, Chyavana felt ashamed. Bowing to him with great devotion, he asked with folded hands.

Chyavana said: Please forgive my offense for the arrogance I displayed. The Ashvini Kumaras were deprived of their share, but now they are entitled to it. Now, please tell me the knowledge of the Lord of the Ganas that brings peace, by which I may abandon my arrogance and become tranquil.

Gritsamada said: Hearing his words, Bhrigu spoke to him about the path to perfection. Listen, I shall tell you about the complete Yoga known as Ganesha-Yoga.

Bhrigu said: Ganesha is known as the form of Brahman, the embodiment of complete peace. Through the Yoga of unity and duality, Ganesha is attained by men. All this is illusory, consisting of differences and non-differences, O son. Without realizing the Lord of the Ganas, the mind cannot find peace. When the five fluctuations of the mind are destroyed, there is no doubt that one attains the peace of Yoga. Therefore, worship the King of the Ganas through the service of Yoga, and by the method I have described, you will become peaceful.

Having said this, Bhrigu became silent and filled with peace. Chyavana bowed to him and departed with great reverence. Going to the forest, he performed penance that grants Yoga, and gradually, he forcibly purified the states of his mind. Through the destruction of worldly desires, he practiced tranquility and self-control. Then, through contemplation of Brahman, he attained everything easily. Having attained the Yoga of peace and established himself in that state, he worshipped Ganesha daily, adopting the nature of a devotee of Ganesha.

Ganesha, the elephant-faced, four-armed, glorious, and large-bellied Lord, came to him riding on a mouse. Seeing him, the sage Chyavana fell to the ground like a staff. After worshipping him according to the rites, he began to praise him. Praised by the Heramba Upanishad, the Lord of the Ganas became pleased and granted him an excellent boon.

Ganesha said: O best of Brahmins, whatever you desire shall be fulfilled. You shall always remain in the state of Yoga and peace. Your devotion to my feet will become firm. Whenever you remember me, O Brahmin, I will appear before you to grant you my vision.

Having said this, the Lord of the Ganas disappeared. Chyavana installed a four-armed idol of Ganesha there. Living in his hermitage, he became a devotee of the Lord from that day on. By seeing and remembering this, one immediately attains both worldly enjoyment and liberation.

This is the holy account of the great-souled Chyavana that I have told you, which destroys all sins and grants various fruits of perfection.

Thus ends the forty-third chapter titled The Glory of Chyavana, in the Ekadanta-charita of the Second Section of the glorious Mudgala Mahapurana.

Chapter 44 : The Coronation of Madasura

Prahlada said: I have heard the greatness of Chyavana, and my mind is now at peace. O great sage, tell me about the deeds of Mada; what did he do?

Mudgala said: When asked in this way, the powerful and great yogi Gritsamada, the best among the followers of Ganesha, spoke; listen to it with reverence.

Gritsamada said: Listen, Prahlada, to the greatness of Mada in sequential order. After being created by Chyavana, Mada went to the netherworld (Patala). Having deliberated, the powerful demon went to the great sage Shukra. Mada stood at a distance, bowing down with folded hands. Seeing Mada filled with humility, the descendant of Bhrigu, Kavya (Shukra), honored him and asked, "Who are you and where have you come from? Tell me."

Then, feeling joyful, he spoke to the best of sages: "O sage, know me to be a descendant of Bhrigu, born from Chyavana. I am your brother's descendant and have come to you for refuge. O lord of yogis, make me your disciple, so that I may be honored on this earth. I desire to rule the great kingdom of the universe, O Bhrigu. I am Mada, and by your grace, I shall conquer through the form of intoxication."

Hearing his words, the best of sages accepted Mada as his disciple, though with a mind not overly pleased. The leader of the Bhrigus, who was well-versed in all meanings, gave him the Shakti mantra along with the rules of the single-syllable incantation. After bowing to him, Mada went to the forest to perform penance. The demon remained without food, devoted to the meditation of Shakti.

Over a long period, an anthill grew over his body, covered with vines and trees, yet he remained in penance. He was reduced to mere bones, chanting the mantra with devotion. The ascetic performed his meditation with a steady mind and all its components. Five thousand divine years passed while he performed penance, and the world of the demon kings became distressed.

Then, the Shakti, influenced by his devotion, went to his hermitage. She was the great goddess, mounted on a lion and surrounded by other Shaktis. She appeared in human form, and those who were under her command moved about, filled with devotion and great strength, acting as her manifestations. The primordial Maya, that Shakti, awakened Mada. The demon Mada, however, did not realize it was his devotee.

Then, the great Mada, having his body cleansed by her water, became alert and, seeing the Shakti, stood up. Bowing to her with great devotion, he worshipped her. Joining his palms, he praised the Goddess according to his intellect.

Mada said: I bow to you, O Great Maya, endowed with all power. This entire world, endowed with energy, functions based on you. The great sages do not know you, who are beginningless and immeasurable. I bow to the Supreme Goddess, whose form is known through Vedanta. I bow to you, the Goddess who creates various distinctions yet is devoid of them, the one who is both the distinction and the non-distinction, the mistress of all. I bow to you, Parvati, the daughter of Daksha, the daughter of the Himalayas, Lakshmi the daughter of the ocean, and Savitri. I bow to you, who are Svaha, Svadha, the embodiment of Vashatkara, the auspicious one, the form of intellect, the embodiment of divinity, and the form of knowledge. I bow to you, the bestower of success in all tasks, the embodiment of success, the ascetic, the Yogini, the giver of Yoga, and the mistress of Yoga. The sages who speak of the Vedas, the gods, Skanda, and Shesha are unable to praise you; how can I praise the Supreme One? My parents, my knowledge, my penance, and my learning are blessed, for I have seen your lotus feet, which liberate one from all sorrows.

Having said this, Madasura stood silent, controlled by his devotion. The Great Shakti, moved by his devotional state, spoke to him.

Shakti said: This hymn composed by you, which increases my pleasure, will be the granter of all desired fruits, O fortunate one. It will be a means to achieve Dharma, wealth, desire, and liberation. O great demon, it will be pleasant to all who read or hear it. Ask for boons; I am pleased by your hymn and your severe penance, O king of demons. Ask for those things that are difficult to obtain and are in your mind.

Gritsamada said: Hearing the words of the Goddess, Madasura was delighted. Bowing to her, he asked for boons that are very difficult to obtain.

Madasura said: If you are pleased, O Goddess, and if you are to grant me supreme boons, then ensure that I have no death anywhere. Grant me the entire kingdom of the three worlds, O Mother of the Universe. May whatever I desire always be fulfilled, O Sadashiva. You are the form of Prakriti, and everything originates from you. O Mother, make everything fivefold and under my control.

Then, the Mother of the Universe, with a surprised mind, said to him: "O great demon, since you were born from Him (Chyavana), there is no death for you. Whatever is divided into five parts will be under your control. Whatever you desire will always be successful. You will have a stable kingdom of the universe, and there will be no one in the entire world who can defeat you in battle, O king of demons."

Having said this, the Goddess, the Mother of the Universe, disappeared, O lord of demons. Mada was extremely happy and returned to his home. He married the daughter of Pramadasura, the beautiful and graceful Salasa, according to the rites, and was highly honored by the demons.

He then built a city that provided all kinds of luxuries, which shone brightly. Great demons from all directions came there to reside. His four ministers became world-renowned: Bana, Tripura, Taraka, and Shankha. The demons brought the great sage Shukra, who knew the highest Yoga, and along with the Brahmins, he performed the coronation of Madasura as king. Madasura became the respected lord of the demons, Rakshasas, and Asuras, and the demon kings were delighted.

Three sons were born to Salasa from Madasura: Vilasi, Lolupa, and Dhanapriya. Thus, he ruled as the lord of the demons. Who could stand against him, filled with such pride and strength?

Thus ends the forty-fourth chapter of the second section of the glorious Mudgala Purana, titled "The Coronation of Madasura."

Chapter 45 : The Conquest of Heaven by Madasura

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gritsamada said: Then, at some point, Madasura was incited by the lords of the Daityas. Filled with arrogance, he became determined to conquer the three worlds. Approaching Shukracharya and bowing to him with great reverence, he consulted the chief sage and made up his mind to achieve victory. Then, the Daitya lords and demons arrived from various directions; armed and hurried, the Rakshasas went to Madasura. Accompanied by countless divisions of his four-fold army, Madasura set out for the conquest of the quarters, praised by bards.

Some of the terrifying Daityas who emerged before him shone like Mount Meru and Mandara, appearing as harbingers of death. Equipped with various weapons and armor, they rode diverse vehicles, causing the earth to tremble. Some held stones, others carried trees, and some were as mighty as mountains, possessing immense strength and valor. Their various terrifying forms shone all around the army. At that time, everyone in the crowded universe became filled with doubt.

The extremely cruel Daityas, urged on by Madasura, were intent on conquering the earth. Some kings fought until they died, while others sought refuge; some, struck by fear, took shelter in the mountains. The people on earth believed that the final dissolution had arrived. Having conquered the entire globe, the Daityas were filled with joy. After conquering the earth with its seven islands, the fierce Daityas turned their attention to conquering the gods.

Moving with the speed of the wind, they reached heaven in half a moment and destroyed the forests and divine gardens, O king of Daityas. Informed by the gods, the King of the Gods (Indra), filled with anger, prepared for battle with his armed deities. The Lord of the Gods approached Brihaspati and bowed to him. The Lord of the Gods, honored by the preceptor, was filled with devotion. Then, the radiant Brihaspati said to Indra: "Listen, O King of the Gods, to my words which are in accordance with time and duty. Because of the boon granted by the Goddess, Madasura is invincible. Therefore, O Lords of the Gods, abandon everything and flee."

Having been told this by Jiva (Brihaspati), the gods, including Brihaspati, abandoned heaven and fled out of fear. Then, Madasura, knowing all the movements of the gods, arrived at Amaravati, accompanied by the Daityas and feeling very happy. Seated on Indra's throne, Madasura shone, being served by Apsaras and Gandharvas intent on singing. Served by the immortals with various divine enjoyments, he shone, filled with the pride of his strength and surrounded by various Daityas.

He installed Daitya lords in the positions of the gods; Tarakasura and other mighty ones settled there and enjoyed themselves in comfort. Then, after consulting with them, he went to the great abode of Brahma. The Creator, abandoning his seat, fled to Kailasa out of fear of him. In Satyaloka, all the gods wandered here and there, while the wicked ones, filled with pride and enjoying various pleasures, shone. Carried by divine chariots that moved at the speed of thought and fulfilled all desires, the great Daityas considered their mission accomplished.

Madasura, the king there, seated on Brahma's throne, rejoiced, surrounded by the lords of the Daityas who struck fear into the times. Then, surrounded by them, the Daitya went to Vaikuntha with great eagerness. Knowing that the cruel one had arrived, the Lord of Rama (Vishnu) fled. He also went to Kailasa, surrounded by his attendants, and the wicked Madasura took up residence in Vaikuntha. Seated on the throne of Narayana and surrounded by powerful Daitya kings, he shone brilliantly, enjoying various pleasures.

The incomparable comfort that Vishnu had created in Vaikuntha was now enjoyed by him, accompanied by the wicked Daityas. The Daitya lords enjoyed the wonderful chariots, the divine gardens, and the wish-fulfilling trees. Then, the heroic and powerful Madasura, along with them, went to Kailasa, the abode of Shankara, with the intention of battle. Shiva, too, accompanied by his attendants and surrounded by gods like Vishnu, went to the realm of the Goddess, filled with fear due to the arrogance of the Daitya.

The Lord of the Daityas, with his great demons, took up residence in Kailasa. He enjoyed immense and unparalleled pleasure with them. The virtuous Kailasa, created by Shiva and filled with wonders, was forcibly enjoyed by the joyful Daitya. Deluded by the state of enjoyment, Madasura considered himself and all the wicked Daityas, led by Tripurasura, to be blessed.

All the gods went to the realm of the Goddess, filled with fear. The Goddess, surrounded by her attendants, comforted them and remained there. The Primal Maya, the best of those who speak, the Mother of the world, the cause of creation, sustenance, and dissolution, said to Shiva and the others:

The Adi Shakti said: "Do not fear, O Lords of the Gods; stay here in my presence. As long as you stay here, this Daitya will never come. Because of the boon granted by me, he has become filled with pride; Madasura, the fortunate one, will fulfill my words. If the Daitya comes, he will worship me; the Lord of the Daityas will not insult me. If, due to his pride, he ever harasses those who dwell in my realm, I will kill him immediately, there is no doubt."

Having been reassured by the Goddess, the gods stayed there, having abandoned their fear of the Daityas, O great Asura.

Thus ends the forty-fifth chapter of the second section of the Mudgala Purana, titled "The Conquest of Heaven by Madasura."

Chapter 46 : The Description of Tarakasura’s Conciliation

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gritsamada said: Madasura resided in Kailasa, accompanied by the demons, enjoying pleasures that were extremely difficult to attain for a long time. After some time had passed, Tripura and others said to him, as they were filled with arrogance and intent on destroying the gods.

Tripura and others said: Listen, O King, O fortunate Madasura, to these beneficial words. All the gods, led by Shambhu, have gone to the realm of the Goddess (Shakti-loka) and are shining there. There, the best of the gods are being honored by the Goddess, and being free from fear, they are enjoying various pleasures. All the gods are our enemies, and they are always considered demons' foes. They are undoubtedly capable of slaughtering the demons. In the past, Hiranyaksha and others were killed by these gods, who were always looking for our weaknesses. This is famous everywhere, and all people know of the enmity between the gods and us, and how they are engaged in mutual slaughter. Our power is entirely directed toward this enmity, yet, O King of demons, the Goddess remains devoted to the welfare of the gods. Therefore, O Lord, we shall undoubtedly go to the realm of the Goddess and kill all the gods who have taken refuge in this enmity.

Hearing the words of the demons, the valiant Madasura spoke to those great demons with humility.

Madasura said: Listen, Tripura and others, to my beneficial words. All my prosperity is born from the grace of the Goddess. She is my chosen deity, and I worship her specifically. Therefore, her welfare must always be considered and upheld by all of you. There is no doubt that all the gods have taken refuge in her, and the Goddess is bound by my devotion and respect. Because of her partiality toward the gods, she has not come with them, but she is the Great Maya (Mahamaya) who seeks to destroy us, O demons. Therefore, those who are devoted to her are always to be respected by me; do not harbor enmity, O demons.

Hearing the words of Madasura, the demon lords spoke to him again, filled with anger and arrogance, intent on the destruction of the gods.

The Demon Lords said: Madasura, listen, O Lord, to the story of the past. Hiranyakashipu was known to be a great one who obtained boons from Brahma. There were many other demon lords who were enemies of the gods; they abandoned the refuge of the gods and followed their own dharma. Even the two sons of Kashyapa abandoned the refuge of the gods and were intent on the destruction of the gods in the past. Therefore, you too must bring about the destruction of the gods. Even in the Vedas, the demons and gods are called opponents. We shall go to the realm of the Goddess with devotion, O Lord, and we shall surely destroy the gods in the presence of the Goddess. If the Great Maya, the Goddess, is on our side, then we shall worship her as our family deity with respect. Otherwise, we shall fight her, for there is no doubt that knowing she has taken refuge in the enemy's side, there is no fault in doing so.

Hearing the words of the demon lords, the foolish Madasura agreed, believing it to be the absolute truth. At that very moment, Madasura, along with the demons, went to that realm and sent a messenger to the presence of the Goddess. He said to Taraka, "Go, O mighty one, to the Goddess. Speak quickly with conciliation, O great demon."

Having bowed to him, he went quickly to the presence of the Goddess with respect. Taraka was honored by those gods, though he was extremely fierce. Bowing to the Great Maya, the Goddess, with folded hands, the wicked Taraka spoke words of conciliation.

Tarakasura said: Listen, O Goddess, O Great Maya, Madasura has arrived. O Mother, I have been sent by him now for your audience. Listen to what he has said, O giver of honor, O knower of the hearts of the devotees, O Mother of the world. You are undoubtedly our family deity. We demons are all considered your servants. Where demons shine, the gods lose their luster; there is no doubt about this, as they are considered enemies by nature. Therefore, abandon the gods and stay here, worshipped by the demons. The gods are our enemies and are considered your enemies as well. As long as we are bowing to you, we shall slaughter the gods; there is no doubt, so Mother, abandon them quickly. Take one side and stay, O Mother of the world, whether it be the demons' or the gods', for you are always worshipped by others. I have told you the words of Madasura, O Goddess; please protect your greatest devotee with supreme respect.

Gritsamada said: The cruel words of Madasura were expressed by Taraka. Hearing that, the angry Goddess spoke to that great demon.

The Goddess said: My name, "Mother of the World," is proclaimed in the Vedas. By what path should I take one side, O King of demons? The gods and the demons are both to be honored by me; there is no doubt. O great demon, both gods and demons must perform their own dharma. The demons should happily rule in the netherworld, and the gods should always perform their duties according to their nature in the heavens. If they act otherwise, I will surely destroy them in my anger. Therefore, the demons must go to the netherworld by my command.

Hearing the words of the Goddess, that great demon, filled with anger, spoke to the Great Goddess, having abandoned fear due to his arrogance.

Taraka said: Do not be proud, O Goddess, for you have taken refuge in the enemy's side. Through our penance, we have become superior; there is no doubt. You create the worlds through action, O Mother of the world. Everything is subject to action; what will you do about that? By his actions, the King of demons, the valiant Madasura, will become the lord of the universe and will kill all the gods. Prepare for battle, O Goddess, along with the gods, O you who are the essence of the world. I go to Madasura with the great demons.

Having said this to the Great Goddess, Taraka, filled with anger, went from the realm of the Goddess to the presence of the demon Madasura.

Thus ends the forty-sixth chapter of the second part of the Mudgala Purana, titled "The Description of Tarakasura’s Conciliation."

Chapter 47 : The Meeting of Indra and Taraka

Gritamada said: Seeing Taraka arrive, the wicked Madasura became joyful, and along with the demons, he became very excited. Taraka bowed to Madasura and narrated the entire situation regarding the side of the gods and their reliance on their family deity. Hearing this, the valorous and intoxicated demon Madasura became filled with rage and prepared to kill the gods. Accompanied by all the armed demon lords, Madasura set out and appeared like Mahakala himself.

The great demons struck the highly renowned attendants of the Goddess stationed at the outskirts of the city with extremely fierce weapons. Those whose limbs were severed fled and returned to their own city, where they narrated the account of the demons' arrival. Hearing this, the Great Goddess, accompanied by all the powerful gods led by Shankara, went to the battlefield. She appeared mounted on a lion, four-armed, holding a sword and a shield, carrying a bow and arrows, filled with rage, and endowed with various powers.

From Her left side emerged Shri, from the right side Savitri, and from Her heart appeared Samjna; the armies of these goddesses appeared distinct. The gods, led by Vishnu and Shankara, stood prepared on their vehicles, and together with their respective armies, they shone like the time of destruction. Seeing this great and terrifying army of the gods, combined with the primordial powers, the demon lords were struck by the barrage of weapons suddenly and forcefully. The massive army of the Goddess became filled with rage.

The gods, sent by the Goddess, fought on the battlefield. The powers, led by Kalika, were extremely enraged in the great war. As the armies met suddenly, both sides sought victory, determined to kill the other. The dust raised by the armies obscured knowledge on all sides; there was no light from the sun to distinguish friend from foe. The directions were filled with the sounds of neighing horses, roaring, trumpeting elephants, the clatter of chariot wheels, and the loud noise of musical instruments.

O Lord of demons, the warriors on the battlefield became as if deaf due to the sounds of conches, lion-roars, and various other noises. They struck each other from all sides with numerous weapons and missiles, and in their war-madness, they used trees and mountain fragments against their own and their enemies. Some died, some were wounded, and others stood holding weapons, killing enemy heroes with their own strength. When weapons were broken, they engaged in wrestling; others, devoid of weapons, fought filled with rage.

Headless trunks fought there, holding weapons, killing both their own and the enemy, devoid of any recognition. They struck each other with arrows, swords, maces, iron clubs, axes, bhindipalas (javelins), and nooses. Those on chariots fought those on elephants, those on elephants fought foot soldiers, and those on horses fought others in great confusion. A tumultuous battle ensued between the demons and the gods, so terrifying and destructive that it cannot be described.

The earth was flooded with abundant blood, and the dust was washed away, creating a light that allowed for the recognition of friend and foe. Some gods, having awakened, killed the demons, and the demons, filled with great rage, did the same to the gods. The Goddesses, enraged, struck the demons and pierced the demon lords with various weapons. Other demons, filled with rage and great strength, cut and tore through the circle of gods.

The gods and demons stood like Mount Meru and the Shita mountains, not moving an inch, intent on mutual destruction. Great rivers of blood flowed everywhere, born from the bodies of thousands of gods and demons. The earth was covered in a thick mire of fat and flesh, and hundreds of gods and demons lost their lives in the war. Flesh lay in the form of mud here and there on the battlefield, and there was no count of the dead who blocked the paths.

People saw a flood created by the flow of blood, which, O Lord of demons, increased the great delusion like a torrent of water. The field was made impassable by dead elephants, broken chariots, horses, various animals, camels, and birds. Some lay dying, some were dead and dismembered, yet even then, filled with rage, they made efforts with devotion. Thus, for ten days and nights, a great war took place between the gods and demons.

Then, the gods turned to flight in the great battle, fleeing in all ten directions, terrified, O Lord of demons. Seeing this, the wielder of the thunderbolt (Indra), surrounded by the gods, waged a fierce battle that brought about the destruction of the demons. With the strike of his thunderbolt, the Lord of the gods quickly crushed the demons, as did Yama and others with their various weapons. Some demon hosts died, some were rendered unconscious, and others were left dying by the wielder of the thunderbolt.

With the strike of weapons and missiles, the gods led by Agni, Chandra, and Yama killed the demons, filled with rage. Then, the entire army of the demons, broken and filled with fear, fled in all directions, finding no savior. Then the gods and the hosts of other deities became joyful and shouted, "Victory to the Great Goddess, the Great Illusion, victory forever!"

Then, Taraka, filled with rage and surrounded by demon lords, his eyes glowing with anger, came to fight with Shakra (Indra). Arriving at the battlefield, he spoke to Purandara (Indra), who was deluded and filled with pride.

Taraka said: Why do you fight, O Lord of gods, with these demon lords who are highly honored? You are no match for me, O Lord of gods, even with a single arrow. If you were endowed with strength, why did you abandon Amaravati? Tell me, you will meet your death in vain.

Hearing his words, the Lord of gods, with eyes reddened by rage, as if wanting to devour the demons, said to him:

Indra said: Why do you boast, Taraka? Behold my great prowess. I will strike your great demon army while you watch. Afterward, I will kill you, your pride, your army, and your vehicles, and then, having bowed to the Goddess, I will go to Amaravati. Previously, devoid of the Goddess's power, what prowess could I show? Now, endowed with Her power, I will wage war against you. Why speak much, O Lord of demons? Show your own prowess; otherwise, go to Patala. Why do you boast in vain?

Thus ends the forty-seventh chapter, titled "The Meeting of Indra and Taraka," in the second part of the Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 48 : The Defeat of Indra

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gritsamada said: Having spoken thus, the Lord of the Devas, filled with rage, showered a rain of arrows upon the demon king Taraka on the battlefield. From a single mantra-infused arrow, countless others emerged, causing the assembled demons to be afflicted on all sides. Then the demon king and his prominent commanders, who were standing there, unleashed extremely fierce showers of arrows in all directions. By those arrows, many heroes of the divine army were struck; their limbs were severed, and they fell to the ground, bleeding profusely. The Devas, overwhelmed by fear, fled here and there, finding no protector, just as living beings find none during the time of universal dissolution.

Seeing their plight, the divine protectors like Agni and others showered arrows that were as firm and deadly as Time and Death itself. Struck by these, the demon forces were decimated everywhere, and the demons lay either dead or dying in the fray. Then, the terrifying demon Taraka, filled with extreme rage, drew a sturdy and formidable arrow and released it. Drawing his bow to his ear, the glowing arrow sped forth and struck the heart of Indra, the Lord of the Devas. Struck by it, the leader of the Devas fainted, but after a moment, he regained consciousness and took up his thunderbolt. When struck by the thunderbolt, the mighty demon Taraka fell to the ground as if he had lost his life.

In this manner, Varuna, Agni, Vayu, Dharma, and Kubera struck down many demons, leaving them either unconscious or dead. Cries of distress arose everywhere in the demon army, and they fled in all directions, terrified by the leaders of the Devas. Then, Shukracharya used his knowledge to revive Taraka and the other great demons, preparing them for battle once more. Taraka, filled with rage, placed an arrow empowered with the fire mantra upon his bow and released the scorching weapon. As the arrow fell upon the divine army, a powerful being emerged from it and began to devour the Devas.

Towering like Mount Meru or Mandara, this mighty being moved through the divine ranks, burning them like the fire of universal dissolution. Terrifying flames erupted everywhere, consuming the Devas along with their chariots on the battlefield. The Devas cried out in terror and fled, but the being continued to devour the immortals. Then, Mahendra, filled with rage, took the great Varuna weapon upon his bow and released it in the battle. From it, a great torrent of water emerged, and clouds roared as they poured down an incomparable rain. By the flood of water, the great fire was suddenly extinguished, and the being created by the weapon also fell on the battlefield.

Then the hosts of Devas regained their composure, while the demons were afflicted by the rain and wind. Some great demons were scorched by lightning flames, while others were swept away along with their chariots by the flood. The powerful army of the demons was afflicted by the Varuna weapon, and the demons met a terrifying death amidst cries of distress. Then, the demon Taraka, his eyes reddened with rage, invoked the terrifying Vayu weapon to counter it. The great demon drew his bow and released it at the divine army, and by it, the Varuna weapon was swallowed and calmed.

The divine army was whirled about by the powerful wind, spinning on the battlefield like dry leaves. Then the terrifying demon Taraka approached and struck the King of the Devas fiercely with his sword. Struck by the sword, Indra fell, deeply unconscious, and the demon king Taraka took him and returned to his own forces. He placed the Lord of the Devas in the presence of Madasura, and the Deva, unconscious from the intoxication, was bound on all sides with ropes.

Then, all the divine forces, crying out in distress, fled in the ten directions, leaderless and filled with fear. Taraka then suddenly approached Yama, struck him in the heart with a mace, and the Lord of the Departed fell into a deep swoon. The great demon, overjoyed, took him to his own forces. In this way, all the deities led by Agni were captured by the demon leaders. The leaderless divine army fled in terror, decimated and broken by the weapons of the mighty demons. Having thus conquered, the demon kings rejoiced with loud roars, while all the Devas, agitated by fear, sought refuge in Shankara.

Thus ends the forty-eighth chapter of the second section of the glorious Mudgala Mahapurana, titled "The Defeat of Indra."

Chapter 49 : The Defeat of Shiva and Other Gods

Gṛtsamada said: Accompanied by Vishnu and surrounded by all the gods and fierce divine hosts, Shiva went to the battlefield. Seeing Shiva arrive, Tāraka, filled with rage, spoke to Mahādeva with a proud heart. Tāraka said: "Why have you come to the battlefield with such arrogance, Sadāshiva? Tell me, what can a rabbit do in a fight against a lion? I am the servant of Madāsura, and I will surely kill you, the destroyer, for I am the great destroyer myself." Hearing these words, Shiva struck Tāraka with his trident. The demon died instantly and fell like a broken tree.

Then, the Lord of gods, Keshava (Vishnu), filled with anger, killed the demon leaders everywhere with his discus. Terrified, the demons fled in all ten directions, crying out in fear to save their lives. The gods captured the demons, including Tāraka's chief followers, but due to the fear of Shukra, the prominent ones were left behind and hid in caves. Seeing the great prowess of the gods, Bāṇa, Tripura, and Śaṅkha arrived on the battlefield with their armies. Making a great clamor with the sound of conch shells, they rained down weapons like clouds in the rainy season.

Then, a fierce and wondrous battle ensued between the gods and the demons, causing destruction to both sides. Bāṇa attacked the Sun, Śaṅkha attacked the powerful Vishnu, and Tripura, the destroyer of enemy armies, swiftly attacked Rudra. Hundreds of other demons engaged the gods; the battle was so vast and wondrous that it is impossible to describe. Filled with rage, the demons struck the gods with various weapons, trees, and mountains. Recognizing the superior strength of the demons, the powerful gods fought with great effort, weapons in hand.

Thus, a fierce and great battle lasted for three days. The divine army was eventually broken and fled in terror. Shiva, filled with great rage, struck the powerful Tripura with his mace. Tripura, also being heroic, struck Shiva with his mace, leading to a terrifying mace-fight. Then, struck by the trident, the demon Tripura fell, suffering greatly from unconsciousness caused by Shiva. Bāṇa was struck down to the earth by the Sun's radiance, and the wicked Śaṅkha was crushed by Vishnu's discus. The gods captured the demon leaders and, fearing a future uprising, bound them with ropes and threw them into a cavern.

The demons were thus defeated by the gods and their hosts. Some died, while others, near death, sought refuge with the gods. The kings of the gods were overjoyed at their victory, roaring with shouts of triumph and laughing loudly. However, Madāsura, hearing this wondrous news, became enraged. With bloodshot eyes, the wicked demon went to the battlefield with the intention of fighting. Accompanied by Shukra and surrounded by great warriors, he rode his chariot with his sons, focusing his mind on the battle.

Then, the three heroic sons of the demon king approached the gods, including Shiva, for battle. Dhanapriya shot arrows at the Sun and said proudly to the illuminator of the three worlds: "Listen to my beneficial words, O wicked Aryaman (Sun). Depart quickly, O mighty-armed one, or you will meet your death. You killed demons before; now suffer the consequences. I will not let you live, O descendant of Kashyapa." Then, the Sun struck the powerful demon with his sword. The demon fell unconscious but regained his senses. The demon's son, full of speed, approached the descendant of Kashyapa and struck him firmly with a sharp-edged sword. Struck hard, the Sun fell unconscious. The victorious demon son captured him and took him to his own place. After bowing to his father, Dhanapriya rejoiced, bound the Sun tightly with ropes, and threw him into a cavern.

Vilāsī approached Vishnu, filled with great rage. Covering him with a barrage of arrows, he said these harsh words: "O Vishnu, you have forcibly destroyed my army and captured the best of the demons while I was not there. Now, struck by my weapons, you shall go to the terrible abode of death; I will not spare you." Then, Vishnu, filled with rage, struck the demon leader Vilāsī with his discus, rendering him unconscious. In a moment, the demon regained consciousness, approached the Lord, and struck him in the chest with a mace, causing Hari (Vishnu) to fall from Garuda. The demon son captured him, took him to his excellent abode, and bowed to his father, who honored him. Mada bound the great Vishnu with ropes and threw him into the cavern. The demons rejoiced and roared terrifically.

Lolupa approached Shiva, who had the bull as his emblem. With eyes red from anger, he said: "Listen, O Nīlalohita (Shiva). You have killed my incomparable army in battle. Now I will kill you and be the one to give you the fruit of your actions." As the fool spoke, Shiva, filled with rage, struck him with his trident, and he fell, suffering greatly from unconsciousness. Shukra suddenly arrived and revived the demon. Lolupa then created a delusion that bewitched all the gods. Suddenly, a terrifying ocean appeared everywhere, and a fierce fire began to burn everyone. Other demons, naked and carrying tridents, appeared, and naked women killed the gods with a barrage of weapons. Shiva, bewildered and then filled with fear, was deluded by the demon's magic.

Meanwhile, as the ocean surrounded everything, the Lord of gods, Shiva, sank into it due to the delusion. Through the power of penance and action, all this was done in the battle. Lolupa captured the sinking Shiva and took him to his place. Honored by his father, the demon Lolupa bound Sadāshiva with ropes and threw him into the cavern. Then, the gods and their hosts, crying out in distress, sought refuge with the Mother of the world (Jagadambā), tormented by terror. The demon kings were all overjoyed and roared like thunder, playing instruments and shouting shouts of victory.

Chapter 50 : The Victory of Madasura

Ghritsamada said: When the powerful sons of the demons attacked the gods and their allies, fear spread throughout the divine army. Some gods were wounded, others were broken, some were killed, and many were captured by the demons in the heat of battle. Some gods sought refuge with the demons, while others fled in terror, reporting the entire situation to the Divine Mother, trembling with fear. Hearing of the unprecedented deeds of the demons, the Great Goddess (Mahamaya), filled with rage, proceeded to the battlefield.

Madasura, knowing that the Mother of the Universe had arrived, went forth surrounded by his sons, chief commanders, and various warriors. Seeing the great demon approaching, the Divine Mother, filled with anger, readied her bow on the battlefield. Hearing the fierce sound of her bowstring, the demons were terrified; some fainted, while others fled in panic. Then, Madasura blew his conch, and its mighty sound filled all three worlds. In response, the sky was filled with the sounds of the goddesses' conches, and a great uproar arose among the demon lords. The warriors and the Shaktis roared like lions, and various musical instruments played as the gods rejoiced.

Meanwhile, the fierce Goddess Kali, surrounded by her Shaktis, crushed the demon army. With tridents, nooses, and various weapons, the Shaktis struck the demons, causing them to flee in all ten directions. Then, the sons of the demons, filled with rage, arrived and rained down arrows like the messengers of death. Struck by these arrows, the divine Shaktis cried out in distress and fled in fear. Kali, assuming a terrifying form, struck the demon army like the fire of universal dissolution. She captured the arrogant sons of the demons, bound them with nooses, and threw them into the pits of the underworld.

Then, the powerful Madasura, filled with rage, readied his bow and released a formidable arrow. From that terrifying weapon of destruction, a powerful being in the shape of a mountain emerged and consumed the strength of the Shaktis. Tormented by the flames of this weapon, the Shaktis were burned and fled in all directions. Helpless and terrified, Kali and the others retreated to the side of Mahamaya and recounted the events. Seeing the Great Goddess herself mounted on a lion and filled with rage, the demon king bowed before her.

Filled with arrogance, the great demon said to the Goddess, "Abandon the gods and take our side. If you do not, O Goddess, I will surely destroy you along with your gods and followers. Behold my great prowess!" Filled with anger, the Great Goddess threw her trident at the demon king to slay him. Struck by the trident, the wicked Madasura did not move; instead, he roared in extreme fury. Abandoning his weapons, the demon lord engaged in a wrestling match with the Goddess. The fierce battle between them was beyond description, as they fought with arms, hands, and feet.

For three days, they fought, each claiming victory and praising the other's skill. Due to the power of his boon, the Goddess’s strength was diminished, and she became powerless. The great demon crushed the Great Goddess with his body, and, filled with joy, he carried away the unconscious Goddess. Other Shaktis were captured or fled, and a great cry of despair arose everywhere. The demon king stopped his men from further slaughter, saying, "These Shaktis are our family deities; there is no doubt."

Madasura then released his three sons, who had previously been captured by the gods. Having conquered the realm of the Shaktis, the arrogant Madasura enjoyed various pleasures with his kin. Deluded by his own nature, the demon king became addicted to women, meat, and wine. He then brought the Goddess before him and said with pride, "O Great Goddess, go wherever you wish along with the gods." Deluded, Madasura told Shiva and the others, "Go wherever you like; the gods are now under my command." He bowed to the Goddess and said with feigned devotion, "Stay here and be worshipped by me, taking our side."

The Goddess, looking weary, replied, "I shall go wherever I wish with the gods, O great demon." The demon king, blinded by pride, released her. The Goddess, along with the gods, left and took refuge in forests and mountain caves, having escaped the terrible danger. The demon, surrounded by his commanders, enjoyed himself in the realm of the Shaktis. He appointed his sons to positions of power and installed them in the realm of the Shaktis. The wicked demon then went to the cities on earth and ruled over all three worlds. He sent his powerful forces to the underworld to conquer the serpent king Shesha. Shesha, learning of the situation, chose diplomacy and agreed to pay an annual tribute to the demon. Thus, the wicked Madasura, having conquered the entire universe, ruled over all the worlds. He gathered all the rare and exquisite pleasures of the gods and enjoyed them with his kin.

Chapter 51 : The Meeting with Sanatkumara

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gritsamada said: Listen, O Prahlada, to the highly astonishing deeds of the demon Madasura, which are great and cause delusion born of pride. After some time had passed, the mighty demon, surrounded by powerful and valiant demons, shone brilliantly. There, the wicked Tripura, along with the demon lords, spoke these bold words to him with folded hands.

Tripura said: Listen, O Lord, O Great King, to these words that are beneficial to the demons, pleasant, achievable, and the source of unbroken joy. In the past, the demons were filled with happiness after conquering the three worlds. Hiranyakashipu was the first among them, possessing great splendor. Free from the fear of death and surrounded by bliss, he undoubtedly enjoyed the sovereignty of the entire three worlds. The gods, who were deceitful and lived in mountain caves, created Narasimha in due time. Hiranyakashipu, the lord, was indeed killed by Narasimha. In this sequence, the great demon lords who followed were also killed one by one. Now, you have become the ruler of the universe yourself. O Great King, consider why you are sitting idle while leaving the gods alone. Our enemies, the gods, are always established in the Vedas. Tell me, O Lord, how can they ever be your friends? Therefore, O best of kings, strive for their destruction. If you do not, they will undoubtedly kill you in time. The gods, tormented by weapons, will never die; they are described in the Vedas as immortal and of terrible form. See, their death is established in the Vedas as being very severe. Since the gods depend on rituals, their destruction will be complete through the cessation of rituals. Therefore, the Brahmins who perform rituals must undoubtedly be punished. You must always consider them as those who perform demonic acts. Other Brahmins who are established in the Varna system and perform rituals will all be destroyed if the Brahmin caste is destroyed. Therefore, O wise one, command the demons to stop the rituals. Let all actions be performed with a demonic nature everywhere.

Having said this to the great demon, Tripura stood silent. Seeing him in the assembly, Madasura spoke to him. Madasura said: O Tripura, you have spoken well, for you are wise. Do exactly as you have said with the help of the demon lords.

Then, the demon Tripura, accompanied by the hosts of demons, departed. Wherever he went on earth, he stopped the performance of rituals. The groups of sages were captured, beaten, and killed by him. Some, following his orders, became destroyers of rituals. Some sages, out of fear, sought refuge with that wicked one, and some base Brahmins became his followers. Others fled to the forests in fear. There, they remained free from anxiety and performed their own duties. Giving up the fear of lions and other beasts, and determined to die, they lived near the lions to perform their rituals. Thus, the Brahmins remained there, unknown to the demon lord. Many Brahmins became fallen from their duties by nature. The demon's agents destroyed pilgrimage sites, sacrificial trees, and temples wherever they went, O Lord of demons. Demonic practices were established everywhere by Tripura, causing wicked confusion among the castes, starting with the Brahmins.

Then, the entire world cried out in agony because of that evil-minded one. Bereft of their own duties, the people became miserable and tormented by the wicked. There was no Svaha, no Vashatkara, and no excellent offerings to the ancestors. Because of this, all the gods, desiring the death of the demon, became emaciated due to fasting over time. Along with their attendants and families, they lost their luster. Then, all the gods, desiring the death of the demon, gathered there and sought a way to kill him. By good fortune, the great yogi Sanatkumara suddenly arrived there, being a benefactor to all. Seeing him, all the gods stood up, bowed to him, and worshipped him with beautiful offerings. Offering him roots and fruits with great devotion, they immediately asked him for the means to destroy Madasura.

The gods said: Blessed is our birth, our penance, our knowledge, our wisdom, and our ancestors. O Yogi, by the sight of your feet, we have attained our purpose. O Great Sage, we have all been conquered by the powerful Madasura. Therefore, O wise one, tell us the way to kill him. O Yogi, the virtuous, the gods, and the Brahmins are tormented by him. Therefore, O all-knowing one, protect the virtuous; we bow to you. You are impartial and stand like the embodiment of Brahman itself. Even so, O Lord, be the destroyer of this one full of faults. Disregard this extremely wicked one and make the whole world happy. Without killing him yourself, be our guide. The virtuous and impartial ones show the path to the poor, the righteous, and the helpless, O Great Sage. The virtuous always engage beings in good deeds so that the destruction of evil deeds may occur. O Great Yogi, that violence is not harmful which stops evil deeds. Through your knowledge, the virtuous destroy ignorance. Therefore, tell us the way for his destruction so that we may act. We are dependent on righteousness, and we wish to establish Dharma.

Thus ends the fifty-first chapter of the second section of the Mudgala Purana, titled "The Meeting with Sanatkumara."

Chapter 52 : The Grace of Ekadanta

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gritsamada said: Being thus requested by the gods who were in a miserable state, the best of sages, Sanatkumara, spoke to them, bringing them joy.

Sanatkumara said: Listen, O Lord of gods and other deities, I shall tell you the remedy for the destruction of the demon Madasura; do as I say, O Lords of the gods. You must all worship Ganesha with great reverence. He, who resides both within and without, will destroy this demon Madasura.

Hearing these words of Sanatkumara, the best of the divine sages, with necks bowed in devotion, bowed to him and spoke.

The Divine Sages said: By what method, O best of sages, should we specifically worship the Lord of gods, Ganesha? Tell us truly.

Being asked thus by the gods and sages, the glorious and great yogi, who was a devotee of Ganesha, spoke to them about the worship.

Worship that Lord, the leader of the Ganas, who resides in the heart, using the single-syllable mantra (Om), and follow the prescribed rituals; He will be pleased by that. Listen, O best of gods, I shall tell you His meditation. You should meditate on Him in that form and please Him with devotion.

He has one tusk, four arms, an elephant face, and a large belly. He is accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi and is mounted on a mouse. He holds a noose, a goad, a lotus, and a gesture of fearlessness, and His face is as pleasant as a lotus. He is the eternal bestower of boons to His devotees and the destroyer of those who are not. Meditate on such an Ekadanta in your hearts and serve Him.

He resides in the hearts of all as the motivator of intellect; He is the Lord of intellect Himself and the true Self of all beings. The word 'Eka' is considered to be Maya, the playful form of the body, and the word 'Danta' is undoubtedly the essence of existence. He who is the holder of Maya and exists by His mere essence is called Ekadanta Ganesha by the knowers of the Vedas. Serve this complete, one-tusked, elephant-faced Lord who holds all existence with a heart full of devotion, and you will always be happy.

Having said this, the yogi Sanatkumara departed with reverence, chanting the 'Jaya Heramba' mantra with his mouth. Then, all the gods and sages engaged in penance and pleased Him with devotion through the single-syllable ritual. Some ate only leaves, some fasted, some lived on air, some on water, and some on roots and fruits. Established in meditation and devoted to chanting and fire sacrifices, they pleased the Lord of the Ganas through various austerities.

After a hundred years had passed, the one-tusked Lord was pleased and appeared to grant them the boon they had sought through their meditation. The elephant-faced Lord spoke to the sages and gods who were engaged in penance: "I am pleased, O Brahmins and immortals, ask for a boon; I shall grant it."

Hearing His words, the divine sages were overjoyed. Opening their eyes, they saw the Lord standing before them. Seeing Him mounted on a mouse, the sages and kings of the gods bowed to Him and worshipped Him with devotion. After worshipping Him according to the scriptures and bowing with folded hands, they praised the one-tusked Lord with necks bowed in devotion.

The Divine Sages said: Salutations to the elephant-faced one, salutations again and again to Ganesha. You are the enjoyer of infinite bliss, the Brahman, the embodiment of Brahman. You are without beginning, middle, or end, and you pervade the moving and non-moving. Salutations to You who reside in the infinite belly and whose navel is the remnant of the universe. You are the creator, the sustainer, and the destroyer; You are the Lord of the three Gunas. Salutations to You who hold all existence and are beyond the Gunas.

Salutations to You, the Lord of Siddhi and Buddhi, and the bestower of Siddhi and Buddhi. Salutations to You, the Lord of gods, who are both Brahman and possessor of attributes. Salutations to You, O large-bellied one, who hold a goad, are adorned with a lotus, and hold a noose and a gesture of fearlessness. Salutations to the God mounted on a mouse, whose banner bears a mouse. Salutations to You, the first to be worshipped, the All, and the one worthy of worship by all. Salutations to You, Ekadanta, who are both the one with a form of attributes and the one whose head is of the nature of the attributeless, appearing as non-different.

Salutations to You, who are beyond the reach of Vedanta and yet attainable through Vedanta. Salutations to You, the Lord of Yoga and the Lord of Brahma. Salutations to You, the source of infinite virtues, the mover of infinite Maya, the one who takes various forms, and the giver of peace. We are blessed, we are blessed, for we have seen the Lord of the Ganas, the very embodiment of Brahman, standing directly before us.

Having praised Him thus, they danced with joy and devotion. Seeing them with tearful eyes and hair standing on end, Dhundhi (Ganesha) spoke.

Ekadanta said: Ask for the boon you desire, O Lords of gods and sages. I shall grant it without doubt, even if it be difficult to obtain. This hymn of mine that you have composed will grant all desires. O gods and Brahmins, whoever reads or hears it will be granted various attainments. It destroys enemies and grants supreme bliss at the end. A person who reads it attains sons, grandsons, and everything else.

Gritsamada said: Hearing His words, the divine sages were filled with joy. Bowing to Ekadanta, they spoke with devotion.

The Divine Sages said: If You are pleased, O Lord of all, O great Ekadanta, and if You wish to grant us a boon, then destroy the wicked great demon. Because of him, the Lords of gods have been displaced from their positions and the sages from their duties. Therefore, O Lord of obstacles, destroy him. Grant us Your unwavering devotion, by which the terrible fear of Maya will never arise.

Being addressed thus, the Lord of the Ganas said "So be it" to them and immediately disappeared, the glorious Ekadanta. Then, the great sages and gods installed Ganesha there, and that region became the place of the remover of obstacles. It is famous in the southeast direction in its own realm. That region of Ekadanta is the bestower of all attainments. The Lords of gods and sages remained there, serving Him with complete devotion in their respective roles. By His vision, a creature attains its highest desire. There is no doubt that one who dies there attains the state of Brahman.

The glory of this cannot be fully told even in hundreds of millions of years; therefore, I have told you the essence of the essence. Having attained special accomplishments there, the pure divine sages become fulfilled, as do many other people. The birth of those who have seen Ekadanta is blessed; He is the Lord who destroys the fear of birth and death and grants one's desired objects.

Thus ends the fifty-second chapter entitled "The Grace of Ekadanta" in the second section of the Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 53 : The Defeat of Madasura

Ghritsamada said: After granting the boon to the gods and sages, Gajanana (Ganesha), the One-Tusked Lord, made Himself invisible and returned to His city. Sometime later, the sage Narada went to the demon king Madasura, who was extremely cruel, and was honored by him. The sage Narada, smiling, said to the great demon, "O King of demons, listen to my words, which bring all happiness. For a hundred years, the gods and sages performed severe penance in the forests to please Ganesha. Pleased by their devotion, Ganesha, the Lord of Brahman, appeared before them and granted them boons. When they begged Him to destroy you, He said, 'He will destroy you quickly, therefore be prepared.' Hearing Narada's words, Madasura became filled with rage. He dismissed the sage and felt deeply distressed. He thought, 'I cannot be killed by those who dwell in this universe. Who is this One-Tusked one? I shall kill him in my anger. I have liberated the gods, and they have repaid me with such enmity; therefore, I shall certainly destroy them.'

Thinking thus, the demon king summoned his great warriors, armed himself for the destruction of the gods, and marched out with his army. Suddenly, the powerful One-Tusked Lord appeared before them, striking terror into their hearts. Seeing Him mounted on a mouse, appearing in a human-serpent form, with four arms and weapons in His hands, the demons were astonished. They began to clamor, "Who is this who has arrived? Look at this great wonder!" Terrified by the sight, the demon leaders stood still and sent a messenger, saying, "Go quickly to this person, find out everything, and return with a peaceful approach." The messenger went to the One-Tusked Lord, bowed, and with folded hands, addressed Ganesha, the Lord of gods.

The messenger said, "O Lord, I am the messenger of Madasura, sent by him. He is the king of the universe and was astonished to see you. Who are you, where have you come from, and what is your purpose? Tell us, O all-knowing one, to remove our doubts." Asked thus, the One-Tusked Lord smiled and replied to the clever messenger, "I dwell in Svananda and have come from there to destroy Madasura for the prosperity of the gods. I am known as Ekadanta (One-Tusked), always established in the bliss of Brahman. O best of messengers, tell the deluded Madasura quickly: if you wish to live, take refuge in Me. Abandon your hatred for the gods and dwell in your own city. Let the gods enjoy their offerings and the demons enjoy the netherworld; let the world be happy, each following their own duty. I have come for this purpose; tell him everything in detail. Otherwise, I shall destroy that cruel Madasura in an instant."

Hearing the words of Ekadanta, the messenger bowed and went to Madasura, reporting everything. Upon hearing the messenger, the demon king fainted, but was revived by his associates and began to lament. He said to all the demon leaders, "The words spoken by Narada have proven true; he is indeed my enemy. I shall kill him with various weapons and missiles; how can my death come to pass, O great demons?" Having said this, the sinful Mada, full of pride, marched out and released a fierce weapon at Ekadanta.
