Seeing the irresistible and infallible weapon, Gajanana lifted His axe and released it, resembling Yama himself. Seeing the axe surrounded by brilliance, the demon leaders were terrified and fled in all ten directions. The axe cut through the demon king's weapon with its own radiance and then struck down the demons everywhere. Seeing the demons killed by the axe, the Tripura demons and others fled in fear.

Then Madasura, enraged, took up his supreme weapon, the Samhara (Destruction) missile, and aimed it at his bow. At that moment, the axe struck the demon king in the heart, and he fell to the ground like a tree struck by a storm. The great weapon then stood before the axe, and after half a period of time, the demon king regained his senses. He saw the great weapon and the axe, which resembled Yama, standing nearby, and was filled with astonishment. "What is this axe of the Lord? It appears to be a mass of brilliance, yet I cannot touch it; this is a wonder." The demon tried to grasp the great axe with his hand, but it felt empty like the sky. Realizing that the weapon was of the nature of Brahman and devoid of physical form, Mada reflected, "Alas, who is this Ekadanta? He is undoubtedly the form of Brahman. He is not limited by the universe, nor is he separate from it. Therefore, this battle will not end well. He will surely kill me; there is no doubt."

Thinking thus, his heart troubled, the demon king Madasura, by divine grace, remembered the auspicious Vedic wisdom. Reflecting on the knowledge derived from Ekadanta and the Lord of Obstacles, the demon king realized Him to be the embodiment of Brahman. The word 'Eka' (One) represents Maya, from which everything arises; the world is full of illusions, delusions, and various plays. The 'Danta' (Tusk) represents the essence of existence, the controller of Maya. Deluded by the reflection, he himself became absorbed in his own bliss. Maya is called illusion, and the essence is called the controller; the union of these two is known as Ganesha, the One-Tusked Lord. "It is my great fortune that He has come within my sight without any effort; I shall take refuge in Him. The great sages like Shuka, who are established in Brahman, always worship Him with devotion; I shall take refuge in Him." Thus, filled with devotion, the demon Mada abandoned the fault of pride and took refuge in Him alone.

Chapter 54 : The Description of the Attainment of Peace by Madasura

Salutations to Lord Ganesha.

Gritsamada said: Madasura, initially terrified, bowed down to the axe that resembled Yama and, out of fear, praised the divine weapon with various words.

Madasura said: Salutations to you, O King of weapons; salutations to you, O great axe. Salutations to you, who are the embodiment of brilliance and the destroyer of time itself. You are the power of Ekadanta, the establisher of righteousness; there is no doubt about this. Protect me, for I have sought your refuge. You are the form of time, the harbinger of the great dissolution; who among those possessing a body is capable of enduring your speed? Therefore, I bow to you, the wonderful form of light. Protect me, for I am terrified and have sought your shelter, O you who are affectionate to those who surrender.

Upon being praised in this manner, the axe became peaceful and, leaving the hand of Ekadanta, it vanished. Then, Madasura quickly went to seek refuge in Ekadanta, bowed to him, and worshipped him with great respect. Having worshipped Ganesha according to the scriptures upon the earth, he prostrated like a staff and praised him with devotion.

Madasura said: Salutations to you, O Ekadanta, the embodiment of Maya and the master of Maya. Salutations again and again to Ganesha, who is always the embodiment of Brahman. Salutations to you, who ride the mouse and have the mouse as your emblem. Salutations to you, who are present everywhere and are free from all bonds. Salutations to you, the four-armed, the beautiful-faced, the pot-bellied Heramba, whose navel is the source of all. Salutations to you, the bearer of the Chintamani, the one who resides in the hearts of all, the elephant-faced Lord of the Ganas, adorned with various ornaments. Salutations to you, the Lord of infinite wealth, the mover of infinite Maya, the giver of joy to your devotees, the Lord of obstacles. Salutations to you, the giver of Yoga to the Yogis, the Lord of all Yogas, the embodiment of the form of Yoga, the bearer of the single tusk. The word 'Eka' signifies your body, which is the creator of Maya, and 'Danta' signifies the essence of existence; salutations to your head. You are devoid of the nature of Maya and are the possessor of the union of both; who is capable of praising you? Therefore, I bow to you. Salutations again and again to you, the protector of those who seek refuge, the one affectionate to those who surrender, and the Lord of Siddhi and Buddhi. Protect me, O Ekadanta, for I have sought your refuge; deliver me from this world, as I have come to you with a sincere heart.

Having said this and bowed down, Madasura circumambulated him and stood before the Lord with folded hands. Seeing his heart devoid of malice and filled with devotion, the Lord of the Ganas spoke to him with a joyful heart.

Ekadanta said: I was filled with anger and intended to destroy you, there is no doubt. But now, I do not kill one who has sought my refuge. Obey my command and remain in your own place forever. Abandon enmity towards the gods and remain devoted to me. Ask for a boon from me, and I shall grant you what you desire, as I am pleased with your humility and especially with your hymn. Whoever reads or listens to this hymn composed by you with effort, O King of demons, will have their desires fulfilled. They will never have any fear born of pride, and in the end, this hymn will grant them my devotion and supreme bliss.

Hearing these words of Ekadanta, Madasura abandoned his demonic nature and, in a peaceful state, spoke to him.

Madasura said: If you are pleased and wish to grant a boon, O Elephant-faced Lord, then grant me firm devotion to your feet, O Ekadanta. Grant me another boon, O Lord of Ganesha, that I may have a permanent livelihood; may I always consume the pride of others while worshipping your lotus feet.

Hearing the words of Madasura, Ekadanta said: O Madasura, your devotion to me will be intense. Wherever I am remembered or worshipped, you shall be present there, ensuring that the work is done peacefully. However, when people act with a demonic nature, you shall manifest the fruits of their actions and consume their pride.

Having been told this, the great demon bowed to him and, saying "So be it," returned to his city, devoted to Ekadanta. The other demons, such as Tripurasura, abandoned him and fled to the netherworlds, while the gods, relieved of their fear, returned to their own abodes.

Chapter 55 : The Conclusion of the Story of Madasura

Gṛtsamada said: Seeing Madasura pacified, the gods led by Vishnu, along with the great sages like Bhrigu, approached Ekadanta. Having bowed to Him and worshipped Him first, they again saluted Him with reverence and, filled with joy, praised Ekadanta, the elephant-faced Lord.

The divine sages said: We take refuge in that Ekadanta, who is the form of pure consciousness, the origin of all, devoid of illusion, of inconceivable knowledge, and who is without beginning, middle, or end. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, who is the embodiment of infinite consci[[[[ousness, Ganesha, the primordial one devoid of distinctions, who dwells in the hearts of the enlightened. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, who rests in the hearts of yogis in deep meditation, shining as the light of consciousness, and who is attained through unwavering contemplation. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, who grants power to the multifaceted Maya, which is full of playfulness and reflects His own image. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, by whose power the universe, created by His own Maya, becomes capable and manifests as the Turiya state, known as the knowledge of the Self. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, who is the support of your own existence, the Lord of the Gunas, the one who reveals the Gunas, and whom the beings of the three realms worship as the unborn primordial Lord. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, by whom the world, driven by the sound of creation, was fashioned into the state of deep sleep, appearing uniform and unified. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, who, out of compassion, created this world, which initially appeared in duality through darkness, yet remains one in essence. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, by whom the subtle world was created, existing in a state of pure Sattva, like the infinite primordial dream. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, who, through your grace, made this dream a form of perfect attainment, yet always remaining one in essence. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, by whose command the world exists in the heart, manifesting as the immeasurable and diverse waking state. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, who, through your grace and remembrance, makes the waking state, which appears through Rajas, seem diverse yet always one in essence. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, who, having created the world from the nature of Prakriti, shines eternally within it as the sole bestower of wisdom, the Lord of the Ganas. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, by whose command all planets and stars shine in the sky as forms of light and revolve eternally in their respective paths. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, by whose command the Creator brings forth creation, Vishnu sustains it, and Hara destroys it. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, by whose command the earth rests in the waters, the rivers flow, and the ocean stays within its shores. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, by whose command the gods in heaven dispense the fruits of actions, and the mountains remain steadfast. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, by whose command Shesha supports the earth, Kama brings delusion, and Aryama governs time. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, by whose command the wind blows, fire dwells in the belly, and this entire moving and non-moving universe exists. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, who dwells in the space between, by whose command all this shines, and who is the infinite form and the awakener of the heart. We take refuge in that Ekadanta, whom the great yogis attain through the power of yoga; who is capable of praising Him? Therefore, may He grant us success through our salutations.

Gṛtsamada said: Having thus praised the Lord Ganesha, the gods and sages became silent and danced with joy. Pleased by the prayers of the divine sages, the compassionate Ekadanta spoke to them.

Ekadanta said: O gods and sages, I am pleased with this hymn. Ask for a boon, and I shall grant your heart's desire. This hymn composed by you, which brings joy, will undoubtedly grant all successes. Whoever recites this hymn will attain whatever they desire—sons, grandsons, wives, wealth, grain, horses, elephants, and royal enjoyments. They will surely attain worldly happiness, liberation, and the peace of yoga. Those who recite or listen to this will be freed from the bondage of black magic, obstacles, and political strife. Whoever recites these twenty-one verses twenty-one times for twenty-one days, while meditating on Me in their heart, will find nothing unattainable in the three worlds; they will overcome the impossible and be victorious everywhere. The person who recites this hymn daily becomes divine; by their mere sight, all the gods are purified.

Hearing these words, the gods and sages were delighted and, with folded hands and devotion, spoke to the elephant-faced Lord.

The divine sages said: We are blessed, O Lord Ganesha, O Ekadanta. There is no doubt that this is due to your vision and your words. Grant us the boon that our devotion to you may become firm, and that we, being free from Maya, may feel fulfilled. O Lord of gods, whenever we are in distress, protect us; this is the boon we ask.

Hearing their request, Ekadanta said, "So be it," and disappeared, returning to His own state of bliss. The gods and sages returned to their respective abodes and, performing their duties, worshipped Ekadanta daily.

Thus, Madasura was pacified by Him. O Prahlada, I have told you this story of Ekadanta. Due to bad company and the pride that filled you, you disregarded the Creator and fell from wisdom; there is no doubt about this. Therefore, O leader of the demons, worship Ganesha, the Ekadanta. By doing so, you will undoubtedly become peaceful. That same Ekadanta, for the success of His devotees, takes on various forms, O demon king. The two formidable demons, Devantaka and Narantaka, were born and then destroyed by Him when He appeared in the house of Kashyapa. Similarly, when the demon Durmati destroyed Dharma, the son of Shiva became Ganesha for his pacification. When the wicked Ganasura destroyed Dharma, the same Ekadanta was born as the son of Kapila. Thus, through various incarnations, Ekadanta, intent on protecting Dharma, sustains the entire world with a portion of His essence. Their stories cannot be fully told by anyone, for even Brahma, the gods, and the sages have not reached the end of His virtues. Therefore, O demon king, worship that Ekadanta with devotion. Through Him, you will attain all knowledge and great peace. Thus, I have briefly told you the story of how Madasura was destroyed by Ekadanta. Whoever reads or listens to this, He grants them devotion and liberation, and the pleased Ekadanta always bestows happiness. You will no longer be tormented by the pride of your demonic nature; the Lord of the demons will always protect you in His peaceful form.

Chapter 56 : The Entry of Vinayaka into Kashi

Daksa said: O great sage, you have narrated the complete story of Madasura as heard from Prahlada. Please tell me what happened next. O Mudgala, after hearing this nectar-like story, my heart is filled with joy, yet I am not satisfied and wish to hear more.

Suta said: Hearing the words of Daksa, Mudgala became delighted and said to that noble soul, listen, O Shaunaka.

Mudgala said: You are blessed, O Daksa, for you understand the essence. Since you rejoice in hearing the nectar of Ganesha’s stories, I shall narrate the tale to you. Upon hearing of the pacification of Madasura, the demon king Prahlada, with humility, asked Ghritsamada about the further story.

Prahlada said: It is my great fortune, O sage, that you have made me hear the greatness of Ekadanta, which bestows supreme bliss. Yet, I am not fully satisfied; please tell me more of the deeds of Ganesha. How did the Lord come to be born as the son of Kashyapa? How did He slay the two demons? Tell me in detail. How did He become the son of Shiva, and how did He slay the wicked ones? You are all-knowing, O ocean of mercy. How did He slay the demon Ganasura in the past? Tell me all this, the story of Ekadanta. Great souls like you are like a boat for those drowning in the ocean of worldly existence.

Mudgala said: Being asked thus, the great yogi Ghritsamada, filled with joy, spoke to the devoted Prahlada.

Ghritsamada said: Listen, Prahlada, I shall briefly tell you everything you have asked, for the story of Ekadanta is blissful. In the past, during the Krita Yuga, there was a demon in the Gauda country. In a city named Gauda, there lived a man of penance named Raudraketu. His wife, Sharada, was a noble and beautiful woman. Once, she became pregnant and was filled with joy. In time, she gave birth to twin sons who struck fear into the world. The Brahmin named them Devantaka and Narantaka. Once, Narada went there and initiated them into the five-syllable mantra of Shiva, and they performed supreme penance. Pleased after a thousand divine years, Sadashiva granted the demons the boon of fearlessness. Proud of the boon, the demon lords conquered all the worlds and brought about the destruction of all rituals on earth. There were no offerings of Svaha or Svadha, and the gods became hungry and thirsty. All the gods prayed to the powerful Lord Ekadanta. Pleased by their intense penance, He granted them a boon, saying, "I shall be born as the son of Kashyapa to slay these demon lords." Having said this, Ganapati departed.

Once, the mother of the gods, Aditi, spoke to the sage Kashyapa with a smile and folded hands. She said, "Indra, Vishnu, Surya, and the gods are my sons. O Lord, how can I have the Supreme Lord as my son? My mind will then become steady, without doubt. I wish to serve Him; tell me the way, O Lord." Hearing her words, he gave her the eight-syllable mantra of Ganesha. By that, she worshipped Ekadanta. At the end of a thousand years, the boon-giver appeared. She asked Him to be her son, and He agreed. Born of Aditi and Kashyapa, He assumed the form of a child, playing like an ordinary human through His Maya.

Once, a terrible demoness named Viraja tried to swallow Him. He tore open her womb and came out, and she died. Then two fierce demons, Dhundhu and Ratya, came in the form of boars. He caught them and smashed them against a mountain, killing them. Once, while Aditi was bathing with Him, a Gandharva in the form of a crocodile grabbed Him by the foot. He dragged the creature out, and it died. Freed from a curse, the creature bowed to Him and went to heaven. During the sacred thread ceremony, five demons disguised as Brahmins came to kill Him, but He slew them in an instant. After the ceremony, the gods led by Brahma and Vishnu, and sages like Vashistha, bowed to Him with devotion. Indra, blinded by pride, did not bow to Him, even when commanded by Kashyapa. To test Him, Mahendra sent the wind god to kill the child, but he was defeated. Then he commanded Agni, who came to burn Him, but the child swallowed him, leaving Mahendra astonished. Through His breath, Vinayaka took the King of Gods into His own belly, where he saw the entire universe. Having seen the various cosmic eggs, Indra’s pride was shattered. He could not find the way out for a hundred years. Then he prayed to the sinless Vinayaka, who released him through His breath.

Then Mahendra witnessed a great wonder, the miracle of time. Listen, O great demon: though Indra stayed in Vinayaka’s belly for a hundred years, outside, only a moment had passed. Then he bowed to Him, praised Him with various hymns, and began to worship Him with devotion. Later, the sage Kashyapa came to the house of the sage, and for the sake of his son’s marriage, he invited Kashyapa. The sage, busy with a sacrifice, gave his young son Vinayaka to him to act as the teacher. The King of Kashi took Ganesha and set out on the road. There, he killed the demon Dhumraksha with the solar weapon. His two sons, of great strength, came to devour Vinayaka. Vinayaka took them into the sky with his breath, and while wandering, they fell into the palace of Narantaka. Learning of this, the demon king Narantaka sent five hundred demons. The Lord saw the mighty demons and killed them all with a single fierce roar.

Then, accompanied by the King of Kashi, Vinayaka reached Kashi and entered, worshipped by the people. The sage Mudgala had previously instructed the King of Kashi, who worshipped Ganapati with devotion. All the people in Kashi became intent on meditating on Ganesha and, having learned of His greatness from Mudgala, became wise. He is the son of Kashyapa who will slay the demons, and He will come to Kashi for the marriage. By His mere sight, all men and women in Kashi will become great yogis, without doubt. Thus, all the people were instructed by the sage Mudgala, and knowing Him to be the divine Vinayaka, they all worshipped Him. All the women came to the main road to see Him, disregarding their fathers, brothers, and husbands, filled with devotion. A great festival took place during His entry. One woman, stopped from going, gave up her body. By the bursting of her palate, she reached Vinayaka first, and seeing Him, she merged into His body with devotion. Wherever the Lord was worshipped by the people on the main road, the two demon boys wandered. Vinayaka crushed them with his hands over ten yojanas; they fell, and the people were astonished, recognizing Him as Ganapati. Then they came in the form of a whirlwind, but He grabbed them by their hair and smashed them, killing them. Then a demon in the form of a stone lay on the path, but the child killed him with his fist. The King of Kashi then took Him to his own house and worshipped Him according to the rites, establishing Him there with devotion.

Chapter 57 : The Description of Bhrushundi’s Devotion

Salutations to Lord Ganesha.

Ghritsamada said: On the second day, the great sage Dharmadatta brought the son of the sage (Ganesha in the form of a child) to his home with great effort. On the way, the child killed two demons who had assumed the form of a donkey; later, he also killed the demons of lust and anger who had taken the form of elephants. Thereafter, the sage worshipped Vinayaka with a pure heart and offered his two beautiful daughters, Siddhi and Buddhi, to him.

Then, a fierce demoness named Jrimbha arrived, having assumed the form of a beautiful woman with a pleasant face. She massaged Vinayaka with poisoned oil, but the Lord of Lords, knowing her true nature, killed her, and she met her death. On the fourth day, powerful demons like Jwalamukhi, Vyaghrasya, and the formidable Daruna arrived. The son of Kashyapa killed them playfully, just as a child might effortlessly destroy a toy through his divine illusion. Brahma, Vishnu, and others stood by, watching the extraordinary wonder of this divine child’s play.

On the fifth day, the King of Kashi, having bowed to the son of the sage, invited him for a meal. On the morning of the fourth day of the bright fortnight, the great sage Bhrushundi worshipped the Lord with his mind. Seeing this worship the next morning, the King was astonished and asked, "O Lord, tell me, who performed this worship?" The Lord replied, "My beloved devotee Bhrushundi performs this worship; look, O King." The Lord added, "This Brahmin worships me with single-minded devotion; he knows nothing other than me, not even holy places or other deities."

Hearing these beautiful words, the King bowed to Vinayaka and asked to be shown this yogi. Upon hearing the King of Kashi’s request, the Lord sent him to Bhrushundi. The King rode his swift horse to the sage's hermitage. Seeing the sage, who possessed a trunk (like Ganesha), the King was amazed, bowed down, and prayed to the great yogi to visit Kashi with devotion.

The King of Kashi said: "O Master, I have come to your hermitage by the command of Vinayaka. The Lord himself, who resides in my home, calls for you. Today, on the fourth day, you worshipped him mentally, and the Lord showed me that great worship. He is known as Vinayaka in Kashyapa’s house, having been worshipped through penance as the leader of the Ganas. I am his servant and patron, and I brought him to my house for the marriage of my son. Vinayaka told me to bring the great sage, so I beseech you, O great yogi, to come to the great city of Kashi."

Hearing this, the great sage, who was a devotee of Ganesha, replied to the wise King of Kashi. Bhrushundi said: "Listen, O fortunate King, if the son of Kashyapa has come to your house due to your devotion, even then, I never bow to anyone who lacks a trunk. Know, O best of kings, that all other deities, including Vishnu and Shiva, are merely partial manifestations of his essence. They all bow to me because I am devoted to the one who possesses the trunk. I do not bow to anyone except the elephant-faced Lord. Therefore, you go to Kashi, and I shall bring Vinayaka there."

Hearing this single-minded devotion, the King of Kashi bowed and said: "O Sage, listen to my words. I am not capable of going to Kashi today, as my vehicle is exhausted by the afternoon." The sage then told the King to close his eyes, and through his power, he sent the King to the city. Upon opening his eyes, the King was astonished to see his own palace and bowed to Vinayaka. Hearing the account from the King, the Lord of Ganas, through his illusion, made it appear as if he were back at the sage's hermitage.

Seeing the King return, the great sage asked, "O King, why have you come to my house again today?" The King replied, "O Master, Vinayaka, the Lord of Obstacles, calls for you. I have been sent by him, the bearer of the trunk. Come quickly with me to see the complete elephant-faced Lord." Hearing these beautiful words, the great sage rolled on the ground with devotion, his body covered in goosebumps and his eyes filled with tears.

The King of Kashi was amazed, thinking, "Behold, this is devotion incarnate." Moved by this state, Bhrushundi left his hermitage to travel. Considering that the journey to the Lord should be made with physical effort, the great sage decided to walk to Avimukta (Kashi). Knowing the resolve in the sage's heart, the Earth goddess transported him and the King to Kashi in an instant.

The King showed the city to the sage and said, "Master, we have arrived in Kashi through the power of your penance." The King then took him to his home and worshipped him. Vinayaka, seeing his devotee arrive, shed tears of joy. Recognizing the sage's state, Vinayaka assumed his form with a trunk, mounted his mouse, and went to the great sage. The Brahmin bowed upon seeing the four-armed Lord, who was adorned with a large belly and a single tusk.

Bhrushundi worshipped him with devotion and, bowing again, praised him with various hymns. Overcome with emotion, he spoke with a faltering voice, feeling fulfilled by the sight of his Lord. Bhrushundi said: "O Elephant-faced Lord, I am fulfilled by your mere sight. By great fortune, I have seen your feet, O Lord. Now, grant me a boon so that I may be satisfied: ensure that I never forget you, O Lord. Remain in my place in this very form and be known as the 'Fulfiller of Desires' (Ashapuraka). I shall worship you there with exclusive, heartfelt devotion. Make me a complete devotee like Mudgala, or even greater than him, O Lord of Obstacles."

The Lord of Ganas replied, "So be it," and then played as a child. The sage bowed and returned to his own place, leaving the King of Kashi astonished. Reflecting on this, the King became single-minded in his devotion to Ganesha, having witnessed the great character of Bhrushundi. He realized that the great sage was blessed, having abandoned the illusion of many forms to worship the Lord of Ganas through yoga.

The King understood that the yoga of all Brahmas resides in Ganesha, which is why the great sage worshipped him with a single heart. Such single-minded devotion has never been seen before and will never be seen again. The Lord of Ganas resides everywhere, and by worshipping him, one receives the fruits of worshipping all others. Bhrushundi had taken refuge in the One who is the source of all. The King realized that there was no one like Bhrushundi, for he saw the elephant-faced Lord directly. The sage had come to Kashi, the city of liberation, but did not even mentally remember Manikarnika or Vishweshwara (Shiva), for he had come solely for Ganesha. Having bowed to him, the great sage returned to his own hermitage. Thus, the King of Kashi also worshipped Ganesha with wonder and attained the same state of grace.

Chapter 58 : Describing the Curse of Yama

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Prahlada said: O protector of the humble, Ghritsamada, listen to my words. Tell me now, who is this sage named Bhrushundi? By what penance did he emerge from Bhrushunda, and what kind of mental worship did he perform? Tell me in detail. Having heard of the greatness of this excellent sage, I am filled with supreme wonder; he is truly blessed on this earth. Those who are devoted to the worship of Ganesha are embodiments of Brahman, and the stories of such people purify both the speaker and the listener.

Mudgala said: Being asked thus, the great yogi Ghritsamada, seeing Prahlada as a worthy vessel of devotion, spoke with joy.

Ghritsamada said: Listen, Prahlada, with devotion to this story which purifies the world, which is sacred and grants the state of Brahman, and which concerns Bhrushundi. Long ago, in a village called Nandura, there lived a fisherman named Nama, who was well-known as a great sinner. He would carry weapons through villages and forests, always killing living beings without any compassion. Seeing travelers on the road, he would kill them out of greed for their wealth, and after gathering his loot, he would return to his village every day. Even if they had no money or clothes, he would kill them; being cruel by nature, he never spared any life. By taking their wealth, he became rich, yet he was never satisfied by the accumulation of goods. There was no limit to his sins; even Brahma and Shesha would fear to account for them. He had committed countless murders of women, cows, children, and Brahmins, and the earth itself felt burdened by his weight.

Once, in the middle of the forest, he saw an excellent man and ran towards him with joy, weapon in hand, to kill him. Seeing the wicked man approaching, the man fled to a distance, and after running for two miles, he reached a village. Nama, exhausted from the chase, fell on the road, feeling great sorrow, and ceased his killing. Walking slowly, he saw a pond on the way. The wicked man bathed there to relieve his fatigue. That was a sacred pond belonging to the Lord of Ganas (Ganesha), and by the power of its influence, his sins were lessened. As his sins diminished, Nama thought in his heart: "Alas, how many sins have I committed? I do not even know! Having died, I will surely fall into a terrible hell and suffer for many kalpas. How will I endure that?"

Mudgala said: Prahlada, who was filled with devotion, asked Ghritsamada with humility about the sage who brought such joy.

Prahlada said: What is the nature of that pond, and what penance was performed there that it became the abode of Ganesha?

Then Ghritsamada, filled with joy and being a great devotee of Ganesha, told Prahlada everything clearly.

Ghritsamada said: I will tell you the ancient history of the dialogue between the Sun and Yama, which destroys all sins. Vishwakarma gave his daughter, the goddess Sanjna, who was full of karma and possessed the power of illusion, to the Sun. The Sun performed penance for a hundred years to please her. Vishwakarma requested, "O goddess, be my daughter." By his boon, she became his daughter. The Sun took her home and lived with her for a long time, and through his power, she gave birth to two sons and a daughter: Vaivasvata Manu, Yama, and the river Yamuna. All of them were righteous and noble.

Being scorched by the intense heat of the Sun, Sanjna created a shadow (Chhaya) of herself, possessing her own form. She told her everything—about the children and the household—and instructed her not to reveal the truth if asked by the Sun. Chhaya replied, "If the Sun tries to force me, O noble one, I will surely tell him." Sanjna said, "Go, goddess, do as you wish; I will accomplish whatever task is yours." Sanjna then went to her father's house. When the Sun asked her not to leave her husband and home, she went to the forest in the form of a mare and performed penance, meditating on Lord Ganesha. She pleased the Lord of Obstacles with a mantra, and after a hundred years, Ganesha appeared. The Lord was worshipped and praised by her, and he granted her the desired boon. He said, "O goddess, your son will be of gentle nature, and you shall be free from the bonds of illusion and devoted to me. Whatever you desire will be fulfilled. The Sun, free from anger, will come here and take you." Having said this, Ganesha disappeared, and she remained there meditating on him.

Meanwhile, the Sun lived with Chhaya and their children, who were righteous and powerful: Savarni Manu, Shanaishchara (Saturn), and the daughter named the river Tapi. Chhaya, out of love for her own children, treated Sanjna's children with partiality. Manu, being wise, endured his mother's injustice, but Yama, the Lord of Dharma, repeatedly forbade her. She did not abandon her nature, and the angry Yama tried to kick her. The mother cursed her son for his lack of discipline, saying, "Your foot shall become diseased and fall to the earth." Dharma, filled with anger, went to the Sun and, fearful of the curse, told him the whole story.

Chapter 59 : The Story of the Origin of the Kund (Sacred Pool)

Gṛtsamada said: Hearing the words of Yama, Savitā (the Sun God) was astonished. He realized that the anger of the righteous Yama towards his mother had manifested. Because the mother had cursed her own son with a sense of differentiation, something contrary to the natural order had occurred.

Then, realizing the truth through his wisdom, the Sun God spoke to Yama, saying that a mother’s curse cannot be rendered false by him. Therefore, he advised Yama to worship Lord Vināyaka with devotion, assuring him that if the Lord were pleased, Yama would attain happiness. Having said this, Āryamā (the Sun) gave his son the five-syllable mantra of Ganesha, and Yama respectfully went to the forest.

The Sun God then went to Chāyā and asked her where Saṃjñā had gone, threatening to curse her if she did not tell the truth. Frightened, she told him the truth, explaining that Saṃjñā had gone to her father’s house after leaving her in the Sun’s home. Pleased by her truthfulness, the Sun did not curse her. Filled with anger, he went to Viśvakarmā.

With red eyes, he bowed and asked about the situation. Viśvakarmā consoled him with logic, saying that he had sent Saṃjñā to the Sun’s house, and if she had not arrived, she must be hiding somewhere in a disguised form. He explained that she had become emaciated, scorched by the Sun’s brilliance, and advised him to become gentle in his radiance so that he might enjoy her company.

The Sun God then asked Viśvakarmā to make him gentle, as he had attained the status of a master craftsman. Viśvakarmā placed the Sun on his machine and removed his intense brilliance, making him mild. The Sun then went to find Saṃjñā. Seeing her in the forest in the form of a mare, he transformed into a horse and attempted to approach her. Realizing he was a stranger, she meditated on Ganesha. Consequently, the Sun’s energy was respectfully deposited in her nostrils. Through her breath, she forcefully expelled the Sun’s energy, and upon realizing it was her husband, she became joyful.

Seeing her firm resolve, the Sun was pleased. He revealed his true form, took her, and returned home. From the energy of the Sun that Saṃjñā had cast aside, the Aśvinī Kumāras were born, who became the best of physicians and were honored by all. Saṃjñā, through her devotion to Ganesha, became happy and constantly worshipped Him with a focused mind.

Yama also performed severe penance with deep devotion. Fasting and meditating on the Lord, he became absorbed in chanting. After a thousand years, Ganesha appeared to grant him a boon. Seeing Him, Yama immediately stood up, worshipped Him, and offered prayers.

Yama said: Salutations to You, O Ganapati, who plays with various illusions. Salutations to You who exist in the guise of an illusion-bearer. Salutations to You who are devoid of illusion, the witness, the origin of the world, the knower of Brahman, and Brahman Itself. Salutations to the Infinite, to Heramba, to Dhundhi, to Vighneśa, to the Three-Eyed One, and to the Pot-Bellied One. Salutations to the Elephant-Faced God, the Lord of the Devas, the destroyer of the pride of the gods, and the crusher of demons. Salutations to the One who loves devotion, who grants various joys to His devotees, who has infinite faces and infinite hands. Salutations to the One with infinite heads, who is the embodiment of Brahman, the Creator, the Auspicious One, worshipped by Shiva, and the giver of auspiciousness. Salutations to the One with unbroken glory, the one whose banner bears a mouse, the one who rides the best of mice, and the one with a single tusk. Salutations to You who are the beginning, middle, and end, who possess all forms, who hold the noose and the goad, and who are the enjoyer of all. Who is capable of praising You, who are the embodiment of Yoga? Therefore, I bow to You; be pleased with me, O Lord. I am blessed in every way to have seen the Lord of Ganas. Now, protect me, Your devotee, for You are affectionate to those who seek refuge.

Hearing Yama, the glorious son of the Sun and a supreme devotee, Ganesha spoke. Ganesha said: O fortunate one, Yama, ask for the boon you desire in your heart. I am pleased by your great devotion; there is no doubt.

Yama then said: O Elephant-Faced One, grant me firm devotion at Your feet. Grant me another boon: to remove my mother’s curse. May whatever I desire be fulfilled, O Lord of Obstacles.

Hearing his words, Ganesha said, "So be it." He assured Yama that his devotion would remain unshakable. He told him that due to the mother’s curse, he would suffer a little pain; worms would quickly consume the flesh from his leg, and it would fall to the earth, but he would then be healed. Whatever he desired would become easily attainable. He would become the guardian of the southern direction, known as Dharmarāja, and would be worshipped everywhere. He would be considered the best among those who uphold duty, and he would be the one to grant the fruits of actions to all living beings.

Ganesha declared that Yama’s command would prevail in the three worlds through righteousness. He promised that those who recite or listen to this holy hymn would be freed from the terrible torments of Yama, and that He would grant them His own bliss at the end. He added that wherever Yama bathed with devotion to Him, it would be known as the Ganesha Tirtha, and bathing there would destroy all sins and grant immediate wisdom. Having said this, the Lord disappeared, and Yama remained there.

Yama established an immovable idol of Heramba with the help of great Brahmins and created a pond marked by the footprints of Ganesha. It became famous as Heramba-Ganapa. Yama was freed from the curse and became the ruler of the direction. He constantly meditated on Ganapati in his heart, became impartial to all, and attained great fame and honor.

This divine story of the origin of the Kund, composed by Yama, which grants all accomplishments, has been told to you. The noble person who listens to or reads this greatness of Yama will attain all that is auspicious. Now, O King of Daityas, listen to the ongoing story of Bhṛśuṇḍi, which destroys sins and grants all desired objects.

Chapter 60 : The Description of the Brahmanhood of Bhrushundi

Gritamada said: A great sinner named Kaivartaka once saw the great sage Mudgala standing in a vast forest. Seeing the great sage, the sinner felt a surge of malice and, holding a sword, rushed toward him with firm intent to kill. However, as he approached to strike, the sword fell from his hand onto the ground. All his other weapons, which were securely fastened, also fell to the earth, leaving him astonished. Looking at Mudgala, the wicked man wondered at this miracle, and as he gazed upon the sage, his impure heart became purified.

Bowing down to the sage with folded hands, the sinner spoke, his words becoming as virtuous as those of a saint merely by the sight of the yogi. Kaivartaka said: From my birth until today, my mind has been set on sin, and the number of sins I have committed is countless. Yet now, by your mere presence, my mind has become pure and begins to fear sin. My heart is filled with contentment, and my soul desires liberation. My weapons have fallen away, and I shall not take them up again. O wise one, please give me instruction that will lead to liberation from this worldly existence.

Hearing his words, Mudgala told him to return home and take his weapons, but the sinner, weeping with tears of repentance, bowed and said: Make me your disciple, O king of yogis; otherwise, I shall abandon this body. In your presence, I no longer desire worldly pleasures. Hearing this, the sage, who is compassionate to those who seek refuge, gave him the mantra of Lord Ganesha. The sage planted his staff in the ground and told the sinner to remain there, chanting the mantra. He instructed him to chant until the staff sprouted, and to abstain from all food, water, and even air, living only on the mantra.

After a thousand years had passed, an anthill grew over him, covered by grass and creepers. Yet, he continued to chant the mantra with devotion, and by its power, the staff sprouted. All his sins were destroyed, and a vast ocean of merit arose. Through his intense devotion and merit, a trunk emerged from between his eyebrows, making him appear like another Gajanana (Ganesha).

The glorious Mudgala returned and remembered his disciple. Seeing the staff had sprouted, he was amazed that the once-wicked man had engaged in such deep penance. He saw the anthill and the eyes of Kaivartaka within it. He cleared the anthill with the staff and sprinkled water on him, bringing him to consciousness. Seeing his guru, the disciple rose and bowed. The sage embraced him and said: You have emerged from the anthill as my son; there is no doubt. From now on, you shall be known as the great Brahmana Bhrushundi. You are perfected by the mantra and shall be worshipped by all; by your mere sight, people shall attain liberation. You shall live for a hundred thousand aeons, and in every aeon, great beings like Shiva shall be your disciples.

After saying this, the sage gave him the single-syllable mantra of Ganesha and disappeared. Bhrushundi continued his penance, and within a year, Gajanana appeared before him. Ganesha asked him to choose a boon, and Bhrushundi began to praise him with hymns born of his own experience. He praised Ganesha as the source of all peace, the creator, protector, and destroyer, and the essence of all existence. He marveled at how he, a low-born sinner, had been transformed into a supreme Brahmana by the power of Ganesha's name.

Ganesha, satisfied with his devotion, told him to ask for any boon. Bhrushundi, with tearful eyes, asked only for unwavering devotion to Ganesha. Ganesha granted this and declared that the place where he sat would become a holy site named Amalashrama, which would destroy the impurities of ignorance and grant pure knowledge. Ganesha then disappeared. Bhrushundi remained there, constantly worshipping him. Sages like Kashyapa and Shuka, and even the gods led by Indra, Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva, came to visit him, finding themselves blessed by his presence. Thus, I have described the greatness of Bhrushundi, who, though a low-born sinner, became a Brahmana through the remembrance of Ganesha.

Chapter 61 : the Story of Bhrishundi

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gritsamada said: That best of Brahmins, the highly fortunate one, performed the mental worship of Ganesha daily, as well as the external worship according to the prescribed rites. O great-minded one, listen to the mental worship you have asked about, by which a person can become a follower of Ganesha in a very short time. O best of the Asuras, mental worship is known to be of four types; it is said to be of two types with a support (sadhar) and two types without a support (niradhar).

The wise consider that to be with support (sadhar) in which one meditates upon the form of Ganesha in the heart and worships the deity there with mental offerings. The other type is the realization of the oneness of the self everywhere with a focused mind; whatever enjoyments or actions are performed with that attitude become worship. In that state, the person attains the support of the form of oneness, and therefore, the wise call it the worship with support.

O Prahlada, know for certain that the worship of the great-souled Ganesha is considered to be without support (niradhar) when, by restraining the fluctuations of the mind, one offers everything—devoid of all individual identity—through the mind and speech as an offering. When the five states of the mind are dissolved and one remains in a state of equilibrium, the peace that is perceived in the form of Yoga is the worship. In that state of Yoga, all injunctions and prohibitions are offered to Ganesha as ritual offerings; that indeed becomes the worship. The Yogis have called this worship without support, which exists in the state of peaceful Yoga. O Daitya, thus mental worship is of four types.

In this way, that Bhrishundi performed worship specifically with devotion in Kashi, which reached Ganesha. The person who hears or narrates this wonderful story of Bhrishundi attains the bliss of the self. Whatever one desires, one surely obtains; there is no doubt about this. I have narrated to you this most meritorious and excellent story.

Thus ends the sixty-first chapter named the Story of Bhrishundi, in the second section of the glorious Mudgala Mahapurana, which is the essence of the Puranic Upanishads.

Chapter 62 : The Story of Vinayaka

Gritamada said: Then, Vinayaka, the increaser of his devotees' joy, played in Kashi along with a group of children, appearing as an ordinary boy. At that time, the demon Hemasura arrived in the form of a Gana (attendant), but the Lord of Ganesha destroyed him through his divine illusion. Following this, the demon Kupasura arrived along with Kandara; the Lord of Ganas caused them to destroy each other. Then, three wicked demons named Ambhosura, the powerful Andhaka, and Tungasura, who had all obtained boons from Brahma, arrived. They were killed by Ganesha, who had taken the form of a bird, and they attained liberation due to the touch of Vinayaka's hand. Their mother, a demoness named Bhramari, arrived in the form of Aditi, but she too was killed by Him.

Astonished by these events, the people who were seeking food invited Him, and they all went along with the King of Kashi. One day, there was a great festival in every house, and the people prepared materials for the meal of Kashyapa (Ganesha). Shukla, a great devotee living on the outskirts of Kashi, lived by the 'Shukla-vritti' (begging only what is needed) and heard of the Lord's activities. Shukla was poor, yet contented and devoted to worship; his wife, Vidruma, was equally devoted to her husband. With a spirit of total sacrifice, they prepared for the festival. His wife cleaned the courtyard for the meal, and the great sage (Shukla) tied Darbha grass to a large pole to create a flag. The virtuous lady prepared a dish made from the flour of all grains, and with great joy, she prepared rice mixed with water. Shukla respectfully brought a small amount of oil. Even though he had only three palas of flour in his house, it miraculously became five palas. He cooked the food for Kashyapa with great devotion and kept a piece of dried myrobalan for mouth-cleansing. After performing the Vaishvadeva offering, he became absorbed in the meditation of Vinayaka with great devotion.

Meanwhile, Sanaka and Sanandana arrived to see the great Kashyapa. Seeing the King honoring the Brahmins and observing Vinayaka accompanied by children, they rebuked Him and left for the Ganga. Vinayaka, along with the children, went to Shukla's house first; after eating the humble food, He granted him supreme prosperity. Then, Vinayaka, the Lord who assumes infinite forms, took the form of the King of Kashi and ate in every house. At that time, Sanaka and Sanandana saw Him wandering from house to house begging for food. Seeing Him engaged in such conduct, the sages, feeling hungry, left Kashi. Then, Vinayaka revealed His true form, and the sages, realizing their mistake, sought His refuge. The great Lord bestowed upon them Ganesha-knowledge, and they departed, living thereafter with the nature of Ganas.

The King of Kashi did not know of these great deeds. Deluded, the King happily went to Shukla's house to bring Him back. The King placed Ganesha in a palanquin and headed toward his palace, but two demons in the form of lightning descended there. Vinayaka released them after grabbing their tufts of hair, and they went to tell their story to Narantaka. The King reached his palace, and then the demon arrived. Hearing the entire account from them, Narantaka was enraged. The people went to the city gate and informed the King of Kashi that Narantaka had arrived. The King of Kashi, fully armed with his army, went to face him. He fought the demon but was captured in an instant.

Then, the angry Lord Vinayaka commanded Siddhi. She created a massive army that appeared as fierce as Death itself. One cruel leader bowed to the Lord of Ganas and devoured the demon's great army in an instant. He captured Narantaka and took him to Vinayaka. The Lord of Ganas absorbed the army commander into His own mouth. Ganesha protected the King of Kashi within His stomach, and the King became absorbed into Ganesha's body. The Lord then showed the King the entire universe from within His stomach and released him through the pores of His skin, along with his two sons. The King, having emerged through his two sons, was bewildered and enchanted by His illusion. Seeing this, the demon king was astonished and reasoned that this must be the Divine Lord, the son of Kashyapa. He thought that if death came at His hands, it would be auspicious and lead to Brahman-realization, so he fought with joy. After a great battle, the Lord of Obstacles, in His cosmic form, deluded Narantaka and killed him. The demon king died, and his head fell into his house. Seeing this, his parents mourned.

Devantaka, seeing his brother's head, was filled with rage and went to Kashi. Vinayaka fought a fierce battle with him and revealed His elephant-headed form to the enemy. Seeing this, the demon broke His left tusk, but the Lord of Obstacles killed the demon king with that very tusk. The Lord was then praised by the gods and sages, and He entered Kashi with the King, who was devoted to Him. The Lord of gods performed the marriage of the King's son. Honored by the King, Dhundhi (Ganesha) became eager to return to His abode. Many devotees left their homes and followed Him; the Lord gave them knowledge, and they attained peace and returned to their homes. The King took the Lord to His abode in a chariot, and the Lord of Ganas sent the King of Kashi back to Kashi. The people established Dhundhiraja in the city and worshipped Him daily with devotion.

Vinayaka spoke to Aditi and Kashyapa, saying, "I was born as your son to remove the burden of the earth. I have accomplished all that was to be done, and now I return to My own abode." Having said this, He bestowed knowledge upon them and disappeared. They established an image of the Lord Ganesha, which became famous by the name Vinayaka. Prahlada and the King of Kashi, following the teachings of Mudgala, attained the Lord of Ganas in His own abode while still in their physical bodies.

This is the entire story of Vinayaka, briefly told, which arose from the incarnation of Ekadanta. Whoever hears or reads this will attain their desired fruits and, at the end, will reside in Svananda (the Lord's abode) and become one with Brahman.

Chapter 63 : The Arrival of Shani

Ghritsamada said: Listen to the glorious and supreme account of the Lord of Prosperity, the great-souled Ganesha, which destroys all sins and grants both worldly enjoyment and liberation.

Long ago, there was a proud and wicked son of the demon king named Durmati, who was as massive as a great mountain. This powerful demon performed severe penance, meditating on the Goddess Shakti with the nine-syllable mantra, having been initiated by Shukracharya. After a thousand years, the Goddess became pleased and appeared before him, the foremost of devotees, asking him to choose a boon. Having worshipped the Goddess and being endowed with her grace, he received the boon of fearlessness. Through her power, he conquered the three worlds, gained victory in battle, and attained health, while the Goddess, riding her lion, returned to her own realm.

By this means, he conquered all living and non-living beings, forcing the gods, led by Vishnu, to take refuge in the forests. The wicked demon, having harassed the Goddess who was united with Shiva, attempted to make her his wife. Shakti then left the world, accompanied by Shiva, and remained hidden in the forest. After some time, Narada arrived there and instructed them on the auspicious worship of the Lord of Obstacles. He told them that if they worshipped Ganesha, he would be born as their son and would undoubtedly destroy Durmati.

Having said this, Narada departed, and Shiva and Shakti performed severe penance, chanting the sixteen-syllable mantra of Ganesha. After a hundred years, Ganesha became pleased and appeared. Seeing the Lord of Ganas, they bowed at his lotus feet and praised him with folded hands.

Shiva and Shakti said: Salutations to the Lord of Ganas, the Lord of all beings. You are dear to your devotees, O Lord of gods, and the giver of happiness to them. Salutations to you, who dwell in Svananda, the bestower of success and wisdom, the Lord of the navel, and the King of Dhundhi. Salutations to you, who hold the boon and fearlessness in your hands, the wielder of the axe, the healer, the all-pervading, and the one worthy of worship by all. Salutations to you, who are both with attributes and without, the Brahman, the giver of Brahman, O Elephant-faced one. Salutations to you, the eldest, the first to be worshipped, the King of the elders, the mother and father of all, O Heramba. Salutations to you, the beginningless, the creator of obstacles, and the remover of obstacles for your devotees, O Lambodara. Through devotion to you, the masters of yoga attain peace. How can we praise you, who are the very form of yoga? We bow to you, O Lord of obstacles. Please be pleased with us, O Lord.

Having said this, they bowed. The Lord of Ganas raised them and said: This hymn composed by you will increase devotion to me; those who read or hear it will attain happiness. It grants worldly enjoyment, liberation, children, grandchildren, and wealth. I am pleased with your devotion and the hymn, so ask for a boon.

Shiva and Shakti said: If you are pleased, O Elephant-faced one, then become our son, so that we may feel fulfilled. Grant us firm devotion to your lotus feet and destroy Durmati to bring happiness to the three worlds.

Ghritsamada said: Hearing their words, the Lord said "So be it" and vanished. Shiva and Shakti, filled with joy, wandered there. Once, during the month of Vaishakha, Shiva and Parvati performed sacred bathing in the Himalayas for the pleasure of the Lord of Ganas. Daily, they bathed in the waters of the Bhagirathi and worshipped a clay image of Ganesha with durva grass and other offerings. On the full moon of Vaishakha, at dawn, they bathed and performed meditation with great devotion. As they meditated, the Lord of Ganas appeared within the image, and it became alive. The four-armed Lord of Ganas appeared and said to his parents: "I am your son, without a doubt. Through the power of the boon and the merit of the Vaishakha bath and penance, protect me as your son always."

Then, Shiva and Shakti bowed to the Lord and praised him with the Upanishads mentioned in the Atharvaveda. Ganesha then delighted them with his divine play, revealing his nature as their son. Shiva performed the birth rituals with a joyful heart. Later, Lakshmi’s daughter, Pushti, was born through penance, and Vishnu joyfully gave her to Ganesha.

Once, the great planet Shani arrived there with his wife to see Ganesha. The demon-slayer bowed to Ganesha and his consort and said: "My birth, knowledge, and penance are blessed, for I have received your vision, O Elephant-faced one. You are the very form of yoga, and Pushti is the nourisher; you are the great power that sustains the universe. Therefore, I bow to you."

Meanwhile, Shakti herself arrived there and, seeing the son of the Sun bowing, said with great affection: "Look, look, O best of planets, look everywhere with proper understanding." Then, Shani, feeling deeply distressed, spoke to the Goddess.

Chapter 64 : The Description of the Embassy of Vishnu

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Prahlada asked: Why did the god Shani become sorrowful? Tell me the entire story of the actions of the son of the Sun.

Gritsamada replied: Listen, O best of the virtuous, to the account of what happened there. By hearing it, all your doubts will certainly be destroyed.

Shani said: Listen, O Shakti, O Mahamaya. Once, I was in my home, engaged in the worship of the Sun and absorbed in meditation. At night, my wife arrived, adorned with all ornaments, and spoke to me in great distress. O son of the Sun, grant me the marital union now. Look at me, fully adorned; salutations to you, my husband. I, being absorbed in meditation, did not see my beloved. After two muhurtas passed, she cursed me in anger: Since you do not look at me, O husband, your union will be fruitless, and whatever you look upon will be destroyed. Having said this, she departed. Since that day, I have not been able to see the world properly, O Mother of the Universe.

Hearing his words, the Mother of the Universe said to him: Look at Ganesha, O planet, and by that, everything will become auspicious. Being told this, Shani looked at Ganesha, who was shining in all his limbs and was with his consort, O best of the virtuous. Then, suddenly, the head of that Ganesha disappeared. Seeing that, there was a great cry of distress. After a muhurta passed, the gods and sages saw him again, endowed with his head and beautiful in all his limbs. Then, all the joyful gods praised the Elephant-faced one. Shani, with a bowed heart, praised the Lord of the Ganas.

Shani said: Salutations to the Lord of the Ganas, salutations to the Lord of Prosperity. Salutations to the sustainer of the universe, the one who is to be worshipped first of all. You exist as the mother of all, the one who bestows prosperity. You are the father and the giver of prosperity, existing as the essence of truth. Forgive this offense of mine, O Lord of the Ganas. I am your son, O great one, I pray to you, O Elephant-faced one. How can I praise you, O Heramba, who are the eternal form of Yoga? You cannot be praised by the Vedas and the sages, nor can I. Still, the great ones praise you based on the authority of their intellect; therefore, I, O great King, praise you with devotion. Salutations to the Lord of Intellect, salutations to the Lord of Success. Salutations to the Lord of the Vedas, salutations to you, O Heramba. Salutations to you who are with form when possessing qualities, and in the form of an elephant when beyond qualities. Salutations to you who are the form of peace, the union of both. Because you save one from the hell named 'Put', you are called 'Putra' (son). Salutations to you, the son of Shiva, salutations to Ganesha. Siddhi and Maya are on your left side, and intellect is on your right. Salutations to you, the One-Tusked, who are the non-dual union of both.

Having said this, he was filled with joy and his hair stood on end. He bowed down with his whole body, overwhelmed with devotion. The Lord of the Ganas raised him and said to Shani: Ask for a boon, I will grant whatever is in your heart. This hymn composed by you will be pleasing to me. Any mortal who reads or hears it will attain everything. Then the son of the Sun said to the Lord of the Ganas: Grant me your devotion and release me from the curse, O Lord. Then the King of the Ganas said to him, O Lord of the Asuras: There is no doubt that my devotion will be firm in you, O son of the Sun. Whomever you look upon with your cruel gaze, O Shani, may they attain destruction; but you shall always be free from the curse.

Then the son of the Sun went to Vishnu and, having bowed to him, said: Lord, give me the auspicious worship of Ganesha. Then he (Vishnu) told him the excellent armor (Kavacha) and other things, gave him the essence of the Gita, and the single-syllable mantra of the Lord. Then Shani, having bowed, went to his home and practiced yoga, becoming a devotee of Ganesha.

After some time had passed, Ganesha, who is fond of his devotees, said to Shankara with a heart full of emotion: O Father, O Lord of gods, O Sadashiva, why do you live in the forest? Tell me the reason for this. Shankara, being pleased and filled with devotion, said to him: The entire world has been conquered by the evil-minded Lord of the Asuras. He has become the master of the universe through the power of boons. Having stolen all our prosperity, he has become supreme himself.

Gritsamada said: Hearing the words of Shiva, the Elephant-faced one became angry and spoke these beneficial words. Ganesha said: When you have me as your son, what is difficult for you? I will kill the great demon; watch my prowess, O Shiva. Having said this to Mahadeva, the Elephant-faced one, prepared and mounted on his mouse, went to the leader of the demons. The gods and sages, full of devotion, followed him. Having reached the outskirts of the city, he stayed there. He sent Vishnu, the best of the gods, as an ambassador. He went and tried to enlighten the evil-minded one with logic.

Vishnu said: Ganesha, who dwells in Svananda and is the Lord of the Vedas, was requested by Shiva through penance with the desire for a son. He has become his son and has come as the Lord of Prosperity. He has sent me to enlighten you, O evil-minded one. Abandoning enmity towards the gods, live in the netherworld. Let the people, devoted to their own duties, be free from distress.

Having been told this, the great demon said to Janardana: You have come as an ambassador; otherwise, I would kill you with my power. Who is that Lord of Svananda? He has become a supporter of the gods. I will kill him quickly; you shall see, O Elephant-faced one. Then Vishnu came and told Ganesha the harsh account of that evil-minded one. Hearing this, the remover of obstacles became angry. He mounted his mouse and, holding weapons, went to kill him. All the gods also became prepared, O Lord of the Asuras.

Chapter 65 : The Quest for Wealth

Ghritsamada said: Then, the demon king, surrounded by his army, set out. Mounted on a chariot, the radiant demon appeared, armed with various weapons. The wicked and foolish demon, upon seeing the Lord of the Ganas (Ganesha), became extremely enraged and ordered his army to destroy the gods.

Then, the demons and the gods fought a fierce and terrible battle. Driven by great anger, they fought incessantly for seven days. The army of the gods was broken and fled in all ten directions. Terrified, Lord Shankara (Shiva) fought with great fury. The demon Durmati waged a wondrous battle; many gods were wounded, fell unconscious, or were severed by his arrows. Seeing Shambhu (Shiva), Vishnu, Indra, and all the others lying unconscious, the Lord of the Ganas became filled with rage.

He arrived for battle mounted on his mouse. Seeing him, Durmati, his eyes red with anger, spoke: "Why have you come to fight like a fool? I will kill you with a single arrow; behold my prowess, you wretch! You dare to fight one who has conquered the entire universe? You are surely blinded by ignorance; go away, I shall not kill you."

To the fierce demon king who spoke thus, the powerful Lord of Prosperity (Ganesha), smiling, replied: "Do not be proud, O wicked demon king, listen to my words. I will surely kill you; behold my great prowess."

Even as he spoke, Durmati suddenly rushed forward. Seizing Ganesha’s tusk, he pulled it with great anger. The demon, possessing immense strength, broke off the left tusk. Ganesha caught the tusk as it fell to the ground and, piercing the demon’s head with it, killed him. Then, the Lord of Prosperity revived the gods.

Following this, all the demon hosts fled in all ten directions, terrified, and descended into the netherworld (Patala). The gods and sages were filled with joy and worshipped the Lord of Prosperity, praising him again and again.

The divine sages said: "Victory to you, O Lord of the Ganas! Victory to you, the remover of obstacles, the eternal one! Victory to you, O Lord of Prosperity, O Dhundhi, the best of all lords! Victory to you, the foundation of infinite virtues, the bestower of success, O Lord! Victory to you, the embodiment of Yoga, the giver of peace! Victory to you, O knower of Brahman, the all-knowing, the benefactor of all! Victory to you, who dwells in self-bliss, the best of those who know the Vedas! Victory to you, the knower of Vedanta, the cause of Vedanta! Victory to you, the wise one, the beloved of all gods! Victory to you, who plays in the realm of Maya, the unmanifest, the elephant-faced! Victory to you, the pot-bellied witness, the destroyer of Durmati! Victory to you, who holds the tusk in your hand, the bearer of the single tusk! Victory to you, who dwells in the hearts of Yogis, worshipped by the Brahmins! Victory to you, the form of action and penance, the giver of knowledge! Victory to you, the immeasurable, the great-souled, the fulfiller of desires! Victory to you, O blissful Ganesha, lover of the noose and goad! Victory to you, the bearer of the axe, the purifier! Victory to you, the protector of devotees, the beloved of devotees! Victory to you, the Lord of devotees, the Lord of obstacles, the great-bellied one! Salutations to the leader of the Ganas, salutations to the embodiment of all, salutations to the liberator from worldly existence, salutations to the giver of supreme happiness!"

Having praised the Lord of the Ganas thus, the great-minded sages bowed down. Rising, they all danced with joy. Seeing their great celebration, the great Lord, the Lord of Prosperity, who is fond of his devotees, spoke to them with a pleased heart.

The Lord of Prosperity said: "Whoever reads or hears this hymn composed by you daily, will always be victorious. Through reading or hearing this, a human will conquer all, and in the end, will undoubtedly attain union with me."

Having said this, the Lord of Prosperity, the leader of the Ganas, vanished. The gods and sages were filled with wonder. They returned to their respective realms, happy and content, and continued to rejoice in their own domains. The sages performed their duties, and the people, devoted to their respective castes and stages of life, worshipped the elephant-faced Lord according to their own dharma. Shiva, united with Shakti and holding Ganesha in his heart, worshipped him with devotion.

They created an image of Ganesha and installed it with reverence. A great festival was held there on the full moon of Vaishakha. There, Shiva attained success, and the divine sages established a supreme sacred site named Siddhashrama. It was there that the gods and the great sages, including Sanaka and others, attained success and became followers of Ganesha.

I shall now narrate to you an ancient story, a dialogue between Agastya and Brahma from long ago. Once, the sage Agastya was engaged in penance in the forest, and his wife, Lopamudra, was devoted to serving him. She, a princess of noble birth, was dedicated to her husband, but she became frail due to her eagerness to serve him. Seeing her body covered in dust from the heat and her hair matted, the great sage was pleased. He told her to ask for a boon. She asked him to provide her with the luxuries befitting a princess.

Desiring wealth for the sake of royal luxuries, the sage went to King Dilipa, the emperor, to beg for it. Dilipa asked him, "Tell me the amount of wealth you require, and I will give it to you with devotion, O best of sages." Hearing the king's words, the sage replied, "Give me the wealth that remains after your expenses." The king then asked him to specify the amount, as he had much wealth. The sage, filled with wonder, said, "I will not take your wealth, for you are surely a pauper. If I take your wealth, O King, all your subjects will be oppressed. Therefore, O King, come with me to where great wealth is stored."

The king then told the sage the story of the demon Vatapi, who possessed great wealth and was invincible to the gods. He would kill Brahmins daily by turning his brother Ilvala into a sacrificial animal for rituals. After the Brahmins ate the meat, the demon would call out to his brother, who would then burst forth from the stomach, killing the Brahmin. "Therefore, O great Yogi, go and kill Vatapi and take the wealth, along with his brother," the king said. Hearing Dilipa's words, the great sage, accompanied by kings and Brahmins, decided to go there.

Chapter 66 : The Description of the Curse of Krishna and Radha

Gautama said: Agastya, accompanied by kings and Brahmins, went to the beautiful and pleasant abode of Vatapi, filled with anger. Vatapi, knowing he had arrived, went to him joyfully, bowed down, and spoke with humility. Vatapi said: "Welcome, O Lord! My fortune is fulfilled today. I see a sage here along with Brahmins and kings. Today, a Shradh ceremony for my father is taking place here. I invite you all, O great one, to join."

The demon was delighted when the sage agreed. The demon king, knowing the art of transformation, turned his brother Ilvala into a sacrificial animal, slaughtered him, and cooked the meat according to the scriptures for the Brahmins. When Agastya arrived with his followers for the meal, Vatapi worshipped him and seated him. The sage pressed the ground with his toe, creating a pit, and all the food made from the demon's body was consumed into it. The demon, astonished, said nothing. He fed the others with other food. After Agastya finished eating all the food made from Ilvala, he chanted a Vedic mantra over his stomach. By the power of the mantra, the demon was digested. A fierce wind escaped from the sage, causing a loud sound that astonished everyone.

The sage then stood up to wash his mouth. After washing his hands, he stood there. Vatapi then called out to his brother, but the great sage said to the demon, "He is dead, digested by my mantra in my stomach. Now I will destroy you, you wicked killer of Brahmins." Hearing this, Vatapi fled in fear. The sage then established the kings and Brahmins there and went after the demon himself. Seeing Agastya behind him, the demon took refuge in the ocean. Vatapi hid within the waters. The sage, filled with anger, said to the ocean, "Give me the demon, O Ocean, or I shall curse you."

The ocean, filled with fear, said to Agastya, "O great one, it is my nature, so I cannot give him up. The demons have come to me for refuge out of fear of the gods. I have not given up other mountain-kings either, O sage. Therefore, I pray to you, show mercy. Leave the demon, O best of sages, and go; I bow to you." Agastya was agitated and thought, "This ocean is very arrogant; I shall humble his pride." He had never seen such arrogance. The sage then went to the Creator for refuge. He bowed to the Lord of the world and asked the teacher of the universe.

Agastya said: "Lord, the Brahmin-killer Vatapi is hidden in the ocean. I wish to dry up the ocean because of his arrogance. Tell me the way, O Brahma, and I will strive to do it. You are all-knowing and especially the grandfather of all." Hearing his words, the Lord of the world spoke to the great sage with a joyful heart. Brahma said: "Listen, my son, this ocean is very arrogant. He has certainly protected the demons who act against Dharma. Vatapi should not be protected, yet he has been honored by the ocean. Therefore, to dry it up, take refuge in Ganapati."

Hearing this, he said to the Grandfather, "Why do you praise Ganesha, abandoning the gods, O Creator?" The Creator, Prajapati, then said to the chief of sages: "Listen, my son, to the story that destroys sins. Ganesha is the creator and remover of obstacles, there is no doubt. That is why he is known as Vighneshwara in all the worlds. Obstacles are said to be of the nature of existence, and their master is Ganesha. He is the complete holder of all existence, therefore I tell you of him. Also, listen, my son, he is called Vinayaka because he is served by all leaders for their respective tasks with respect. Without him, one becomes independent. The entire universe is created by a small part of his essence, O great sage. All powers are parts of his essence. We are not capable of moving the ocean, my son. I will describe an ancient history to you; by hearing it, your doubts will surely be destroyed."

"In Goloka, Krishna, accompanied by Radha and the residents of Goloka, lived there, worshipped by all and powerful. He became the destroyer of everyone's pride by his own radiance. Being our protector, he was naturally free of obstacles. Similarly, Radha also forcibly removed the pride of the goddesses, became worthy of worship herself, and lived free of obstacles. Due to mutual conflict, Shiva and others, afflicted by obstacles from Tripura and others, went to the supreme refuge. Pride entered the heart of Krishna, who had no other refuge. He became constantly filled with pride and was very strange. He thought, 'I am Brahma, there is no doubt. This whole world is based on my pride; there is no one higher than me. By my grace alone, the gods, led by Shambhu, are capable of their actions; there is no one higher than me.'

"Thus, filled with pride in his mind, Radha also became filled with pride, O best of sages. Then, the remover of obstacles created a great wonder to grant his own devotion and for the sake of peace. Once, while enjoying with Radha in the forest, he was filled with desire. There, a maiden named Viraja, who gave him pleasure, reminded him of her at that moment. Krishna left Radha, who was below, and went to Viraja, becoming absorbed in play with her. Radha, angered by the interruption of her pleasure, said to the nearby Gopis, 'Where is that arrogant Krishna, the leader of the lustful? Tell me, goddesses, I shall abandon him as my husband in my heart.'

"Then a Gopi said to her with humility, 'Radha, I saw him near the ashram of Viraja.' Then she, with eyes reddened by anger, went on a chariot to the vicinity of Viraja's ashram, surrounded by Gopis. Knowing she had arrived, Krishna disappeared out of fear. Viraja became water, filled with fear. Radha did not see the Lord of the gods with the woman there. She returned to her palace, filled with anger. Then Krishna appeared and played with her, who had become water, filled with great joy. After making Viraja satisfied, he went to Radha. Seeing him arrive at the door, she said: 'Why have you come to me, you lustful one? Go to that Viraja, O God; I will never touch you. This world was created by my essence long ago. Leave my world and go wherever you like.'

"After rebuking her husband with various such words, Radhika, filled with anger, cursed him, saying, 'Fall into the mortal world!' Then, knowing he had been cursed, Shridama, the leader of the Gopis and a powerful friend of Krishna, rebuked her in anger. Radhika, filled with extreme anger, cursed him, 'Become a demon!' O best of sages, he also became filled with anger. He cursed Radha, 'Become a woman among mortals immediately, and for a hundred years, be filled with the separation from Krishna.'"

Chapter 67 : The Description of Radha and Krishna Attaining Goloka

Brahma said: Having cursed each other, they became filled with sorrow. Their pride vanished, and they began to reflect with reverence. I am the Lord of Goloka and the master of all, there is no doubt. Why have these two, who are embodiments of Prakriti, committed an offense against me? I am Krishna, the Lord of all gods, yet a hindrance has arisen for me. Therefore, Ganesha, the Lord of Obstacles, must be the one causing all this. He is the foundation of this world, and there is no doubt that this world rests upon him. I shall take refuge in him along with my consort, for he is the embodiment of Brahman.

Having reflected thus, the Lord of gods, Krishna, along with his consort and friends, descended to the mortal world, O great sage. He was born in Mathura to Devaki and Vasudeva in the Yadu dynasty, and he became the source of their happiness. Radha, the daughter of Vrishabhanu, was born in Gokula, and the great demon Shridama was born as Shankhachuda. Then, due to the fear of Kamsa, Krishna went to Gokula, where he was united with Radha.

There, they naturally began to worship the Lord of Obstacles with great devotion. They engaged in supreme penance using the sixteen-syllable mantra. They performed the purashcharana with great reverence and remained constantly absorbed in meditation. Abandoning other austerities, they focused specifically on meditation within their hearts, for meditation is considered superior to penance in the scriptures. Krishna remained there for eleven years, and then he went to Mathura with Akrura.

Radha and Krishna spent a hundred years in separation, suffering great pain and meditating upon the Lord of Ganas. Then, their union, which bestows peace, was achieved. Being satisfied by this, they became devotees of Ganesha. After a hundred years had passed, O brahmin, they went with their followers to Badarikashrama on the full moon day of Vaishakha. There, gods like Shambhu, accompanied by their consorts, and sages also arrived with joy to participate in the pilgrimage of the Lord of Ganas.

Great serpents like Shesha, kings, and all the people of the three worlds went there, O great sage. Yogis like Kapila and I myself also gathered there. A great annual festival is always held there by everyone. After worshipping him, they all fasted and broke their fast on the first day of the lunar fortnight, O great sage. There, at night, Radha and Krishna met the Lord of Ganas, and with deep devotion, they meditated upon him and chanted the supreme mantra.

Then, the Lord of Ganesha revealed his supreme form to them—the four-armed form, marked with the elephant face and other signs. Seeing him, they suddenly rose and bowed at his lotus feet. With hair standing on end and eyes filled with tears, they praised the Lord of Ganas.

Radha and Krishna said: Salutations to you, O Lord of prosperity, salutations to the son of Shankara. You are the divine Brahman, the bestower of all accomplishments. Salutations to you, who dwell in your own bliss, the son of Brahma. Salutations again and again to Ganesha, the Lord of Siddhi and Buddhi. Salutations to Heramba, salutations to the embodiment of Yoga. Salutations to the beginning of all, the Lord of all, the all-knowing one. Salutations to you who are without attributes, and salutations to you who possess attributes. Salutations to the elephant-faced one, who is non-dual. Salutations to you, the embodiment of peace, the peaceful one, the bestower of peace, O great-bellied one. You whose vehicle is the best of mice, you who are dear to the Ganas. Salutations to you, who have infinite forms, the protector of devotees, the God who is fond of devotion and controlled by devotion. Salutations to you, the four-armed one, who wears a serpent as a sacred thread, the one with winnowing-basket ears, the brave one, the wielder of the axe. Salutations to the son of Vishnu, the father and mother of all. Salutations to Vinayaka, the son of the brahmins. Salutations to the destroyer of the pride of all, the bestower of happiness to all, O Lambodara, Lord of obstacles, Lord of Brahma.

How can we praise you, O Lord of Ganas, when even the Vedas, Shambhu, Shuka, and others have been rendered speechless?
