We were filled with pride and thought ourselves to be the embodiment of Brahman in vain, O Lord. It was through your obstacle that our pride was shattered. You are the creator of obstacles for the virtuous to grant them the peace of Yoga, and you are the destroyer of the wicked; therefore, we bow to you. Because of the obstacle, O Dhundhi, we have attained your vision through the practice of Yoga; this was done well. We see you as the Chintamani in our hearts, O Lord. Therefore, that obstacle became auspicious for us, O elephant-faced one. Now, O Lord of obstacles, grant us firm devotion to your lotus feet, so that we may never be filled with pride again. Having said this, they danced with joy, bowing to him and calling themselves blessed for having had the vision of the Lord of Ganas.

Brahma said: Then the Lord of Ganas, seeing Radha and Janardana filled with the joy of divine love, spoke to them with a heart full of devotion.

The Lord of Prosperity said: O Radha, O Krishna, listen to my words, you who are the Lords of the world. Do not doubt my devotion. I manifest whenever I am remembered in times of distress. Whatever happiness you desire will be fulfilled. Those who read or listen to this hymn composed by you, O Lords of gods, will attain all accomplishments. It will always increase my love and fulfill all desires. In the end, it will grant them their own bliss, O Krishna, there is no doubt.

Having said this, Ganesha, the Lord of Brahma, disappeared. They remained there at night, seeing him especially within their hearts. Then, Radha and Hari returned to their own Goloka and became endowed with infinite majesty, O great sage. Therefore, you too should go to the Lord of Ganas, free from pride, and take refuge in him. O Agastya, that Lord will fulfill your desires. The entire universe rests upon his foundation, O best of sages. This Lord of Ganas plays everywhere according to his own will.

Chapter 68 : the Story of Pushtipati

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gritsamada said: Having heard this wonderful story, Agastya, bowing down with devotion, spoke to Brahma, the knower of all.

Agastya said: O Lord, knower of all truths, you have explained everything clearly; I am extremely satisfied, O best of the knowers of Brahman. How can one attain the knowledge of such a Ganesha? Please tell me this, O ocean of compassion, so that I may be free from sorrow.

Then the Grandsire (Brahma) spoke these words to him, which were full of knowledge and devotion to Ganesha, and were most wonderful.

Brahma said: Listen, my son, I will tell you the great knowledge related to Yoga, by the sincere practice of which you will become a devotee of Ganesha. Know that Brahman, which is the essence of both the body and the soul, is the cause of its own manifestation; know it especially as the form of pure consciousness in every way. The Sankhya philosophy describes Brahman as the cause of manifestation beyond; by the nature of being devoid of consciousness, one attains the state of self-happiness. In the union of both, know that which is beyond, which is self-perceived, and which is the cause of all connections through the practice of Yoga.

There are five divisions of this; listen to them, O wise one. Know that the form of division is unreal and represents power (Shakti). Truth is said to be effulgent like the sun, and within it, know that the soul is always established, devoid of divisions. The one who causes the union of these two is Vishnu, who represents bliss; know that Brahman, which is the essence of all, is the supreme giver of happiness. Shankara is described as the one who is not the creator of these three; know that the unmanifest Brahman is devoid of delusion. The three-fold is full of delusion, while the fourth (Turiya) is devoid of it; through the union of these, self-bliss is attained.

There is no other holder of union beyond the self-perceived Brahman. This self-bliss is everywhere and becomes complete in self-bliss. Brahman, which is beyond union, is considered beyond self-bliss; it is attained by the state of being devoid of union, there is no doubt in this. When the union and non-union are destroyed, O great sage, that very state is called Yoga, and it is attained in the form of peace. Know that he is the Lord of Ganas, the leader of Brahman. The letter 'Ga' is said to be union, and the letter 'Na' is said to be Yoga. He is the master of both in the Vedas, O wise one; worship him with effort, and you will attain happiness.

Now, you are in great distress; therefore, meditate on Gajanan in your heart on the fourth day of the dark fortnight. By this, you will remove the pride of the ocean, which is a great wonder. Go to the forest of penance, my son, and I will perform the task.

Gritsamada said: Having bowed to Brahma, he went to his forest of penance. The best of sages, pleasing the Lord of obstacles with the six-syllabled mantra, remained absorbed in meditation and performed excellent penance. He observed the fourth day, which removes distress. After three months, Ganesha was pleased with his vow, meditation, and devotional penance. On the fifth day of the dark fortnight, a supreme voice from the sky occurred. Agastya heard that voice which bestows happiness.

The voice said: Go, O sage, and drink the ocean, remembering the Lord of obstacles. Kill Vatapi, who is there, with the power of your penance.

Then, filled with joy, he went to the ocean, and meditating on Ganesha in his mind, he drank it with anger. Having dried up all the water, the best of sages stood there and burned Vatapi, the killer of Brahmins, with his brilliance. Then all the gods praised the great sage, and the gods killed all the demons present there with their weapons. Then, bringing the Ganga and filling the ocean with its waters, the gods, along with Bhagiratha, became free from distress.

Then Agastya, the lord, came to the city of Vatapi, took plenty of wealth from the kings, and went to his own place. He pleased Lopamudra with effort. Afterwards, established in penance, he chanted the supreme mantra. Having conquered the five-fold mind through the practice of Yoga, the great sage attained peace in the Lord of Ganas. Again, with devotion, he came to Ganesha, who was on his peacock, and worshipped him daily in that region with joy.

Thus, the glory of Pushtipati has been described to you. O demon, listen briefly to the story of Chintamani. Whoever listens to or reads this wonderful story of Pushtipati, everything will surely come into his hands.

Thus ends the sixty-eighth chapter named the Story of Pushtipati in the second part of the Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 69 : The Description of Devahuti and Kardama Attaining Peace

Gritsamada said: Svayambhuva Manu, filled with great joy, gave his daughter Devahuti in marriage to Kardama. From their union, the Lord Himself, in the form of Kapila—the manifestation of Vishnu—was born. He was the one who illuminated the knowledge of the Absolute Truth, having been worshipped by the penance of Kardama.

The great sage Kardama worshipped his son, who was endowed with great majesty. Brahma and other deities arrived at the sage’s hermitage to behold Him. Filled with immense joy, Kardama performed the sacred thread ceremony and diligently taught his son the Vedas along with their auxiliary branches. Kapila, by merely hearing them, grasped everything his teacher spoke and, in a very short time, became master of all knowledge.

One day, Kardama, filled with devotion and accompanied by his wife, bowed to his highly fortunate son and asked with humility: "O son, you are the direct form of Lord Vishnu, pure and omniscient. O mighty-armed one, please impart that knowledge which can liberate me from the ocean of worldly existence."

Being thus asked, the great soul Kapila spoke to him about the essence of all knowledge—the Ganesha-related wisdom that grants liberation. Kapila said: "O father, listen to that peace-granting knowledge which I have realized, the knowledge that bestows the state of Yoga. Know that everything is composed of Maya, consisting of groups of five. There are three types of illusions that manifest in various forms. The fourth is that which sustains these illusions, and the union of these without distinction is considered the union with Brahman."

Kapila continued to explain the various pentads (groups of five) that constitute the universe: the five elements (earth, water, fire, air, and ether), the five organs of action, the five organs of perception, the five subtle elements, the five types of bodies, the five cosmic regions, the five social orders (varnas), the five stages of life (ashramas), the five modes of birth, the five colors, the five types of ego-identities, and the five divine powers. He explained that all these are manifestations of the Supreme, and through the worship of Ganesha—the Lord of the Ganas—one attains the supreme peace of Yoga.

Kapila taught that Ganesha, the Lord of the Ganas, is the primordial being, the support of all, and the one who remains even after the dissolution of the universe. He is the 'Jyeshtharaja' (the eldest king) mentioned in the Vedas. By worshipping Him with the single-syllable mantra (Om), one attains the peace-granting Yoga.

Having said this, the great Yogi Kapila, the sustainer of truth and the eternal Vishnu, became silent. Kardama, having bowed to his son, immediately went to the forest to perform supreme penance. Pleased by his devotion and the chanting of the single-syllable mantra, Ganesha, who is affectionate to His devotees, granted him the peace of Yoga. Kardama, now a great Yogi, wandered the earth in a state of supreme peace.

Devahuti also practiced the supreme Yoga in the presence of Kapila, keeping Ganesha in her heart. Gradually, the great chaste lady attained peace, abandoned all mental fluctuations, and became absorbed in the form of the Chintamani (the wish-fulfilling jewel/Ganesha). Seeing her attain this perfection, Kapila departed and went to the southern bank of the Ganges to worship the Lord of the Ganas.

Chapter 70 : The Stealing of the Chintamani

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gritsamada said: Once, the Lord of the Devas (Indra), accompanied by all the celestials, went to the hermitage of Kapila. He bowed down to the sage with folded hands. Seeing Indra arrive in the afternoon, the great sage, who was a master of Yoga, joyfully invited him and his retinue, who were hungry. Meditating upon Lord Ganesha, who is endowed with all accomplishments, the sage invoked the supreme nectar. By the power of the gem, he provided a feast for them all. Satisfied by the consumption of the divine nectar, the King of the Gods, having brought it through the wind, gave a supreme gem to the sage. This gem is known as the Chintamani, the chief ornament of Ganesha. By wearing it, the Yogi considered himself fulfilled.

Kapila thought to himself, "I am not different from Ganesha, and I am devoted to the path of Yoga. Therefore, this great gem has been inspired by Lord Ganesha himself." He tied it around his neck, just as Ganapati wears it. Seeing this, the jubilant gods returned to their respective abodes, and Kapila remained in his hermitage, adorned with the gem.

Prahlada asked: O Yogi, tell me, how did Indra obtain this gem from Ganesha, who dwells in Svananda and is the Lord of Brahman?

Gritsamada said: Listen, Prahlada, to the greatness and origin of the Chintamani; I shall tell you with affection, for it destroys all sins. Once, Lord Vishnu performed severe penance and attained peace through the six-syllable mantra. To test his devotion, the Lord gave him the Chintamani, which grants all desired objects. However, Vishnu, considering himself a devotee of Ganesha, thought, "I am not worthy to wear this gem, which is the ornament of the Lord of the Ganas. It should be worshipped, not worn by the servants of the Divine."

Having attained peace through Yoga, Vishnu held Ganesha in his heart, thinking, "The Lord is not different from me, yet I am naturally His servant." After a long time, Indra performed penance and pleased the Lord of the Ganas through the single-syllable mantra. Being constantly absorbed in meditation and Yoga, Indra eventually attained the status of a Gana. Having attained peace and becoming one with Ganesha, he returned to his home and ruled the heavens.

Meanwhile, Vishnu heard a bodiless voice in the house of the Daitya: "O wise Vishnu, give the Chintamani to Indra, for I give it to my devotee who possesses the peace of Yoga." Hearing this celestial voice, Vishnu went and gave the wish-fulfilling gem to Mahendra in Amaravati. Indra worshipped the gem with the attitude of a servant, but he did not attain the state of Yoga. Seeing the great sage Kapila, who possessed the peace of Yoga, Mahendra gave the gem to him with a devoted heart, thinking, "This master of Yoga is truly worthy of wearing the gem; we, being householders, are not."

Thus, know the sequence of how the Chintamani was acquired. Kapila wore it around his neck daily, just like the Lord of the Ganas. After some time, a Daitya named Ganasura, who was fond of hunting, came to Kapila’s hermitage. Leaving his army outside, he approached the sage alone and bowed down with folded hands. Seeing him humble, Kapila asked, "Who are you, and why have you come here?"

Ganasura said: "I am known as Ganasura. While hunting, I arrived here by fate. I was hungry and my mount was tired, but I was satisfied by the hospitality found in your hermitage. Having eaten fruits and drunk water with my soldiers, I have come to see you; my army is waiting by the lake. I have become purified by your sight, O best of sages. Give me permission, and I shall return to my city with my army."

Hearing this, the satisfied Kapila said, "O best of kings, eat something that I provide. You came to my hermitage hungry, so I request you to stay as a guest." The powerful Ganasura agreed and was well-honored by Kapila. The sage’s disciples brought the army, and the sage worshipped the Daitya king along with his troops. He worshipped the Chintamani and prayed for food, asking the great gem to provide whatever everyone desired. By the grace of the gem, food with six flavors appeared in all the vessels and was served with respect. The Daitya king and his army were astonished by the taste.

The Daitya king bowed to the sage and begged for the great gem, but the sage did not give him the ornament of Ganesha. Then, the Daitya insulted the sage, snatched the gem from his hand, and left, feeling full of joy. The sage, however, became agitated and, desiring to retrieve the gem, thought of the destruction of the wicked Daitya. O Prahlada, see how the wicked, even when they appear virtuous, act according to their nature. Therefore, a wicked and arrogant person should never be trusted, for association with the wicked leads to the loss of wisdom.

Thus ends the seventieth chapter entitled "The Stealing of the Chintamani" in the second section of the glorious Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 71 : The Bestowal of Boons upon Kapila

Prahlad said: Who is this demon Ganasura by whom the sage was insulted? Kapila is capable and radiant like the fire of universal destruction. His angry gaze burned the sons of Sagara to ashes. How could he, after insulting such a person, return to his city alive?

Gritsamada said: Listen, Prahlad, with attention to the deeds of Ganasura. I shall tell you everything, which destroys sins and is rooted in the life of Ganesha. There was a royal sage named Abhijit who had no children. Desiring a son, the king left his kingdom and went to the forest. While wandering, he saw the great yogi Vaishampayana. Bowing to him with his wife, he asked with humility: "O omniscient one, tell me the way to obtain a son, salutations to you." The sage gave him the king of mantras and instructed him in the method of the Chaturmasya vow. After instructing the king, the sage went to the holy place of Prabhasa.

Abhijit, having bowed to the sage, went with his wife to that great holy place and performed the rituals as prescribed. There, Brahma was performing penance, accompanied by Saraswati. Once, he went with his daughter to bathe. Seeing Saraswati in the water, wearing only one garment, in the bloom of youth, Brahma was astonished. Suddenly, struck by the arrows of desire in the water, his mind became restless, taking her for a woman. Afflicted by intense desire, the Creator’s semen fell into the water. Brahma then went to Satyaloka with a pure mind. On that day, the king’s ritual concluded.

The king went to the holy place with his wife for the final bath. His thirsty wife drank the water. By divine providence, that semen of Brahma, mixed with the water, entered her womb. The king, having completed the mantra rituals with devotion, returned to his city and ruled excellently. In time, the queen gave birth to a son, radiant, powerful, and adorned with auspicious marks. The king performed the birth rites through Brahmins and, after the thread ceremony, focused his mind on the study of the Vedas.

In a short time, the son, named Gana, became a repository of Vedic knowledge and great strength, and then went to the forest himself. On the way, Shukra appeared and gave him the five-syllable mantra. He performed the initiation of Shiva with devotion. Meditating on Shiva, he performed severe penance, subsisting only on air. After ten thousand years, Sadashiva, pleased by his devotion, appeared. Seeing Shankara, Gana worshipped him with devotion and praised him with various hymns. The Lord asked him to seek a boon. Gana said: "O Sadashiva, grant me the kingdom of the three worlds, free from the fear of the three qualities, and endowed with health." Saying "So be it," Mahadeva returned to his abode, and the demon Gana went home, overjoyed.

He brought joy to his friends and parents. Then, with his army and his own radiance, he conquered the earth. His father, going to the forest, anointed him as king. Gana ruled in his place. Then, joined by demons from various directions, he conquered the entire earth along with its islands. With his strength, he conquered the netherworld from the Nagas. After a fierce battle, Gana went to heaven. Having conquered heaven along with Indra, he then conquered Brahma, Vishnu, and Shiva in order, with his radiance. He made them pay tribute and placed them in their respective positions, leaving a demon in charge, and returned to earth.

Having thus conquered the three worlds, he stole the Chintamani jewel from Kapila. Kapila was a devotee of the Lord of Ganas and performed severe penance for the great Ganesha. After one year, the Lord, riding a mouse and accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi, appeared and enlightened the great sage. Kapila, worshipping the Lord who had arrived, praised him with tearful eyes and a voice choked with emotion.

Kapila said: Salutations to the King of Obstacles, salutations to the Remover of Obstacles. Salutations to the one who creates obstacles for the non-devotees. Salutations to you, who are the sky for the elements, the mind for the gods, the intellect for the senses, and the three-fold nature. Salutations to you, who are the seed of bodies, the 'I-am' of living beings, and the knowledge of their non-duality. Salutations to you, who are the Sankhya for the disembodied, the Self for the yogis, and the fifth for the four. Salutations to you, who are the power behind names and forms, the sun of the souls, and Heramba. Salutations to you, who are the form of Mahavishnu for the blissful, the 'Neti' for those who seek, and Shankara for all. Salutations to you, O Lambodara, who are the Karma-yoga for actions and Jnana-yoga for the wise, and the equal form for those who are equal. Salutations to you, O Lord of Ganas, who are the natural state of the independent, and salutations to your own bliss in their non-dual state. Salutations to you, who are the non-union for those of formless nature, and salutations to you, O Ganesha, who are the form of yoga for all yogas. You are the giver of peace and the embodiment of peace; what can I praise? I simply bow to you.

The Lord of Ganas, filled with great joy, spoke to his best devotee, delighting the great sage. Ganesha said: This hymn composed by you will grant yoga and will undoubtedly bestow righteousness, wealth, desire, and liberation. Ask for a boon; I will grant it, as you are bound by devotion. There is no one like you in the manifestation of true knowledge. Hearing these words, Kapila said: O Lord of Obstacles, grant me your unwavering devotion. The demon Gana has stolen your jewel and taken it away. O Lord, destroy him and bring back the Chintamani. Whenever I remember you, show yourself to me. Grant me this complete boon, O Lord, salutations to you.

Gritsamada said: Hearing these words, the elephant-faced Lord was pleased and spoke to the great sage with special affection. Ganesha said: O Vishnu, whatever you have requested will come to pass. I will become your son to slay Ganasura. Having said this, the glorious Dhundhiraja disappeared. The sage, meditating on him in his heart, remained in his hermitage.

Thus ends the seventy-first chapter of the second section of the Mudgala Purana, regarding the story of Ekadanta and the bestowal of boons upon Kapila.

Chapter 72 : The Killing of Ganasura

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gritamada said: Shortly after this, the sage, devoted to fire rituals, performed his oblations and became absorbed in meditation on the Great Lord Ganesha. From the midst of the sacrificial fire pit, Ganesha emerged before him. He was four-armed, red in complexion, and radiated supreme splendor. He was accompanied by Siddhi and Buddhi, mounted on a lion, holding a battle-axe, possessing one tusk, large eyes, and adorned with a trunk. Seeing this supreme form, whose feet jingled as he walked, Kapila stood up and bowed with a heart filled with devotion. After worshipping him, he praised him with the Atharvashirsha hymn. Pleased by that prayer, the Elephant-faced Lord spoke.

Ganesha said: O Sage, I have been born as your son to slay the demon Gana. I shall kill the great demon and retrieve the great jewel.

The great sage, having bowed down, replied: My birth, my parents, my penance, and my hermitage are all blessed. By the sight of your feet, all my knowledge and vows have been fulfilled. Since you have come in the form of my son, there is no doubt that I shall be liberated.

Thereafter, the sage practiced great devotion toward him daily. Upon learning the secret of that devotion, he became sorrowful. He began to reproach himself in his mind, thinking: Alas, I am in human form, attached to the path of peace. Due to the pride of my knowledge, I wore the Chintamani jewel around my neck, lacking the devotion of a true devotee of Ganesha. One who seeks to worship the Lord should not consider himself equal to Him. Ganesha, known as Brahmanaspati, is the sole sovereign. In his body, the fullness of existence resides for my sake; therefore, a great soul worships Him with the body of a yogi. I obtained the Chintamani, but instead of placing it in worship, I wore it around my neck out of delusion, and thus, the jewel was lost.

Through the greatness of devotion to the Lord, a devotee receives ornaments, garments, and vehicles, which should be worshipped without doubt. One should especially wear the remnants of His offerings, and the food offered to Him should be consumed with devotion and respect. A servant should never consider himself equal to the Lord; a wise devotee should always serve with a sense of awe. My Chintamani, which was in my association, is now in the hands of the demon, and he is surely tormenting it. Therefore, I pray to Ganesha for his destruction, so that by killing the demon, the Chintamani may be easily recovered.

Having reflected thus, the great yogi said to the One-Tusked Lord: O Master, kill Gana quickly and bring back the jewel, O Remover of Obstacles. Hearing the words of Kapila, the One-Tusked Lord, being pleased, said: I shall certainly do what is dear to you, O father.

That night, the One-Tusked Lord gave a dream to Gana. In the dream, Gana became confused and pondered to himself: Who is this being shown in my dream—mounted on a lion, four-armed, elephant-faced, holding a battle-axe, and cruel? He was told by Kapila that he would cut off my head and take the jewel to the great-souled Kapila. Is this dream I saw true, or will it happen? Therefore, I shall go with my army to destroy Kapila.

Then, taking up his weapons and accompanied by many warriors, the fierce Gana went at night to destroy Kapila’s hermitage. At dawn, the demon king reached the hermitage. His disciples saw the wicked demon and went to Kapila. The disciples, filled with fear, bowed and said: Master, the demon Gana has arrived with his army. Then, the sage bowed to Ganapati and prayed for the destruction of the demon with humility.

Then, the angry Ganesha, mounted on a lion, went toward Gana. Gana saw the person he had seen in his dream. Then, Gana ordered his warriors and demon commanders to fight. They came toward the Lord with raised weapons. Seeing them, the angry Goddess Siddhi, who resides on his left side, meditated on Ganesha and created an incomparable son. By the command of Ganesha, that son destroyed the entire army of Gana, and Siddhi caused his army to flee in all ten directions.

The victorious person bowed to Ganesha. The Lord named him "Laksha" and said: O son, dwell everywhere in the world as Brahman. Whatever "Laksha" (mark/goal) a man sets, you shall enjoy the same. You are the one with the power of a hundred thousand in the heart of all, there is no doubt. O son of Siddhi, great one, protect my devotees with effort. Your brother, the son of Buddhi, will be the giver of fruits. According to the "Laksha," your brother will be of great strength. Those who perform the "Laksha" in the heart of Brahman regarding enjoyment, liberation, and sin, shall receive such benefits.

Having said this to his son, the One-Tusked Lord went to fight where the demon king was. Knowing he had arrived, the powerful Ganasura took up an infallible weapon on his bow to kill him. Just then, the Lord of Ganas, with his swift battle-axe, cut off the head of the demon king, and he fell. All the demons cried out and fled to the netherworld. All the gods were filled with joy, and the sages went to the Lord.

Ganesha consoled Kapila and, having retrieved the jewel, gave it back to him. Kapila bowed to him. Kapila, filled with devotion, said to the Lord of Ganas: O Master, this is your ornament, please take it. It is to be worshipped by us and is not fit for us to wear, O Lord. Therefore, O Remover of Obstacles, you alone should wear it around your neck. O Master, you who dwell in the heart, you knew my thoughts and made them all fruitful. Therefore, you shall be known as Chintamani, there is no doubt. Having said this, he himself placed the jewel around Ganesha's neck.

Then, taking the jewel, the One-Tusked Lord disappeared, and the gods were amazed. Kapila, through the power of constant meditation and knowledge of truth, worshipped the Lord Chintamani in his heart with pure devotion. Thus, having killed Ganasura and granted devotion, the One-Tusked Lord protected the saintly Kapila. Whoever hears or reads this story of the One-Tusked Lord, which destroys all sins, shall attain everything.

Thus ends the seventy-second chapter named "The Killing of Ganasura" in the second part of the Mudgala Purana.

Chapter 73 : The Conclusion of the Dialogue between Gritsamada and Prahlada

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Gritsamada said: O wise one, it is impossible for me to describe the vast extent of the characters of the many incarnations of the one-tusked Lord. Where even the Vedas and other scriptures are unable to comprehend Him, who am I, a person of limited intellect, to speak of Him? This one-tusked Ganesha is the Supreme Brahman Himself, the Lord who bestows knowledge upon all embodied beings and governs their respective actions. He is the Lord of all, the holder of perfect Yoga; the wise should understand Him as the Supreme Being beyond all other interpretations. The Great Maya is His expression, and He is the sustainer of that power; the tusk is merely a manifestation of this illusion, and through their union, He is known. O fortunate Prahlada, worship Him according to the prescribed methods, and through the grace of the one-tusked Lord, your curse will be destroyed.

Mudgala said: Prahlada, the crest-jewel of the Vaishnavas, then spoke to Gritsamada again, desiring to understand the knowledge of Ganesha.

Prahlada said: O Sage, I have been a devoted worshipper of Vishnu since my very birth. Therefore, how should I worship the Lord of the Ganas? You are all-knowing, and I ask you, O Lord, to tell me everything regarding the supreme practice of Ganesha-yoga.

Gritsamada said: Four incarnations of Ganesha have been proclaimed. I will tell you about their secondary forms to dispel your doubts. In the city of Svananda, the Lord Ganesha resides. He, the Lord of the gods, created this universe for the sake of His own divine play. Know that the Lord of the Ganas performs various sports within these worlds, and through the practice of Yoga, you shall realize Him as the Brahman. For the sake of play, He manifested in four forms within the Brahman, while the fifth remains in His own abode, acting as the connector of them all. Having deluded the four with His Maya, the elephant-faced Lord resides within their hearts in His true form. Know that the Brahman which resides in names and forms is described in the Vedas as the power that manifests as the unreal. Within them, the Atman is the nectar-like Sun, the symbol of Truth. When there is no distinction between these two, that bliss is called Vishnu. That which is the unmanifest form in the three, which is beyond the "not this, not this" negation, is the fourth, the Shiva, as described in the Vedas. The union of these four is called Svananda, and that Brahman became the Lord of the Ganas, who performs various plays. Thus, His four incarnations are described, and from them, the secondary forms arose to establish the four states of being. Know, O best of the demons, that Vishnu, who is of the form of the Ganas, is not different from Ganesha. By worshipping these four, one attains purity of mind, and then one becomes qualified for Ganesha-yoga. Just as a man follows the order of life, leaving one stage to enter the next with devotion, so too does the Yogi eventually reach a state of peace, transcending all stages. Similarly, one should progress from the Vaishnava path and beyond.

Now, listen to the supreme practice of the Lord that leads to Yoga. By practicing this, you will undoubtedly become a Yogi. Svananda is especially associated with divine play, and because He is the cause of all unions, Ganesha is associated with Maya. Without this play, He is considered devoid of Yoga and free from Maya. Because He is beyond both union and separation, the Lord of the Ganas is the destroyer of worldly existence. The union of union and separation is called the perfect Yoga. O Prahlada, the Lord of the Ganas is the perfect, supreme Brahman. Yogis attain the Lord of the Ganas through this Yoga; know Him as the one who is perfect, devoid of both union and separation. His Maya is said to be twofold: Siddhi (perfection) and Buddhi (intellect). Buddhi is fivefold, being of the nature of the mind: scattered, dull, distracted, focused, and restrained. These five states of the mind are the Maya of Ganesha. The scattered and dull mind dwells in karma and forbidden acts, and through it, the world moves according to its nature. Know, O giver of respect, that the distracted mind dwells in inaction; through it, one attains liberation via the bright path, without doubt. The focused mind is eightfold and dwells in the one Self; know this as the state of Samprajnata Samadhi. The restrained mind is the form of withdrawal; know this as the state of Asamprajnata Yoga through the practice of Yoga. This fivefold state of the mind is called the form of Buddhi, which is the Maya of the Lord of the Ganas, to be abandoned through the practice of Yoga. Siddhi is described as manifold and is considered a source of delusion; because of it, people in this world become confused. The Siddhis related to Dharma, Artha, Kama, and Moksha are described as distinct; the Siddhi that leads to the realization of Brahman must always be abandoned in its fivefold form. Siddhi is highly delusive, and the Lord of obstacles plays with both Buddhi and Siddhi everywhere. Whatever is understood by the intellect is followed by delusion; therefore, through devotion to Ganesha, one becomes free from both these Mayas. By abandoning the fivefold states of the mind and the fivefold Siddhis, worship Ganesha with devotion through Ganesha-yoga.

Then, the sage himself gave him the mantra of the Lord of the Ganas, the Vedic mantra beginning with "Ganam Tva," along with the proper procedure. Having been initiated and worshipped by the great-souled Prahlada, the sage Gritsamada departed for the heavenly realm through the sky. Prahlada, the virtuous one, practiced this diligently, becoming a Yogi revered by the masters of Yoga, and he became a source of peace. He gave his kingdom to his son Virochana and remained constantly absorbed in the worship of Ganesha. Know that everything, whether the Saguna form of Vishnu or the Nirguna Brahman, is held by Ganesha through a portion of His power. Having realized this, the great Yogi Prahlada attained non-duality. Knowing the Chintamani within his heart, he worshipped Him with undivided devotion. In a short time, the king of the demons, devoted to the Yoga of peace, attained tranquility and became one with Ganesha. The curse was also removed by Ganesha, and Prahlada was no longer deluded by the company of the wicked. Thus, by abandoning pride and taking refuge in the one-tusked Lord, even the demon Prahlada became a great Yogi. The noble person who hears or reads this greatness of Prahlada will have all their desires fulfilled.

Chapter 74 : The Glory of the Narrative

Salutations to Shri Ganesha.

Daksha said: O Great Sage, I have heard in brief about the sacred territory of Ekadanta. Please describe to me in detail the pilgrimage and other related matters.

Mudgala said: O bestower of honor, know that the territory of Ekadanta is situated on the shore of the ocean, in the corner of the directions, within its own designated region. It extends for ten yojanas in all directions, and in its center, the Lord of Ganas, Ekadanta, is worshipped by those who seek spiritual accomplishments. On His left side resides Siddhi herself, and on His right is Prajapati. His intellect (Buddhi) and His mount, the mouse, are stationed near the Lord. The eight Siddhis, bestowing devotion, stand before Him, and on His left, all the gods and others remain with devotion. On His right side, the Vedas and scriptures praise Him, and behind Him, sacred places like Kashi and other holy sites worship Him. All the noble entities existing in the three worlds, having become residents of this territory, worship Ekadanta.

Those who bathe in the ocean at the holy sites marked by their own names, while assuming subtle forms, always remain there. The Ekadanta Tirtha is renowned in all worlds; its mere sight destroys all sins and grants both worldly enjoyment and liberation. A great festival takes place there on the fourth day of the bright fortnight of Bhadrapada, and people from all three worlds travel there for pilgrimage. This territory is known to grant the state of Brahman, as confirmed by the scriptures; those who die there attain residence in Svananda.

I have described the pure glory of this territory in brief; who would be capable of detailing it even in ten thousand years? O wise one, I have narrated this primary account and the glory of Ekadanta to you briefly. Even Brahma, Vishnu, Maheshwara, or Shuka would never be capable of describing it in full. Whoever listens to, causes others to hear, or reads this narrative of Ekadanta, which grants all accomplishments, attains everything. There is no doubt that a person attains sons, grandsons, wealth, grains, and a happy spouse.

One who praises Ekadanta daily with such devotion becomes like Ekadanta himself on this earth. By his mere sight, even the gods become fulfilled, for he is of the form of Brahman. By listening to this chapter, a man, a woman, or even an animal becomes fulfilled, as determined by the sages of old. The merit gained by listening to this is a hundred times greater than that gained by bathing in all holy rivers, performing all charities, practicing daily penance, completing all pilgrimages, and performing all rituals of sacrifice and public welfare with devotion. By listening to this chapter, a person attains Dharma, Artha, Kama, and Moksha, and there is no doubt that he becomes one with Brahman. Whatever a great man desires becomes fruitful by listening to the narrative of Ekadanta. O Daksha, the complete glory cannot be expressed by me, nor by Brahma and others; therefore, I have told you the essence clearly.

Suta said: Having spoken thus, the great yogi Mudgala became silent. Daksha, extremely delighted, bowed to him with folded hands. Being eager to hear the glory of Mahodara, Daksha spoke to him again, which I shall describe before you. O Shaunaka and others, I have told you this narrative just as Daksha heard it all from Mudgala. I have told you the Ekadanta narrative, which grants all accomplishments. O Brahmana, having heard this with devotion, what more do you wish to hear?

Thus ends the seventy-fourth chapter named The Glory of the Narrative in the Ekadanta section of the second part of the glorious Mudgala Purana. The second part is thus concluded.
